0. Core Java Foundations & Language Evolution — Java 8 → Java 22

Start here if you want the "explain it to a 10-year-old" version before the Principal-level material in the sections that follow. Every concept below is introduced with a plain-English analogy, then a real diagram, then real Java code, then the interview-ready technical definition.

How this module is organized: 0.1–0.5 cover core Java itself (JVM/JDK/JRE, OOP basics, data types). 0.6–0.14 walk the entire language evolution from Java 8 to Java 22, feature by feature. 0.15–0.26 are a complete Threads, Concurrency & Synchronization primer — the exact foundation the Principal-level sections 6–14 (Java Memory Model → Virtual Threads) build on. Jump to any card below.
0.1 JVM/JDK/JRE 0.2 OOP Basics 0.3 Types & Variables 0.4 Control Flow 0.5 Collections Basics 0.6 Release Timeline 0.7 Java 8 0.8 Java 9–10 0.9 Java 11 LTS 0.10 Java 12–16 0.11 Java 17 LTS 0.12 Java 18–20 0.13 Java 21 LTS 0.14 Java 22 0.15 Version Cheat Sheet 0.16 What Is a Thread? 0.17 Creating Threads 0.18 Thread Lifecycle 0.19 Synchronization Basics 0.20 wait / notify 0.21 ThreadLocal 0.22 Atomic & CAS 0.23 Concurrent Collections 0.24 Fork/Join 0.25 Structured Concurrency 0.26 Platform vs Virtual Threads

0.1 What Are JVM, JDK and JRE?

🧒 Kid analogy: Think of Java code as a recipe written in English. The JVM is the kitchen that actually cooks the food. The JRE is the kitchen plus the fridge full of ready-made ingredients (the standard library) so cooking can happen. The JDK is the JRE plus the chef's knives and tools (compiler, debugger) so you can also create new recipes, not just cook existing ones.
JDK (Java Development Kit) — for developers └── JRE (Java Runtime Environment) — for running apps └── JVM (Java Virtual Machine) — executes bytecode └── Class Loader → Bytecode Verifier → Interpreter/JIT → Native machine code
PieceContainsWho needs it
JVMBytecode execution engine, JIT compiler, garbage collectorEveryone (even end users, invisibly)
JREJVM + core class libraries (java.lang, java.util…)Anyone running a Java app
JDKJRE + javac (compiler), jshell, jar, debugger, javadocDevelopers writing/compiling Java

Java code compiles to bytecode (.class files) — a platform-neutral instruction set — which the JVM then interprets or JIT-compiles to native machine code. This is the literal meaning of "write once, run anywhere": the same bytecode runs on any OS that has a JVM.

0.2 OOP in One Page (see §2 for the Principal-level treatment)

🧒 Kid analogy: A class is a cookie cutter. An object is an actual cookie made from it. Encapsulation is putting the cookie in a wrapper so nobody pokes at the ingredients directly. Inheritance is a "chocolate chip cookie" recipe that reuses the base "cookie" recipe and adds chips. Polymorphism is that you can ask any cookie to "be eaten" and each kind does it a little differently.
Encapsulation
Hide internal state behind methods (private fields + public getters/behavior).
Abstraction
Expose what something does, hide how, via interfaces/abstract classes.
Inheritance
A class reuses and extends another class's fields/behavior.
Polymorphism
The same method call behaves differently depending on the actual object type.
class Account {
    private double balance; // encapsulated

    public void deposit(double amt) { balance += amt; }
    public double getBalance() { return balance; }
}

class SavingsAccount extends Account { // inheritance
    private double interestRate;
}

// Polymorphism
Account acc = new SavingsAccount();
acc.deposit(100); // resolved at runtime based on actual object

For SOLID principles, composition-vs-inheritance trade-offs and a real banking-domain OOP walkthrough, continue to Section 2 — OOP at Principal Level.

0.3 Data Types & Variables

🧒 Kid analogy: A variable is a labeled box. Primitive types (int, boolean, double…) are small boxes that hold the value itself. Reference types (String, objects, arrays) are boxes that hold a note with directions to where the real thing lives.
CategoryTypesDefaultSize
Integerbyte, short, int, long08 / 16 / 32 / 64 bits
Floatingfloat, double0.032 / 64 bits
Otherchar, boolean'\u0000', false16 / 1 bit (JVM-dependent)
ReferenceString, arrays, objectsnull4/8 bytes (pointer)
int age = 30;                 // primitive, stack-held value
String name = "Asha";         // reference — variable holds an address
int[] scores = {90, 85, 77};  // array — reference type
var rate = 4.5;               // Java 10+: compiler infers 'double'

0.4 Control Flow

🧒 Kid analogy: if/else is a fork in a hiking trail. A for loop is doing the same chore a fixed number of times ("water each of these 10 plants"). A while loop is "keep doing this until told to stop." A traditional switch is a vending machine: press a button, get one specific slot.
if (balance < 0) {
    flagOverdraft();
} else if (balance == 0) {
    // zero balance
} else {
    process();
}

for (int i = 0; i < accounts.size(); i++) { ... }

for (Account a : accounts) { ... } // enhanced for-each

// Java 14+ switch expression (see 0.10)
String tier = switch (customerType) {
    case "GOLD"   -> "Priority";
    case "SILVER" -> "Standard";
    default       -> "Basic";
};

0.5 Collections in 60 Seconds

🧒 Kid analogy: List is a numbered row of lockers (order matters, duplicates allowed). Set is a guest list where nobody's name appears twice. Map is a phonebook: look someone up by name (key), get their number (value).
InterfaceCommon implOrdered?Duplicates?
ListArrayList, LinkedListYes (insertion)Yes
SetHashSet, TreeSet, LinkedHashSetNo / sorted / insertionNo
MapHashMap, TreeMap, LinkedHashMapNo / sorted / insertionKeys: no, Values: yes
Queue/DequeArrayDeque, LinkedList, PriorityQueueFIFO/LIFO/priorityYes

The deep internals of HashMap (buckets, treeification, resizing) are covered in Section 19 — HashMap Interview Drill.

0.6 The Java Release Train (Java 8 → 22)

🧒 Kid analogy: Since Java 9, a new Java version leaves the station every 6 months, like a train on a fixed schedule — whether a feature is fully baked or not, it either boards this train or waits for the next one. Every few years, one of those trains is marked LTS (Long-Term Support) — the "express train" companies actually plan their journey around.

LTS (Long-Term Support) releases — 8, 11, 17, 21 — get years of vendor patches and are what most banks/enterprises run in production. Non-LTS releases (9, 10, 12–16, 18–20, 22…) are stepping stones that preview features before they graduate into the next LTS.

0.7 Java 8 (2014) — The Big Bang

🧒 Kid analogy: Before Java 8, if you wanted someone to "do a small task," you had to write them a whole formal job contract (an anonymous class). Java 8's lambda lets you just say the task out loud: "add these two numbers." Streams turn a collection into a factory conveyor belt: items go in one end, get filtered/transformed/counted, and results come out the other end.
Lambda expressions
Anonymous functions: (a, b) -> a + b
Functional interfaces
Runnable, Comparator, Function, Predicate, Supplier, Consumer
Stream API
Declarative, pipelined collection processing
Optional<T>
Explicit "may be absent" container instead of null
Default & static interface methods
Interfaces can now ship behavior, not just signatures
java.time
LocalDate/LocalDateTime/Instant replace the broken old Date/Calendar
Stream pipeline (conveyor belt): customers.stream() | ├── .filter(c -> c.isActive()) ⇢ quality-check station | ├── .map(Customer::getBalance) ⇢ repackaging station | ├── .sorted() ⇢ sorting station | └── .collect(toList()) ⇢ boxing station → output
// Before Java 8
Collections.sort(customers, new Comparator<Customer>() {
    public int compare(Customer a, Customer b) {
        return a.getName().compareTo(b.getName());
    }
});

// Java 8 lambda
customers.sort((a, b) -> a.getName().compareTo(b.getName()));

// Stream pipeline
List<String> goldCustomerNames = customers.stream()
    .filter(c -> c.getTier() == Tier.GOLD)
    .map(Customer::getName)
    .sorted()
    .collect(Collectors.toList());

// Optional instead of null-checks
Optional<Customer> found = repository.findById(id);
found.ifPresentOrElse(
    c -> notify(c),
    () -> log.warn("not found")
);

// java.time
LocalDate today = LocalDate.now();
LocalDateTime settleAt = LocalDateTime.of(2026, 8, 25, 17, 0);
Interview trap: Streams are lazy — nothing runs until a terminal operation (collect, forEach, count, reduce) is called. Also: a stream can only be consumed once; reusing it throws IllegalStateException.

0.8 Java 9 & 10 (2017–2018)

FeatureWhat it means
JPMS (Project Jigsaw)The JDK itself and your app can be split into modules (module-info.java) with explicit exports — like sealed shipping containers instead of one giant warehouse.
JShellAn interactive REPL — try Java snippets instantly, like a calculator, no compile step.
Private interface methodsInterfaces can now have private helper methods for their own default methods to share code.
Immutable collection factoriesList.of(1,2,3), Map.of("a",1) — instantly unmodifiable.
var (Java 10)Local-variable type inference: var list = new ArrayList<String>(); — compiler figures out the type, still statically typed.
module com.bank.payments {
    requires com.bank.core;
    exports com.bank.payments.api;
}

var accounts = new ArrayList<Account>(); // Java 10
List<Integer> primes = List.of(2, 3, 5, 7); // Java 9, immutable

0.9 Java 11 (2018) — LTS

🧒 Kid analogy: Java 11 is like tidying your room and getting a new phone: old clunky tools (CORBA, Applets) get thrown out, and a shiny new HttpClient arrives so you can talk to web APIs without dragging in extra libraries.
FeatureExample
New String methodsisBlank(), strip(), lines(), repeat(n)
Standard HttpClientBuilt-in HTTP/2 client — no more manual HttpURLConnection
Single-file source launchjava HelloWorld.java — no separate compile step for quick scripts
var in lambda params(var a, var b) -> a + b (lets you add annotations)
RemovedJava EE modules, CORBA, Applet API
HttpClient client = HttpClient.newHttpClient();
HttpRequest request = HttpRequest.newBuilder()
    .uri(URI.create("https://api.bank.com/rates"))
    .GET().build();

HttpResponse<String> response =
    client.send(request, HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString());

0.10 Java 12–16 (2019–2021)

🧒 Kid analogy: A record is a pre-printed form: name it once ("Point has an x and a y") and Java writes the constructor, getters, equals, hashCode and toString for you — no more boilerplate homework. A text block is finally being allowed to paste multi-line text without escaping every quote.
VersionFeatureWhy it matters
12Switch expressions (preview)switch can now return a value, not just branch
13Text blocks (preview)Multi-line strings with """, no escaping
14Switch expressions (final), instanceof pattern matching (preview), Records (preview), Helpful NullPointerExceptionsBig quality-of-life jump
15Text blocks (final), Sealed classes (preview)
16Records (final), Pattern matching for instanceof (final)Data carriers become first-class
// Switch expression (Java 14)
int riskScore = switch (customerType) {
    case "GOLD" -> 10;
    case "SILVER" -> 25;
    default -> 50;
};

// Text block (Java 15)
String json = """
    {
      "account": "%s",
      "balance": %.2f
    }
    """.formatted(id, balance);

// Records (Java 16)
record Money(String currency, BigDecimal amount) {}
Money price = new Money("INR", new BigDecimal("499.00"));
price.amount();   // auto-generated accessor
price.equals(other); // auto-generated equals/hashCode/toString

// Pattern matching for instanceof (Java 16)
if (obj instanceof Customer c && c.isActive()) {
    process(c); // 'c' is already cast, no manual (Customer) obj
}
Helpful NPEs (Java 14+): A NullPointerException message now names the exact null reference, e.g. "Cannot invoke `Account.getBalance()` because `acc` is null" — a real production-debugging upgrade, enabled by default since Java 15.

0.11 Java 17 (2021) — LTS

🧒 Kid analogy: A sealed class is a guest list you write yourself: "only these named classes are allowed to extend me" — like a family tree that can't have surprise strangers added to it.
// Sealed classes — the closed set of subtypes is explicit
sealed interface PaymentEvent
    permits PaymentInitiated, PaymentCompleted, PaymentFailed {}

record PaymentInitiated(String id) implements PaymentEvent {}
record PaymentCompleted(String id) implements PaymentEvent {}
record PaymentFailed(String id, String reason) implements PaymentEvent {}

// The compiler KNOWS these are the only 3 subtypes —
// a switch over them can be exhaustive with no 'default' needed
// once pattern matching for switch becomes final (Java 21).

Java 17 is the current enterprise-standard LTS in most 2024–2026 job descriptions, alongside Java 21. It finalized sealed classes and shipped pattern matching for switch as a preview.

0.12 Java 18–20 (2022–2023)

VersionFeature
18UTF-8 as the default charset everywhere (no more platform-dependent surprises); simple built-in web server (jwebserver) for quick local testing
19Virtual Threads (incubator/preview); Structured Concurrency (incubator); Record patterns (preview)
20Refinements to virtual threads, structured concurrency, record patterns and pattern matching for switch (still previews)

18–20 are the "test flight" versions where Project Loom's virtual threads were being road-tested before locking in for Java 21.

0.13 Java 21 (2023) — LTS, the Modern Baseline

🧒 Kid analogy: Imagine a school with only 200 chairs (heavy platform threads) trying to serve 50,000 pen-pal letters (requests) that each involve waiting for a reply. Most chairs sit empty while waiting for the mail. Virtual threads are like giving every letter its own tiny sticky note instead of a chair — millions of sticky notes cost nothing, and only the notes actively being read need a real desk.
Virtual Threads (final)
Lightweight JVM-managed threads — millions possible, huge throughput win for blocking I/O code. See 0.26.
Sequenced Collections
New SequencedCollection/Map/Set interfaces add getFirst(), getLast(), reversed() uniformly.
Record Patterns (final)
Destructure records directly in instanceof/switch.
Pattern Matching for switch (final)
Type-safe, exhaustive switches over sealed hierarchies.
Structured Concurrency (preview)
Group related child threads into one unit with one lifetime.
String Templates (preview)
STR."Hello \{name}" — safer interpolation than concatenation.
// Record patterns + pattern matching switch (Java 21)
sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rectangle {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
record Rectangle(double w, double h) implements Shape {}

double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {
        case Circle(double r) -> Math.PI * r * r;
        case Rectangle(double w, double h) -> w * h;
        // no 'default' needed — sealed + exhaustive
    };
}

// Sequenced collections
List<String> names = new ArrayList<>(List.of("Ann","Bo","Cy"));
names.getFirst();       // "Ann"
names.reversed();       // ["Cy","Bo","Ann"] view

// Virtual threads (final)
try (var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) {
        executor.submit(() -> callSlowBankingApi());
    }
}
Why Java 21 matters most: it's the LTS most companies are migrating to right now (2025–2026), and it's the version where concurrency in Java fundamentally changed. Expect Principal-level interviews to probe virtual threads deeply — see 0.26 and Section 14.

0.14 Java 22 (2024)

FeatureWhat it means
Statements before super()You can now validate/prepare arguments before calling the superclass constructor, instead of being forced to call super() as the literal first line.
Unnamed variables & patterns (final)Use _ for a value you must declare but never use: catch (Exception _) { } or case Point(int x, int _).
Foreign Function & Memory API (final)Call native (C) code and manage off-heap memory safely without JNI.
Stream Gatherers (preview)Custom intermediate stream operations beyond filter/map/reduce.
Unnamed classes & instance main methods (preview)void main() { System.out.println("hi"); } — no class/public/static ceremony needed for a simple script, great for teaching beginners.
// Java 22 preview: no class wrapper needed for a simple program
void main() {
    System.out.println("Hello, Java 22");
}

// Unnamed variable
try {
    risky();
} catch (IOException _) {
    log.warn("ignored, reason not needed");
}

// Statements before super()
class SavingsAccount extends Account {
    SavingsAccount(double rate) {
        if (rate < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("rate");
        super(rate); // now legal to validate first
    }
}

Continue the concurrency story → Sections 0.16–0.26 below build the foundation for the Principal-level treatment in Section 6 (Java Memory Model) through Section 14 (Virtual Threads). Read these first if any of those sections feel like they're skipping steps.

0.16 What Is a Thread?

🧒 Kid analogy: A program is a kitchen. A process is the whole kitchen (its own walls, its own fridge — isolated from other kitchens). A thread is one chef working inside that kitchen. One kitchen can have several chefs (threads) all sharing the same fridge, counters and ingredients (memory) at the same time — which is powerful, but also means two chefs can bump into each other reaching for the same bowl.
Process (JVM instance) ├── Heap (shared by ALL threads — the shared fridge) ├── Thread 1 → own Stack, own Program Counter (chef #1) ├── Thread 2 → own Stack, own Program Counter (chef #2) └── Thread 3 → own Stack, own Program Counter (chef #3)

Threads inside one process share the heap (objects, static fields) but each has its own private stack (local variables, method call frames). That single fact — shared heap, private stacks — is the root cause of almost every concurrency bug covered in Sections 6–10.

0.17 Creating Threads

// 1) Extend Thread
class ReportThread extends Thread {
    public void run() { generateReport(); }
}
new ReportThread().start();

// 2) Implement Runnable (preferred — no wasted inheritance slot)
Runnable task = () -> generateReport();
new Thread(task).start();

// 3) Callable — a Runnable that can return a value and throw checked exceptions
Callable<Report> callable = () -> buildReport();
Future<Report> future = executorService.submit(callable);

// 4) Virtual thread (Java 21+) — cheapest of all
Thread.startVirtualThread(() -> generateReport());
Interview trap: calling run() directly just runs the method on the current thread — no new thread is created. You must call start() to actually hand work to the JVM's thread scheduler.

0.18 Thread Lifecycle

A thread moves through NEW (created, not started) → RUNNABLE (eligible to run, may or may not currently have CPU) → possibly BLOCKED/WAITING/TIMED_WAITING (paused for a lock, a signal, or a timer) → back to RUNNABLE → finally TERMINATED. Thread.getState() returns exactly these six values.

0.19 Synchronization Basics

🧒 Kid analogy: A shared bathroom has one key hanging outside. synchronized is that key: whoever grabs the key can go in and use the bathroom (the critical section); everyone else must wait in line outside. The "lock" in Java is literally called a monitor — every object has one built in.
// Method-level lock — locks on 'this'
public synchronized void withdraw(double amt) {
    if (balance >= amt) balance -= amt;
}

// Block-level lock — lock on a specific object, smaller critical section
private final Object lock = new Object();
public void withdraw(double amt) {
    synchronized (lock) {
        if (balance >= amt) balance -= amt;
    }
}

// Class-level lock — locks on Account.class, shared by ALL instances
public static synchronized void resetGlobalCounter() { counter = 0; }

For the full synchronized vs ReentrantLock trade-off table and fairness/tryLock/interruptibility details, see Section 9.

0.20 wait() / notify() / notifyAll()

🧒 Kid analogy: A restaurant waiting room with a buzzer. If there's no table (condition not met), you wait() — you literally hand back the room and sit down, buzzer in hand, doing nothing until someone notify()s you. The host can wake one waiting party (notify()) or everyone at once (notifyAll()) when a table frees up.
class BoundedBuffer {
    private final Queue<Order> queue = new LinkedList<>();
    private final int capacity = 100;

    public synchronized void put(Order o) throws InterruptedException {
        while (queue.size() == capacity) {
            wait(); // release lock, sleep until notified
        }
        queue.add(o);
        notifyAll(); // wake any waiting consumers
    }

    public synchronized Order take() throws InterruptedException {
        while (queue.isEmpty()) {
            wait();
        }
        Order o = queue.remove();
        notifyAll(); // wake any waiting producers
        return o;
    }
}
Always use a while loop, never if, around wait(). A thread can wake up spuriously, or wake up to find another thread already consumed the resource — the condition must be re-checked. This exact bug is a classic Principal-round whiteboard trap.

In modern code, prefer java.util.concurrent primitives (BlockingQueue, Condition) over raw wait/notify — see Section 11 — Producer-Consumer.

0.21 ThreadLocal

🧒 Kid analogy: An open-plan office with one shared filing cabinet (the heap) — but every employee also gets their own personal desk drawer that nobody else can open. ThreadLocal is that personal drawer: each thread gets its own private copy of the variable, with zero synchronization needed because nothing is shared.
public class RequestContext {
    private static final ThreadLocal<String> CORRELATION_ID =
        ThreadLocal.withInitial(() -> UUID.randomUUID().toString());

    public static String getCorrelationId() { return CORRELATION_ID.get(); }
    public static void clear() { CORRELATION_ID.remove(); } // avoid leaks in pooled threads!
}
Production gotcha: in a thread-pooled server, threads are reused across requests. If you forget to call remove(), stale data leaks into the next request handled by that same thread — a real memory-leak and data-bleed source. Always clear ThreadLocals in a finally block or filter.

0.22 Atomic Variables & Compare-And-Swap (CAS)

🧒 Kid analogy: Instead of a bathroom key (a lock everyone must wait for), imagine a whiteboard eraser rule: "Before you write a new number, check the number currently on the board still matches what you last saw — if it does, swap it for your new number in one instant motion; if someone changed it first, try again." No waiting in line, no key — just a fast optimistic check-and-swap.
AtomicInteger counter = new AtomicInteger(0);
counter.incrementAndGet();      // atomic, lock-free

AtomicLong idGenerator = new AtomicLong();
long nextId = idGenerator.getAndIncrement();

AtomicReference<Account> current = new AtomicReference<>(account);
current.compareAndSet(oldAcc, newAcc); // CAS — the real mechanism underneath

CAS is a single CPU instruction: "if memory location still equals expected, set it to new value; otherwise fail and let the caller retry." It underpins every class in java.util.concurrent.atomic and is generally far cheaper than acquiring a lock under low-to-moderate contention.

0.23 Concurrent Collections

ClassKid analogyUse when
ConcurrentHashMapA phonebook split into many small sections, each with its own tiny lock, so two people can edit different sections at onceHigh-read, high-write shared map
CopyOnWriteArrayListEvery edit photocopies the whole list first, edits the copy, then swaps it in — readers never see a half-changed listRare writes, frequent iteration/reads
BlockingQueueA pass-through kitchen window: producers place orders, consumers take them, and everyone waits automatically when it's empty/fullProducer-consumer pipelines
ConcurrentLinkedQueueA lock-free line — join or leave without ever blockingHigh-throughput non-blocking queueing
Map<String, Account> cache = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
cache.computeIfAbsent(id, k -> loadFromDb(k)); // atomic check-then-act

0.24 Fork/Join Framework

🧒 Kid analogy: Cleaning a huge house: instead of one kid cleaning every room, you split the house in half, give each half to a sibling, and each sibling keeps splitting their half until the chunks are tiny (one room). Everyone cleans their tiny piece, then the results get combined back up the chain — that's divide-and-conquer with automatic work-stealing between siblings who finish early.
class SumTask extends RecursiveTask<Long> {
    private final long[] arr; private final int lo, hi;
    // ... constructor ...
    protected Long compute() {
        if (hi - lo <= 1000) {
            long sum = 0;
            for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++) sum += arr[i];
            return sum;
        }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        SumTask left  = new SumTask(arr, lo, mid);
        SumTask right = new SumTask(arr, mid, hi);
        left.fork();                       // hand off to another worker
        long rightResult = right.compute(); // do the right half ourselves
        return left.join() + rightResult;   // combine
    }
}
new ForkJoinPool().invoke(new SumTask(data, 0, data.length));

Parallel streams (list.parallelStream()) are built on the common ForkJoinPool under the hood.

0.25 Structured Concurrency (Java 21/22, preview)

🧒 Kid analogy: A school field trip rule: the bus cannot leave until every child who got off to explore has checked back in — and if one child gets hurt, the teacher immediately calls back all the others instead of leaving them wandering. Structured concurrency gives child tasks that exact "same trip, all-or-nothing" lifetime and error propagation.
try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
    Supplier<Account> account = scope.fork(() -> accountService.get(id));
    Supplier<Credit>  credit  = scope.fork(() -> creditService.get(id));

    scope.join();           // wait for both — or fail fast together
    scope.throwIfFailed();  // propagate the first failure

    return combine(account.get(), credit.get());
}

Unlike a bare CompletableFuture.allOf(...), structured concurrency guarantees no child task outlives its parent scope — eliminating a whole class of thread leaks. See Section 13 — CompletableFuture for the pre-structured-concurrency approach still dominant in most codebases today.

0.26 Platform Threads vs Virtual Threads

🧒 Kid analogy: A platform thread is a full-size delivery truck — expensive, and your city only has room to park a couple thousand of them (OS thread limits, ~1MB stack each). A virtual thread is a bicycle courier — cheap, tiny, and a single dispatcher (the JVM scheduler) can manage millions of them, automatically "parking the bike" (unmounting from the real OS thread) the instant a courier is stuck waiting, and handing that real truck to another courier who's ready to move.
Platform ThreadVirtual Thread (Java 21+)
Backed by1 OS thread, alwaysA small "carrier" OS thread pool; unmounts on blocking calls
Stack size~1 MB fixedStarts tiny (~KBs), grows on the heap
Creation costExpensiveExtremely cheap — create millions
Best forCPU-bound workI/O-bound, blocking-style code at massive concurrency
PoolingYes, essential (thread pools)No pooling — create a new one per task, never reuse
ThreadLocalCheapWorks but discouraged at huge scale — memory adds up per-thread

See Section 14 for the Principal-level production guidance — pinning, when NOT to use virtual threads (tight CPU loops, synchronized blocks around blocking calls), and migration strategy.

1. Java Architecture You Must Be Able to Explain

At Principal level, you should be comfortable moving across abstraction levels: application, framework, JVM, operating system, container, Kubernetes and cloud infrastructure.

Java Application | ↓ Java Bytecode | ↓ JVM ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Class Runtime JIT Loader Memory Compiler | | | | ┌────┴─────┐ | | ↓ ↓ | | Heap Stack | | | | | Objects Native | | Memory | ↓ | GC | └────────→ Java Libraries

2. OOP — But at Principal Level

The four classical concepts are:

EncapsulationProtect object invariants and implementation details.
AbstractionExpose what callers need without exposing unnecessary implementation.
InheritanceReuse/extend behavior where the relationship is stable and genuinely “is-a”.
PolymorphismProgram against abstractions and allow implementations to vary.

At your level, pay particular attention to composition, immutability, dependency inversion, separation of concerns, domain modeling, loose coupling and high cohesion.

3. Composition vs Inheritance

Consider a banking system. Deep inheritance can become brittle as business rules evolve.

class BankAccount {}

class SavingsAccount extends BankAccount {}

class PremiumSavingsAccount extends SavingsAccount {}

class InternationalPremiumSavingsAccount
        extends PremiumSavingsAccount {}

Prefer composition when behavior changes independently:

class Account {

    private AccountType type;
    private InterestPolicy interestPolicy;
    private WithdrawalPolicy withdrawalPolicy;
}
Account | ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ InterestPolicy WithdrawalPolicy FeePolicy ↓ ↓ ↓ SavingsPolicy PremiumPolicy InternationalPolicy
Interview answer: Inheritance creates strong coupling between a parent/child hierarchy. In enterprise systems where business rules evolve frequently, composition provides more flexibility because behavior can change independently. Use inheritance where there is a genuine and stable “is-a” relationship.

4. SOLID in a Real Banking System

A single service that validates payments, performs fraud checks, persists data, sends email, publishes events and generates reports has too many responsibilities.

PaymentService | ├── PaymentValidator ├── FraudService ├── PaymentRepository ├── EventPublisher └── NotificationService
class PaymentService {

    private final PaymentValidator validator;
    private final FraudService fraudService;
    private final PaymentRepository repository;
    private final EventPublisher eventPublisher;

    public PaymentResponse process(PaymentRequest request) {

        validator.validate(request);

        fraudService.check(request);

        Payment payment = repository.save(
            createPayment(request)
        );

        eventPublisher.publish(
            new PaymentCreatedEvent(payment.getId())
        );

        return PaymentResponse.from(payment);
    }
}

5. Immutability

Immutable objects are easier to reason about and safely share between threads.

public final class Customer {

    private final String id;
    private final String name;

    public Customer(String id, String name) {
        this.id = id;
        this.name = name;
    }

    public String getId() { return id; }
    public String getName() { return name; }
}

For simple immutable data carriers, Java records are concise:

public record Customer(
    String id,
    String name
) {}
Thread safetyLess shared mutable state.
PredictabilityState cannot change unexpectedly.
CachingStable values are easier to cache.
Safe sharingMultiple threads can safely reuse immutable values.

6. Java Memory Model

🧒 New to threads? Read 0.16 What Is a Thread? first — one shared fridge (heap), many private chefs (thread stacks).
Thread | ├── Program Counter ├── Stack └── Local Variables Shared Memory | └── Heap

Multiple threads accessing shared state create the possibility of race conditions, visibility problems, atomicity problems and instruction reordering concerns.

7. Race Condition

🧒 Two kids both look at a cookie jar showing "3 cookies left," each takes one thinking there'll be 2 left — but the jar ends up at 1, not 2, because neither saw the other's update. That's a race condition.
class Counter {

    private int count = 0;

    public void increment() {
        count++;
    }
}

count++ is effectively a read → add → write sequence. Two threads can read the same old value and overwrite each other's update.

Thread A Thread B READ 10 READ 10 ADD 1 ADD 1 WRITE 11 WRITE 11 Expected: 12 Actual: 11

Possible fixes include synchronization or atomic classes:

public synchronized void increment() {
    count++;
}
private final AtomicInteger count =
    new AtomicInteger();

public void increment() {
    count.incrementAndGet();
}
Principal discussion: Choose based on contention, operation complexity, scalability, lock granularity and whether multiple variables must change atomically.

8. volatile

🧒 volatile is a sticky note on the shared fridge door, written in permanent marker everyone can see instantly — but it doesn't stop two people from grabbing the last snack at the same time. It fixes visibility, not atomicity.

volatile provides visibility guarantees for reads/writes between threads, but it does not make compound operations such as count++ atomic.

private volatile boolean running = true;

while (running) {
    process();
}

A second thread can set running = false, and the volatile declaration helps ensure the worker observes the update.

9. synchronized vs Lock

🧒 See 0.19 Synchronization Basics for the bathroom-key analogy before diving into the trade-offs below. ReentrantLock is that same key, but with extra features: a timer (tryLock), a way to give up waiting (interruptibly), and a fairness queue.
public synchronized void process() {
    // critical section
}
private final Lock lock =
    new ReentrantLock();

public void process() {
    lock.lock();
    try {
        // critical section
    } finally {
        lock.unlock();
    }
}

ReentrantLock offers features such as tryLock(), timed acquisition and interruptible locking. Use the simplest synchronization primitive that satisfies correctness and performance requirements.

10. Deadlock

🧒 Two kids doing a jigsaw puzzle: Kid A is holding the corner piece Kid B needs, and Kid B is holding the edge piece Kid A needs. Neither will let go of their own piece until they get the other one — so nobody ever finishes. Forever stuck. That's deadlock.
Thread A: Lock A ↓ wait for Lock B Thread B: Lock B ↓ wait for Lock A

Typical mitigations:

  • Consistent lock ordering
  • Timeouts and tryLock()
  • Reducing lock scope
  • Avoiding unnecessary nested locks

11. Producer-Consumer

🧒 A chef (producer) cooks dishes and slides them onto a pass-through window (the queue). A waiter (consumer) picks dishes up from that window. If the window is full, the chef pauses; if it's empty, the waiter waits. Neither needs to know what the other is doing directly — the window (queue) handles the handoff.
Producer | ↓ BlockingQueue | ↓ Consumer
BlockingQueue<Order> queue =
    new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(1000);

queue.put(order);

Order order = queue.take();

The queue provides a form of backpressure. If consumers cannot keep up, the bounded queue prevents unlimited memory growth.

12. ExecutorService

🧒 Instead of hiring a brand-new chef for every single order (creating a new thread each time — slow and wasteful), a restaurant keeps a fixed team of chefs (a thread pool) and hands each new order to whichever chef is free.

Avoid creating a new platform thread for every unit of work.

ExecutorService executor =
    Executors.newFixedThreadPool(20);

executor.submit(() -> process());

A 1,000-thread pool is not automatically better. Excessive threads can cause context switching, memory consumption, CPU contention, thread starvation and latency spikes.

13. CompletableFuture

🧒 Ordering food at a counter: you get a buzzer (the CompletableFuture) and walk off to do other things instead of standing there waiting. When the food's ready, the buzzer goes off (.thenApply/.thenAccept) — and you can even chain "when my food's ready, also grab a drink" (.thenCombine).

For independent downstream calls, parallel execution can reduce end-to-end latency.

CompletableFuture<Account> account =
    CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
        () -> accountService.get(customerId)
    );

CompletableFuture<Credit> credit =
    CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
        () -> creditService.get(customerId)
    );

CompletableFuture<Rewards> rewards =
    CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
        () -> rewardService.get(customerId)
    );

CompletableFuture.allOf(
    account,
    credit,
    rewards
).join();
Production concerns: timeout, exception handling, cancellation, executor selection, circuit breakers and metrics.

Be careful with blocking I/O on the common ForkJoinPool. A dedicated, appropriately sized executor may be more suitable.

14. Virtual Threads

🧒 See the full truck-vs-bicycle-courier picture in 0.26 Platform vs Virtual Threads — this section covers the production trade-offs once you know the basic idea.
Traditional: Request ↓ Platform Thread ↓ Blocking I/O ↓ Thread remains occupied Virtual Thread: Request ↓ Virtual Thread ↓ Blocking I/O ↓ Carrier thread can execute other work

Virtual threads are especially useful for high-concurrency workloads dominated by blocking I/O. They do not remove database connection limits, downstream service capacity limits or CPU constraints.

Principal insight: A virtual thread can reduce thread-management overhead, but it cannot fix poor architecture or an overloaded dependency.

15. JVM Memory

JVM | ┌─────────┴─────────┐ ↓ ↓ Heap Non-Heap | | ┌─────┴─────┐ Metaspace ↓ ↓ Young Old

Know the concepts of Eden, survivor spaces, object promotion, heap sizing and metaspace.

16. Garbage Collection

For this role, you should be able to reason about production symptoms rather than merely name garbage collectors.

API latency ↓ increasing ↓ GC pauses ↓ CPU increases ↓ Throughput falls

Investigate heap usage, GC frequency, pause duration, allocation rate, old-generation occupancy, retained objects and heap dumps.

17. Java Memory Leak

Garbage collection cannot reclaim objects that are still reachable.

static List<Object> cache =
    new ArrayList<>();
Object ↓ Static List ↓ Still reachable ↓ GC cannot reclaim ↓ Heap grows ↓ GC pressure ↓ OutOfMemoryError

18. Production Incident Question

Scenario

Your Java API suddenly reaches 95% CPU in production. What do you do?

Strong investigation sequence

  1. Confirm scope and affected instances.
  2. Check traffic and request rate.
  3. Check recent deployments/configuration changes.
  4. Inspect CPU by process/thread.
  5. Take thread dumps.
  6. Check GC and heap behavior.
  7. Inspect database and downstream calls.
  8. Identify root cause.

Possible immediate mitigations

  • Scale out when appropriate.
  • Rollback a problematic deployment.
  • Rate-limit an abnormal traffic source.
  • Disable a problematic feature behind a feature flag.

Permanent correction

  • Code optimization
  • Query optimization
  • Concurrency correction
  • Capacity adjustment
  • Better test coverage
  • Improved monitoring and alerting

19. HashMap Interview Drill

When you execute:

map.put("CUSTOMER123", customer);
CUSTOMER123 ↓ hashCode() ↓ hash spreading ↓ bucket index ↓ existing entry? ↓ ┌────┴────┐ No Yes ↓ ↓ Insert Compare ↓ equals()

Average lookup is generally O(1) under a reasonable hash distribution. Collision-heavy buckets can change behavior; modern Java implementations can treeify sufficiently collision-heavy buckets under appropriate conditions.

20. equals() and hashCode()

If:

a.equals(b) == true

then:

a.hashCode() == b.hashCode()

must also hold. Violating this contract can break expected behavior in HashMap, HashSet and other hash-based collections.

Interview question: What happens if you override equals but not hashCode? Equal objects may produce different hash codes, causing lookups or duplicate detection to behave incorrectly.

21. String Immutability

String immutability supports security, thread safety, pooling and predictable hashing.

String s = "ABC";
s = s + "DEF";

A new String is created rather than modifying the original.

For repeated mutation, use StringBuilder in typical single-threaded code. StringBuffer is synchronized and is generally a legacy choice unless that synchronization behavior is specifically needed.

22. Exception Handling

Avoid swallowing failures:

try {
    process();
} catch (Exception e) {
    e.printStackTrace();
}

Enterprise applications should provide meaningful exception types, structured logging, correlation IDs, error codes, proper propagation and centralized handling.

In Spring, @RestControllerAdvice is useful for consistent API error handling.

Checked vs unchecked: Avoid dogmatic answers. Choose based on whether callers are expected to recover, the application error model and whether forcing every layer to catch an exception adds useful semantics or merely noise.

23. Java Design Patterns You Should Know

CreationalFactory, Abstract Factory, Builder, Singleton
StructuralAdapter, Decorator, Facade
BehavioralStrategy, Observer, Template Method, Chain of Responsibility, Command, State

Do not memorize UML diagrams. Be able to explain the problem each pattern solves and its trade-offs.

24. Strategy Pattern — Banking Example

Instead of a growing conditional block:

if (type == CARD) {
   ...
} else if (type == UPI) {
   ...
} else if (type == NEFT) {
   ...
}

Define a stable abstraction:

interface PaymentStrategy {

    PaymentResult process(
        PaymentRequest request
    );
}
PaymentService | ↓ PaymentStrategy | ┌────┼─────┬─────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ ↓ Card UPI NEFT RTGS

This makes new payment strategies easier to add without continually modifying the core service.

25. Factory Pattern

class PaymentStrategyFactory {

    PaymentStrategy get(PaymentType type) {

        return switch (type) {
            case CARD -> cardStrategy;
            case UPI -> upiStrategy;
            case NEFT -> neftStrategy;
            case RTGS -> rtgsStrategy;
        };
    }
}

26. Builder Pattern

Useful for objects with many optional parameters:

Payment payment =
    Payment.builder()
        .customerId(customerId)
        .amount(amount)
        .currency("INR")
        .channel("UPI")
        .build();

This is generally clearer than a constructor with a long list of positional parameters.

37. SOLID + Design Patterns — Complete Zero-to-Hero Java Guide

Every principle and pattern in this module follows the same learning sequence: simple meaning → real-world analogy → bad design → improved design → Java implementation → when to use → when not to use → interview question → exercise.

Important: Design patterns are not rules that must be applied everywhere. A pattern is a reusable way of solving a recurring design problem. The best design is often the simplest design that meets the requirements.
🧒 Kid analogy: SOLID principles are like tidy-room rules for code: each toy box should hold one kind of toy (Single Responsibility), you should be able to add a new toy box without reorganizing the whole room (Open/Closed), and toy boxes should be labeled clearly enough that anyone could swap one box for a similar one without surprises (Liskov/Interface Segregation/Dependency Inversion).

37.1 SOLID Overview

LetterPrincipleSimple Meaning
SSingle ResponsibilityOne class should have one primary reason to change.
OOpen/ClosedExtend behavior without repeatedly modifying stable code.
LLiskov SubstitutionA subtype must behave correctly wherever its abstraction is expected.
IInterface SegregationDo not force clients to depend on methods they do not need.
DDependency InversionHigh-level business logic should depend on abstractions, not implementation details.

37.2 S — Single Responsibility Principle

Simple meaning: A class should have one primary responsibility and therefore one main reason to change.

Analogy: In a restaurant, the chef cooks, the cashier handles payment and the delivery person delivers. One person doing everything becomes difficult to manage as the restaurant grows.

Bad Code

class Invoice {

    void calculateTotal() {
        // calculate price
    }

    void saveToDatabase() {
        // database code
    }

    void printInvoice() {
        // printing code
    }

    void sendEmail() {
        // email code
    }
}

This class has several unrelated responsibilities. Database technology, printing and email changes can all force changes to the same class.

Better Code

class InvoiceCalculator {
    BigDecimal calculate(Invoice invoice) {
        return invoice.items().stream()
            .map(Item::price)
            .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
    }
}

class InvoiceRepository {
    void save(Invoice invoice) {
        // database implementation
    }
}

class InvoicePrinter {
    void print(Invoice invoice) {
        // printing implementation
    }
}

class InvoiceMailer {
    void send(Invoice invoice) {
        // email implementation
    }
}

When to use

Use SRP when a class is accumulating unrelated business, infrastructure and presentation responsibilities or becomes difficult to test.

Do not misunderstand SRP

SRP does not mean “one method per class”. A class can contain several methods belonging to one cohesive responsibility.

Interview Question

Q: How do you identify an SRP violation?

A: Look for multiple unrelated reasons for change, mixed abstraction levels, excessive dependencies and tests that need to set up unrelated infrastructure.

Exercise

Refactor a UserService that validates users, writes SQL, sends email, generates PDF and logs audit events into cohesive components.

37.3 O — Open/Closed Principle

Simple meaning: Stable business logic should be open to new behavior through extension points but should not require repeated modification for every new variation.

Analogy: A power socket can support many compatible devices without rebuilding the house every time you buy a new appliance.

Bad Code

class PaymentService {

    void pay(String type, BigDecimal amount) {

        if (type.equals("CARD")) {
            // card payment
        } else if (type.equals("UPI")) {
            // UPI payment
        } else if (type.equals("BANK")) {
            // bank transfer
        }
    }
}

Better Code

interface PaymentMethod {
    void pay(BigDecimal amount);
}

class CardPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(BigDecimal amount) {
        System.out.println("Card payment: " + amount);
    }
}

class UpiPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(BigDecimal amount) {
        System.out.println("UPI payment: " + amount);
    }
}

class PaymentService {

    private final PaymentMethod method;

    PaymentService(PaymentMethod method) {
        this.method = method;
    }

    void pay(BigDecimal amount) {
        method.pay(amount);
    }
}

Production version

@Service
class PaymentService {

    private final Map<String, PaymentMethod> methods;

    PaymentService(List<PaymentMethod> implementations) {
        this.methods = implementations.stream()
            .collect(Collectors.toMap(
                PaymentMethod::type,
                Function.identity()
            ));
    }

    void pay(String type, BigDecimal amount) {
        PaymentMethod method = methods.get(type);

        if (method == null) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                "Unsupported payment type"
            );
        }

        method.pay(amount);
    }
}

Interview Question

Q: Is OCP the same as never modifying code?

A: No. The principle means designing stable areas so foreseeable variations can be added through appropriate abstractions instead of creating repeated conditional modifications.

37.4 L — Liskov Substitution Principle

Simple meaning: If code expects a parent abstraction, every valid subtype should behave in a way that does not violate the expectations of that abstraction.

Classic analogy: If a program expects a bird that can fly, making a penguin subtype of that specific “flying bird” abstraction creates a behavioral problem.

Bad Code

class Bird {
    void fly() {
        System.out.println("Flying");
    }
}

class Penguin extends Bird {
    @Override
    void fly() {
        throw new UnsupportedOperationException();
    }
}

Code using Bird reasonably expects fly() to work, but the subtype breaks that expectation.

Better Code

interface Bird {
    void move();
}

interface FlyingBird extends Bird {
    void fly();
}

class Eagle implements FlyingBird {
    public void move() {
        fly();
    }

    public void fly() {
        System.out.println("Flying");
    }
}

class Penguin implements Bird {
    public void move() {
        System.out.println("Walking/swimming");
    }
}

Enterprise Example

Do not create an abstraction such as DatabaseRepository that promises transactions, joins and relational semantics if one implementation is a document database that cannot preserve those guarantees.

Interview Question

Q: How do you detect an LSP violation?

A: Look for subtype methods throwing “not supported”, surprising state changes, stronger preconditions, weaker postconditions or callers needing explicit subtype checks.

37.5 I — Interface Segregation Principle

Simple meaning: Clients should not be forced to depend on methods they do not use.

Analogy: A driving licence should not require a taxi driver to learn unrelated aircraft controls.

Bad Code

interface Machine {
    void print();
    void scan();
    void fax();
}

class SimplePrinter implements Machine {

    public void print() {
        System.out.println("Printing");
    }

    public void scan() {
        throw new UnsupportedOperationException();
    }

    public void fax() {
        throw new UnsupportedOperationException();
    }
}

Better Code

interface Printer {
    void print();
}

interface Scanner {
    void scan();
}

interface Fax {
    void fax();
}

class SimplePrinter implements Printer {
    public void print() {
        System.out.println("Printing");
    }
}

class OfficeMachine
        implements Printer, Scanner, Fax {

    public void print() {}
    public void scan() {}
    public void fax() {}
}

Spring Boot example

interface CustomerReader {
    Customer findById(Long id);
}

interface CustomerWriter {
    Customer save(Customer customer);
}

A read-only service does not need to depend on write operations.

37.6 D — Dependency Inversion Principle

Simple meaning: Business logic should depend on abstractions. Infrastructure details should implement those abstractions.

Analogy: A phone charger follows a standard connector. The phone does not need to know the internal wiring of the electricity company.

Bad Code

class OrderService {

    private final MySqlOrderRepository repository =
        new MySqlOrderRepository();

    void create(Order order) {
        repository.save(order);
    }
}

Better Code

interface OrderRepository {
    void save(Order order);
}

class OrderService {

    private final OrderRepository repository;

    OrderService(OrderRepository repository) {
        this.repository = repository;
    }

    void create(Order order) {
        repository.save(order);
    }
}

class MySqlOrderRepository
        implements OrderRepository {

    public void save(Order order) {
        // MySQL implementation
    }
}

Why Spring loves DIP

Spring's dependency injection container can provide implementations of interfaces, allowing business code to remain independent of infrastructure details.

37.7 SOLID — One Complete Enterprise Example

interface PaymentGateway {
    PaymentResult charge(Money money);
}

interface PaymentRepository {
    void save(Payment payment);
}

interface FraudChecker {
    FraudResult check(Payment payment);
}

class PaymentApplicationService {

    private final PaymentGateway gateway;
    private final PaymentRepository repository;
    private final FraudChecker fraudChecker;

    PaymentApplicationService(
            PaymentGateway gateway,
            PaymentRepository repository,
            FraudChecker fraudChecker) {

        this.gateway = gateway;
        this.repository = repository;
        this.fraudChecker = fraudChecker;
    }

    PaymentResult pay(Payment payment) {

        FraudResult fraud =
            fraudChecker.check(payment);

        if (fraud.isRejected()) {
            return PaymentResult.rejected();
        }

        PaymentResult result =
            gateway.charge(payment.money());

        repository.save(payment);

        return result;
    }
}

This example demonstrates SRP, OCP, LSP, ISP and DIP together through focused abstractions.

37.8 Design Patterns — How to Learn Them

Problem ↓ Naive Solution ↓ Problem at Scale ↓ Pattern ↓ Java Implementation ↓ Trade-offs ↓ Production Usage

37.9 Creational Patterns

Pattern 1 — Singleton

Problem: Ensure a single shared instance where that lifecycle is genuinely required.

Java-safe implementation:

public enum Configuration {
    INSTANCE;

    private final String environment = "prod";

    public String environment() {
        return environment;
    }
}

Spring note: Spring beans are singleton-scoped by default, but Spring-managed singleton is not identical to a globally accessible Java singleton.

Use: Carefully controlled stateless shared components.

Avoid: Global mutable state disguised as a singleton.

Pattern 2 — Factory Method

Problem: Create objects without forcing callers to know concrete implementation details.

interface Notification {
    void send(String message);
}

class EmailNotification implements Notification {
    public void send(String message) {
        System.out.println("Email: " + message);
    }
}

class SmsNotification implements Notification {
    public void send(String message) {
        System.out.println("SMS: " + message);
    }
}

class NotificationFactory {

    static Notification create(String type) {

        return switch (type) {
            case "EMAIL" -> new EmailNotification();
            case "SMS" -> new SmsNotification();
            default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                "Unknown notification type"
            );
        };
    }
}

Interview point: Factory centralizes creation logic; it does not automatically mean the whole application should use factories.

Pattern 3 — Abstract Factory

Problem: Create related families of objects that must work together.

interface Button {
    void render();
}

interface Dialog {
    void render();
}

interface UiFactory {
    Button createButton();
    Dialog createDialog();
}

class WebFactory implements UiFactory {
    public Button createButton() {
        return new WebButton();
    }

    public Dialog createDialog() {
        return new WebDialog();
    }
}

Example: A platform may need a complete family of AWS-specific or Azure-specific infrastructure adapters.

Pattern 4 — Builder

Problem: Construct objects with many optional parameters without unreadable constructors.

public class ServerConfig {

    private final String host;
    private final int port;
    private final boolean tls;
    private final int timeout;

    private ServerConfig(Builder b) {
        this.host = b.host;
        this.port = b.port;
        this.tls = b.tls;
        this.timeout = b.timeout;
    }

    public static class Builder {

        private String host;
        private int port = 8080;
        private boolean tls;
        private int timeout = 2000;

        public Builder host(String host) {
            this.host = host;
            return this;
        }

        public Builder port(int port) {
            this.port = port;
            return this;
        }

        public Builder tls(boolean tls) {
            this.tls = tls;
            return this;
        }

        public Builder timeout(int timeout) {
            this.timeout = timeout;
            return this;
        }

        public ServerConfig build() {
            return new ServerConfig(this);
        }
    }
}
ServerConfig config =
    new ServerConfig.Builder()
        .host("api.example.com")
        .port(443)
        .tls(true)
        .timeout(3000)
        .build();

Pattern 5 — Prototype

Problem: Create a new object by copying an existing configured object.

record ReportTemplate(
    String name,
    List<String> columns
) {
    ReportTemplate copy() {
        return new ReportTemplate(
            name,
            List.copyOf(columns)
        );
    }
}

Useful when object construction is expensive or when a configured template is reused as a starting point.

37.10 Structural Patterns

Pattern 6 — Adapter

Problem: Make incompatible interfaces work together.

interface PaymentGateway {
    void pay(BigDecimal amount);
}

class LegacyBankApi {
    void transfer(double amount) {
        System.out.println("Legacy transfer");
    }
}

class BankAdapter implements PaymentGateway {

    private final LegacyBankApi bank;

    BankAdapter(LegacyBankApi bank) {
        this.bank = bank;
    }

    public void pay(BigDecimal amount) {
        bank.transfer(amount.doubleValue());
    }
}

Enterprise use: Integrating an old SOAP provider behind a modern REST-oriented interface.

Pattern 7 — Bridge

Problem: Separate two dimensions that should evolve independently.

interface MessageSender {
    void send(String message);
}

class EmailSender implements MessageSender {
    public void send(String message) {}
}

class SmsSender implements MessageSender {
    public void send(String message) {}
}

abstract class Notification {
    protected final MessageSender sender;

    protected Notification(MessageSender sender) {
        this.sender = sender;
    }

    abstract void notifyUser(String message);
}

class Alert extends Notification {

    Alert(MessageSender sender) {
        super(sender);
    }

    void notifyUser(String message) {
        sender.send(message);
    }
}

Pattern 8 — Composite

Problem: Treat individual objects and groups of objects uniformly.

interface FileSystemNode {
    void print();
}

class FileNode implements FileSystemNode {
    public void print() {
        System.out.println("File");
    }
}

class Folder implements FileSystemNode {

    private final List<FileSystemNode> children =
        new ArrayList<>();

    void add(FileSystemNode node) {
        children.add(node);
    }

    public void print() {
        children.forEach(FileSystemNode::print);
    }
}

Pattern 9 — Decorator

Problem: Add behavior dynamically without creating many subclasses.

interface Coffee {
    double cost();
}

class BasicCoffee implements Coffee {
    public double cost() {
        return 100;
    }
}

class MilkDecorator implements Coffee {

    private final Coffee coffee;

    MilkDecorator(Coffee coffee) {
        this.coffee = coffee;
    }

    public double cost() {
        return coffee.cost() + 20;
    }
}

Pattern 10 — Facade

Problem: Give callers a simple interface over a complicated subsystem.

class PaymentFacade {

    private final FraudService fraud;
    private final BankService bank;
    private final LedgerService ledger;

    PaymentFacade(
            FraudService fraud,
            BankService bank,
            LedgerService ledger) {
        this.fraud = fraud;
        this.bank = bank;
        this.ledger = ledger;
    }

    void pay(Payment payment) {

        fraud.check(payment);
        bank.charge(payment);
        ledger.record(payment);
    }
}

The facade hides workflow complexity from the caller.

Pattern 11 — Flyweight

Problem: Share common immutable state to reduce memory use.

class IconFactory {

    private static final Map<String, Icon> cache =
        new HashMap<>();

    static Icon get(String name) {
        return cache.computeIfAbsent(
            name,
            Icon::new
        );
    }
}

Pattern 12 — Proxy

Problem: Control access to another object or add behavior around it.

interface CustomerService {
    Customer find(Long id);
}

class LoggingProxy implements CustomerService {

    private final CustomerService target;

    LoggingProxy(CustomerService target) {
        this.target = target;
    }

    public Customer find(Long id) {
        System.out.println("find customer " + id);
        return target.find(id);
    }
}

Spring examples: transactions, security, caching and many AOP features rely on proxy-based mechanisms.

37.11 Behavioral Patterns

Pattern 13 — Strategy

Problem: Select one of several algorithms or business rules.

interface PricingStrategy {
    BigDecimal price(Order order);
}

class RegularPricing implements PricingStrategy {
    public BigDecimal price(Order order) {
        return order.total();
    }
}

class PremiumPricing implements PricingStrategy {
    public BigDecimal price(Order order) {
        return order.total()
            .multiply(new BigDecimal("0.90"));
    }
}

class PricingService {

    private final PricingStrategy strategy;

    PricingService(PricingStrategy strategy) {
        this.strategy = strategy;
    }

    BigDecimal calculate(Order order) {
        return strategy.price(order);
    }
}

Use: Pricing, tax, fraud rules, routing, authentication providers.

Pattern 14 — Observer

Problem: Notify multiple interested components when something changes.

interface OrderListener {
    void orderCreated(Order order);
}

class EmailListener implements OrderListener {
    public void orderCreated(Order order) {
        System.out.println("Send email");
    }
}

class OrderPublisher {

    private final List<OrderListener> listeners =
        new ArrayList<>();

    void register(OrderListener listener) {
        listeners.add(listener);
    }

    void publish(Order order) {
        listeners.forEach(
            l -> l.orderCreated(order)
        );
    }
}

In distributed systems, the event-bus version of this idea decouples services more strongly.

Pattern 15 — Command

Problem: Represent an action as an object so it can be queued, logged, retried or undone where appropriate.

interface Command {
    void execute();
}

class CreateOrderCommand implements Command {

    private final OrderService service;

    CreateOrderCommand(OrderService service) {
        this.service = service;
    }

    public void execute() {
        service.create();
    }
}

Pattern 16 — Chain of Responsibility

Problem: Pass a request through a sequence of handlers until it is processed or rejected.

interface Handler {
    void handle(Request request);
}

class AuthenticationHandler implements Handler {

    private Handler next;

    public void handle(Request request) {

        if (!request.authenticated()) {
            throw new SecurityException();
        }

        if (next != null) {
            next.handle(request);
        }
    }

    void next(Handler next) {
        this.next = next;
    }
}

Spring example: Servlet filters and security filter chains use similar ideas.

Pattern 17 — State

Problem: An object's behavior changes according to its current state.

enum OrderState {
    CREATED,
    PAID,
    SHIPPED,
    CANCELLED
}

class Order {

    private OrderState state = OrderState.CREATED;

    void pay() {
        if (state != OrderState.CREATED) {
            throw new IllegalStateException();
        }
        state = OrderState.PAID;
    }

    void ship() {
        if (state != OrderState.PAID) {
            throw new IllegalStateException();
        }
        state = OrderState.SHIPPED;
    }
}

For complex workflows, explicit state-machine modeling can be clearer than scattered conditionals.

Pattern 18 — Template Method

Problem: Define a common algorithm structure while allowing subclasses to customize selected steps.

abstract class DataImporter {

    public final void importData() {
        read();
        validate();
        transform();
        save();
    }

    abstract void read();
    abstract void validate();
    abstract void transform();

    void save() {
        System.out.println("Saving");
    }
}

Pattern 19 — Iterator

Problem: Traverse a collection without exposing its internal structure.

List<String> users =
    List.of("A", "B", "C");

Iterator<String> iterator =
    users.iterator();

while (iterator.hasNext()) {
    System.out.println(iterator.next());
}

Pattern 20 — Mediator

Problem: Reduce direct dependencies between many interacting objects by centralizing coordination.

class ChatMediator {

    private final List<User> users =
        new ArrayList<>();

    void register(User user) {
        users.add(user);
    }

    void send(User sender, String message) {
        for (User user : users) {
            if (user != sender) {
                user.receive(message);
            }
        }
    }
}

Pattern 21 — Memento

Problem: Save an object's state so it can later be restored.

record EditorState(String text) {}

class Editor {

    private String text;

    EditorState save() {
        return new EditorState(text);
    }

    void restore(EditorState state) {
        this.text = state.text();
    }
}

Pattern 22 — Visitor

Problem: Add operations over a stable object structure without putting every operation into the structure itself.

interface Shape {
    void accept(ShapeVisitor visitor);
}

interface ShapeVisitor {
    void visit(Circle circle);
    void visit(Rectangle rectangle);
}

Useful when the object structure is stable but operations change frequently. Avoid it when the hierarchy changes constantly.

37.12 Enterprise Patterns You Must Also Know

Pattern 23 — Repository

Provides a domain-oriented interface to persistence.

interface CustomerRepository {
    Optional<Customer> findById(CustomerId id);
    Customer save(Customer customer);
}

Pattern 24 — Service Layer

Groups application/business operations and transaction boundaries.

@Service
class TransferService {

    @Transactional
    public void transfer(
            AccountId from,
            AccountId to,
            Money amount) {

        // business workflow
    }
}

Pattern 25 — DTO

Separates external API representation from internal domain objects.

record CustomerResponse(
    Long id,
    String name
) {}

Pattern 26 — Dependency Injection

@Service
class OrderService {

    private final PaymentGateway gateway;

    OrderService(PaymentGateway gateway) {
        this.gateway = gateway;
    }
}

Pattern 27 — Specification

Represents reusable business predicates.

interface Specification<T> {
    boolean isSatisfiedBy(T value);
}

class PremiumCustomerSpecification
        implements Specification<Customer> {

    public boolean isSatisfiedBy(Customer c) {
        return c.totalSpend()
            .compareTo(new BigDecimal("100000")) >= 0;
    }
}

Pattern 28 — Unit of Work

Tracks changes and commits them as a coordinated unit. In modern Spring/JPA applications, transaction and persistence-context mechanisms often provide much of this behavior.

Pattern 29 — CQRS

Command Query Responsibility Segregation separates operations that change state from operations that read state.

Commands ↓ Write Model ↓ Events ↓ Read Model ↓ Queries

Use only when the business and scaling requirements justify the extra complexity.

Pattern 30 — Saga

A distributed business transaction is implemented as a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions.

Order Created
 ↓
Payment
 ↓
Inventory
 ↓
Shipment

If Inventory fails:
    compensate Payment
    cancel Order

Pattern 31 — Transactional Outbox

@Transactional
void createOrder(Order order) {

    orderRepository.save(order);

    outboxRepository.save(
        OutboxEvent.orderCreated(order)
    );
}

Pattern 32 — Circuit Breaker

@CircuitBreaker(
    name = "payment",
    fallbackMethod = "fallback"
)
PaymentResult pay(Payment payment) {
    return gateway.pay(payment);
}

Pattern 33 — Retry with Backoff

@Retry(
    name = "bank",
    fallbackMethod = "fallback"
)
PaymentResult callBank() {
    return bankClient.pay();
}

Pattern 34 — Bulkhead

Separate resources so one failing workload does not consume all threads/connections.

Shared Application ├── Payment Pool ├── Notification Pool └── Reporting Pool Reporting overload ↓ Reporting pool exhausted Payment pool remains healthy

Pattern 35 — Rate Limiter

if (limiter.allow(customerId)) {
    process();
} else {
    throw new TooManyRequestsException();
}

Pattern 36 — Strangler Fig

Legacy Monolith ↓ Routing Layer ├── Old Capability └── New Capability Repeat until old capability disappears.

37.13 Pattern Selection Cheat Sheet

ProblemConsider
Many object construction optionsBuilder
Object creation variesFactory
Incompatible external interfaceAdapter
Add behavior around an objectDecorator / Proxy
Hide complex subsystemFacade
Multiple algorithmsStrategy
Notify many listenersObserver / Events
Queue or log actionsCommand
Different behavior by stateState
Sequential validationChain of Responsibility
Stable workflow with variable stepsTemplate Method
Shared persistence abstractionRepository
Distributed workflowSaga
DB + event atomicityTransactional Outbox
Read/write model complexityCQRS
Prevent dependency failure cascadeCircuit Breaker / Bulkhead
Gradual legacy replacementStrangler

37.14 Design Pattern Interview Questions

  1. Explain Factory vs Abstract Factory.
  2. Builder vs Factory — when would you choose each?
  3. Adapter vs Facade?
  4. Decorator vs Proxy?
  5. Strategy vs State?
  6. Observer vs event-driven architecture?
  7. Why is Singleton often criticized?
  8. Where does Spring use proxies?
  9. Where does Spring use Factory-like behavior?
  10. Explain Repository and Service Layer.
  11. Explain CQRS and when not to use it.
  12. Explain Saga and compensation.
  13. Explain transactional outbox.
  14. How would you combine Strategy + Factory?
  15. How would you combine Circuit Breaker + Retry + Timeout?

37.15 Combined Enterprise Example — Payment Architecture

Payment API ↓ Facade ↓ Payment Service ├── Strategy → Payment Provider ├── Repository → Database ├── Specification → Validation ├── Circuit Breaker → Provider ├── Retry → Transient Errors ├── Outbox → Event ↓ Event Bus ↓ Observer-like Consumers ├── Fraud ├── Ledger └── Notification

37.16 Pattern Anti-Patterns

  • Pattern explosion: Creating interfaces and factories for every two-line operation.
  • Singleton abuse: Using global mutable state because it is convenient.
  • Repository everywhere: Adding unnecessary abstractions around simple persistence.
  • CQRS everywhere: Introducing separate read/write models without a real requirement.
  • Microservices by pattern: Splitting services because architecture diagrams look impressive.
  • Inheritance for reuse: Using inheritance where composition is safer.

37.17 Pattern Exercise — Build a Payment Engine

Implement these stages:

  1. Start with one PaymentService.
  2. Add Strategy for payment method.
  3. Add Factory for selecting the strategy.
  4. Add Adapter for a legacy bank API.
  5. Add Facade for the payment workflow.
  6. Add Repository for persistence.
  7. Add Specification for validation.
  8. Add Circuit Breaker and Retry.
  9. Add Outbox for events.
  10. Add Saga if the payment workflow becomes distributed.

37.18 Pattern Mastery Test

Before calling yourself comfortable with a pattern, you should be able to:

  • Explain the problem in simple language.
  • Draw the pattern without looking at notes.
  • Write a small Java implementation.
  • Explain an alternative design.
  • Explain why the pattern may be unnecessary.
  • Identify a real Spring Boot usage.
  • Explain failure modes.
  • Explain performance/complexity trade-offs.
Principal Engineer rule: A strong architect does not say “I used Factory, Strategy and CQRS.” A strong architect says “We had three independent payment algorithms and frequent provider additions, so I isolated the algorithm behind Strategy and used a small creation mechanism. CQRS was rejected because our read/write scale and consistency requirements did not justify its operational cost.”

27. Principal-Level Coding Exercises

Exercise 1 — Thread-Safe Rate Limiter

Requirement: a customer can make 100 requests per minute.

Request ↓ RateLimiter ↓ Redis ↓ Allowed? ┌─┴─┐ Yes No ↓ ↓ API Reject

Know the trade-offs among Fixed Window, Sliding Window, Token Bucket and Leaky Bucket. In a distributed deployment, explain why an in-memory counter is insufficient across multiple application instances.

Exercise 2 — LRU Cache

Requirements: capacity 1,000; GET O(1); PUT O(1); thread-safe.

HashMap | ↓ Node ↔ Node ↔ Node

Use a HashMap for lookup and a doubly linked list for recency ordering. Then discuss concurrency and eviction semantics.

Exercise 3 — Retry with Exponential Backoff

Attempt 1 → immediate Attempt 2 → 100 ms Attempt 3 → 200 ms Attempt 4 → 400 ms Attempt 5 → 800 ms

Production implementation should consider maximum retries, maximum delay, jitter, retryable/non-retryable errors, timeout, circuit breaker and idempotency.

Important: Retry storms.

1,000 requests × 3 retries can create 3,000 additional calls against an already unhealthy dependency. Use exponential backoff, jitter, circuit breakers, retry budgets, timeouts, bulkheads and rate limiting.

28. Mock Interview — System Design Challenge

Interviewer Question

You have a Spring Boot payment service receiving 10,000 requests/sec. Each request needs to:

  1. Validate customer
  2. Check account balance
  3. Run fraud detection
  4. Debit account
  5. Write ledger entry
  6. Send notification
  7. Publish analytics event

The system must provide 99.99% availability, low latency, no duplicate payments, strong financial consistency, horizontal scalability and auditability.

Design this system.

Do not jump immediately to Kafka, Redis or microservices.

A Principal Engineer should first clarify:

  • What must be synchronous?
  • What can be asynchronous?
  • What consistency is required?
  • What happens if the fraud service is unavailable?
  • What happens if notification fails?
  • What happens if the client retries?
  • What happens if Kafka is unavailable?
  • What is the transaction boundary?
  • How do we guarantee no double debit?
  • How do we recover from partial failure?

Target answer structure

1Requirements
2Assumptions
3Capacity
4High-level architecture
5Transaction boundary
6Idempotency
7Data model
8Event architecture
9Failure handling
10Security
11Scaling
12Observability
13Disaster recovery
14Trade-offs
Interview standard: The strongest answer will explain the business and consistency requirements first, establish the financial transaction boundary, design idempotency, separate critical synchronous work from asynchronous downstream work, address partial failures, and then cover scalability, security, observability, disaster recovery and cost.

29. Module 2 — Spring Boot Internals + Microservices Deep Dive

This module covers the Spring and distributed-architecture depth expected from a Principal Java Full Stack Engineer.

Principal mindset: Explain not only what annotation or pattern you use, but why, how it behaves at runtime, how it fails, how it scales, how it is secured and how it is observed in production.
🧒 Kid analogy: Spring Boot is like a smart toy-building kit. Instead of screwing every tiny part together yourself (creating objects, wiring dependencies by hand), you tell the kit "I need a car" and it hands you a fully assembled one, batteries included (auto-configuration) — you just snap on the special parts you actually care about.

29.1 Spring Boot Architecture

Spring Boot Application | ↓ SpringApplication | ↓ ApplicationContext ┌────┼──────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Beans Environment Auto Configuration | | └──────────┬──────────┘ ↓ Bean Creation ↓ Dependency Injection ↓ BeanPostProcessors ↓ AOP Proxies ↓ Ready Application

29.2 IoC and Dependency Injection

public interface PaymentGateway {
    PaymentResponse process(PaymentRequest request);
}

@Service
public class PaymentService {
    private final PaymentGateway gateway;

    public PaymentService(PaymentGateway gateway) {
        this.gateway = gateway;
    }
}

Constructor injection makes dependencies explicit, supports immutability and simplifies testing.

🧒 IoC/DI in one line: instead of a chef going out to buy their own ingredients (creating dependencies with new), the kitchen manager (Spring) delivers exactly the ingredients the recipe asked for, right before cooking starts.

29.3 Bean Lifecycle and AOP

Bean Definition → Instantiate → Inject Dependencies ↓ BeanPostProcessor → @PostConstruct → Ready ↓ @PreDestroy → Destroy Caller → Spring Proxy → Advice/Interceptor → Target Bean

Know singleton/prototype scopes, BeanPostProcessor, initialization order, proxy creation and why self-invocation can bypass proxy-based advice.

🧒 AOP is like a security guard standing invisibly in front of certain methods: before you enter (a method call), the guard can check your badge, log your visit, or even stop you — all without the room itself (your actual code) knowing the guard is there.

29.4 @Transactional — Principal Level

@Transactional
public PaymentResult processPayment(PaymentRequest request) {
    validate(request);
    Payment payment = paymentRepository.save(createPayment(request));
    ledgerRepository.save(createLedgerEntry(payment));
    return PaymentResult.success(payment);
}
PropagationHow a method joins or creates a transaction.
IsolationHow concurrent transactions observe data.
RollbackWhich failures cause rollback.
BoundaryWhere atomicity starts and ends.
Critical: A database transaction does not automatically span multiple microservices. Use an explicit distributed consistency strategy such as Saga, transactional messaging or another suitable pattern.
🧒 A transaction is like writing a whole letter in pencil, then deciding at the very end: either the whole letter gets inked over permanently (commit), or you erase every pencil mark as if you never started (rollback) — never just half the letter.

29.5 Propagation and Isolation

PropagationMeaningTypical Use
REQUIREDJoin existing or create transactionDefault business operation
REQUIRES_NEWSuspend existing and create newIndependent audit operation
SUPPORTSUse transaction if presentOptional transactional reads
MANDATORYExisting transaction requiredStrict transactional contract
NOT_SUPPORTEDRun without transactionOperations that should not hold one
READ UNCOMMITTEDAllows dirty reads.
READ COMMITTEDPrevents dirty reads; common enterprise choice.
REPEATABLE READStronger repeatability; database behavior matters.
SERIALIZABLEStrongest isolation, potentially expensive.

29.6 REST API Design

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/v1/payments")
public class PaymentController {

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<PaymentResponse> create(
            @RequestHeader("Idempotency-Key") String key,
            @Valid @RequestBody PaymentRequest request) {
        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.CREATED)
            .body(paymentService.create(key, request));
    }

    @GetMapping("/{id}")
    public PaymentResponse get(@PathVariable String id) {
        return paymentService.get(id);
    }
}
  • Resource-oriented URLs and HTTP semantics
  • Versioning and backward compatibility
  • Pagination, filtering and validation
  • Idempotency and correlation IDs
  • Consistent error contracts
  • Authentication, authorization and rate limiting
{
  "timestamp": "2026-08-24T15:20:00Z",
  "status": 409,
  "code": "PAYMENT_ALREADY_PROCESSED",
  "message": "The payment has already been processed.",
  "correlationId": "7c31e2a9"
}

Use @RestControllerAdvice for centralized exception mapping and never expose internal stack traces.

29.7 Spring Security

Client → Identity Provider → OAuth2/OIDC → Access Token → API Gateway → Spring Security → Authentication → Authorization → Controller / Service

Prepare OAuth2, OIDC, JWT validation, issuer/audience, key rotation, RBAC, method security, service-to-service authentication and mTLS concepts.

29.8 Microservices — Do Not Start With Microservices
