Business Domains → Bounded Contexts → Team Ownership → Data Ownership → Scaling → Deployment Independence → Operational Maturity → Service Boundaries
Strong answer: I would not decompose a monolith simply because microservices are fashionable. I would use domain boundaries, ownership, independent scaling, release cadence and operational constraints to determine boundaries.

29.9 Reference Architecture

Clients | API Gateway | ┌───────────────────┼────────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Customer Service Account Service Payment Service | | | Customer DB Account DB Payment DB | ↓ Event Platform Kafka / Pulsar | ┌──────────────┼───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Fraud Notification Analytics Service Service Consumer

Prefer database ownership by service. Avoid allowing every service to directly modify another service's tables.

29.10 Synchronous vs Asynchronous Communication

SynchronousAsynchronous
Immediate response requiredWork can happen later
Interactive request/responseLoose coupling
Immediate validationMultiple consumers need an event
Simple transactional flowBuffering/backpressure is useful

29.11 Resilience Patterns

TimeoutNever wait indefinitely.
RetryRecover from transient failures.
Circuit BreakerStop calling unhealthy dependencies.
BulkheadIsolate resources between dependencies.
Rate LimitingProtect services from excessive traffic.
FallbackControlled degraded behavior where business rules allow it.
CLOSED → failures exceed threshold → OPEN OPEN → wait/probe → HALF OPEN HALF OPEN → success → CLOSED HALF OPEN → failure → OPEN

Timeouts and circuit breakers solve different problems. A circuit breaker without timeouts can still leave resources blocked.

🧒 A circuit breaker is like a kid who calls a friend's house, and after the phone rings with no answer three times in a row, stops dialing for a while (OPEN) instead of redialing forever — then tries just once (HALF OPEN) to check if someone's home before going back to calling normally (CLOSED).

29.12 Bulkheads and Retry Storms

Application ├── Pool A → Payment ├── Pool B → Customer └── Pool C → Notification Notification failure → Payment pool remains protected

Retries require exponential backoff, jitter, retry budgets, timeouts and idempotency. Otherwise 1,000 failed requests with three retries can create 3,000 additional calls against an unhealthy dependency.

29.13 API Gateway and Service Discovery

The gateway can handle routing, selected authentication/policy enforcement, rate limiting and observability. Do not put core business logic there.

payment-service → Kubernetes Service → DNS → Payment Pods ┌───┼───┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Pod Pod Pod

29.14 Distributed Tracing

User Request → Gateway traceId=ABC ├→ Customer Service ├→ Account Service └→ Payment Service ├→ Database └→ Fraud Service

Know traces, spans, context propagation, correlation IDs and sampling. Monitor p50/p95/p99 latency, errors, saturation, dependency latency and queue lag.

29.15 Outbox Pattern

Problem: DB commit succeeds → Kafka publish fails → consumers miss event Solution: Single DB transaction ├── Payment record └── Outbox event ↓ Event Publisher ↓ Kafka/Pulsar

The publisher can retry delivery. Consumers must remain idempotent because duplicate delivery is still possible.

🧒 The outbox pattern is like writing both your diary entry AND the "please mail this postcard" note in the very same notebook in one sitting — so you can never end up having written the diary entry but forgotten to write the postcard note, even if the mailbox trip happens later.

29.16 Saga Pattern

Order → Payment → Inventory → Shipping | failure ↓ Compensating action ↓ Refund / Cancel
OrchestrationChoreography
Central coordinator controls workflow.Services react to domain events.
Explicit complex workflow.Less centralized control.
Easier to visualize.Can become hard to trace as event chains grow.
🧒 A Saga is like planning a 4-step group trip (book flight → book hotel → book car → book tour). If the car booking fails, you don't just leave the flight and hotel booked and walk away — you undo them (cancel/refund), step by step backward, so nothing is left half-booked.

29.17 CQRS and Event Sourcing

Commands → Write Model → Events → Read Model → Queries

CQRS is useful when read and write requirements differ substantially. Event sourcing stores the event history rather than only current state. Be ready to discuss replay, snapshots, schema evolution, storage growth and eventual consistency.

29.18 Idempotency

POST /payments
Idempotency-Key: PAYMENT-12345
First request → process → SUCCESS → persist result Retry → same key → already processed → return existing result

Idempotency is a business property. Use keys, persistence and uniqueness constraints where appropriate.

29.19 Failure Matrix

FailurePotential Response
Fraud timeoutTimeout + explicit fail-open/fail-closed risk policy.
Database unavailableFail fast, safe retry where appropriate, alert and recover.
Kafka unavailablePersist through Outbox and publish later.
Notification unavailableDo not roll back a financial transaction merely because notification failed.
Duplicate requestIdempotency key + unique constraint.
Duplicate eventIdempotent consumer.
Out-of-order eventVersioning, ordering strategy or state validation.

29.20 Microservice Anti-Patterns

Distributed MonolithMany services but tightly coupled deployments.
Shared DatabaseEvery service directly changes every table.
Chatty ServicesOne request causes dozens of synchronous calls.
God ServiceOne service owns most business logic.
Event EverythingMessaging without a real decoupling need.
Shared Domain ModelAll services forced into one internal model.

29.21 Legacy Modernization — Strangler Pattern

Legacy Monolith → Routing / Facade ├→ Existing Monolith └→ New Service → New Capability Repeat until legacy capability shrinks

This supports incremental modernization rather than a risky big-bang rewrite.

29.22 Principal-Level Scenario

Interviewer

Your payment microservice calls Account, Fraud and Notification synchronously. Production latency is increasing and the entire payment API sometimes becomes unavailable when Fraud is slow. What would you change?

  1. Measure dependency latency with tracing.
  2. Define what must be synchronous for business correctness.
  3. Add strict timeouts.
  4. Introduce circuit breakers.
  5. Use bulkheads so Fraud cannot exhaust Payment resources.
  6. Make Notification asynchronous when possible.
  7. Use Outbox + event bus for reliable downstream events.
  8. Define fraud failure semantics from risk policy.
  9. Add idempotency.
  10. Load test with degraded dependencies.
  11. Monitor p50/p95/p99, errors, saturation and consumer lag.
Principal-level answer: The goal is not simply “make it asynchronous.” Establish the business transaction boundary, isolate failure domains, preserve financial correctness and prevent a slow dependency from taking down the platform.

29.23 Interview Questions

SpringHow does DI work? What is the bean lifecycle? How does AOP work? Why does self-invocation affect transactions? How does @Transactional work?
MicroservicesWhen would you choose a modular monolith? How do you define boundaries? How do you handle distributed transactions?
ResilienceHow do you prevent cascading failures? When do you retry? How do circuit breakers and bulkheads differ?
EventsWhat problem does Outbox solve? How do you handle duplicates and out-of-order events?
OperationsHow do you debug a request crossing 15 services? How do you perform zero-downtime deployments?
SecurityHow do you secure APIs and service-to-service communication?

29.24 Hands-On Build — Production Payment Service

React Client → API Gateway → Spring Security → Payment Service | ┌───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ PostgreSQL Redis Account/Fraud | ↓ Outbox ↓ Kafka / Pulsar ┌────┼─────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Notification Audit Analytics
  • Spring Boot REST API
  • OAuth2/OIDC
  • Idempotency
  • PostgreSQL transaction boundary
  • Redis where appropriate
  • Timeouts, retries and circuit breakers
  • Kafka/Pulsar + Outbox
  • Idempotent consumers
  • Structured logs, metrics and tracing
  • Unit/integration/performance tests
  • Docker, Kubernetes and CI/CD

30. Module 3 — Distributed Systems + System Design Mastery

This module targets the highest-value Principal Engineer interview capability: designing reliable, scalable, secure and observable distributed systems while clearly explaining trade-offs.

Principal Engineer mindset: A system-design answer is not a list of technologies. Start with business requirements and constraints, quantify scale, define correctness, establish boundaries, then choose architecture based on explicit trade-offs.
🧒 Kid analogy: One lemonade stand (single server) is easy to run — one till, one jug of lemonade. A distributed system is a whole chain of lemonade stands across town: they need to agree on prices (consistency), keep serving customers even if one stand runs out of cups (availability/fault tolerance), and decide how to split the neighborhood between them (partitioning) — that's the whole game of distributed systems.

30.1 The Principal System Design Framework

1. Clarify Requirements ↓ 2. Functional Requirements ↓ 3. Non-Functional Requirements ↓ 4. Capacity Estimation ↓ 5. API / Interface Design ↓ 6. Data Model ↓ 7. High-Level Architecture ↓ 8. Detailed Components ↓ 9. Caching / Messaging / Storage ↓ 10. Security ↓ 11. Reliability / Failure Handling ↓ 12. Observability ↓ 13. Disaster Recovery ↓ 14. Cost ↓ 15. Trade-offs / Alternatives

30.2 Functional vs Non-Functional Requirements

FunctionalNon-Functional
Create payment99.99% availability
Transfer moneyp99 latency target
Generate statementHorizontal scalability
Send notificationSecurity and compliance
Search transactionsDisaster recovery
Audit activityObservability and supportability

Always ask the interviewer what matters most. A banking ledger, social feed and analytics pipeline should not be designed with identical consistency and latency requirements.

30.3 Capacity Estimation

Before drawing 20 boxes, estimate the workload.

TrafficRequests/sec, peak requests/sec, read/write ratio.
StorageRecords/day, record size, retention period.
BandwidthIngress and egress volume.
ConcurrencyConcurrent users and in-flight operations.
GrowthExpected annual/monthly growth.
AvailabilitySLO and allowable downtime.
Interview rule: State assumptions clearly. Precise-looking numbers based on hidden assumptions are less useful than transparent estimates.

30.4 Example Capacity Estimate

Suppose an application has:

  • 10 million users
  • 1 million daily active users
  • 10 requests/user/day on average
  • 10 million requests/day

Average request rate is approximately:

10,000,000 / 86,400
≈ 116 requests/sec

If peak traffic is 10× average:
≈ 1,160 requests/sec peak

Now design for the stated peak plus a reasonable safety margin rather than simply sizing for the daily average.

30.5 Latency Percentiles

Requests | ├── p50 = median ├── p95 = 95% faster than this ├── p99 = 99% faster than this └── p99.9 = tail latency

Principal engineers should discuss tail latency. A system can have a healthy average while a small but important percentage of users experience severe delays.

30.6 Availability and SLO Thinking

AvailabilityApprox. downtime/year
99%~3.65 days
99.9%~8.76 hours
99.99%~52.6 minutes
99.999%~5.26 minutes

Do not promise five nines casually. Higher availability usually requires additional redundancy, operational maturity and cost.

30.7 CAP Theorem

Consistency /\ / \ / \ / \ / \ Partition ---------------- Availability Tolerance

CAP concerns behavior when a network partition occurs. A distributed system cannot simultaneously guarantee perfect consistency and perfect availability during a partition.

Interview correction: Do not say “CAP means you can only choose two.” Explain the partition scenario and the consistency/availability trade-off under that failure condition.
🧒 Two friends keep matching notebooks in different cities and normally text each other to stay in sync (consistency) while both notebooks stay usable at all times (availability). If the phone lines go down between the cities (a network partition), each friend must choose: stop writing in their notebook until the phones work again (favor consistency), or keep writing anyway and reconcile any mismatches later (favor availability). You can't have "phones are down AND both notebooks are instantly, perfectly in sync AND both are always writable" all at once.

30.8 Consistency Models

Strong consistencyReads reflect the latest committed state according to the system's consistency model.
Eventual consistencyReplicas converge if updates stop.
Read-after-writeA writer can subsequently observe its own successful update.
Causal consistencyCausally related operations preserve an appropriate ordering.

30.9 Partitioning / Sharding

Large Dataset | ↓ Partition Key | ┌───┼────┬────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ ↓ P0 P1 P2 P3

Partitioning can improve scalability, but the partition key is critical. Poor key selection can create hot partitions and uneven load.

30.10 Choosing a Partition Key

A good key should generally provide:

  • Reasonably even distribution
  • High cardinality
  • Alignment with access patterns
  • Predictable query routing
  • Low likelihood of a single hot key
Classic trap: Choosing a monotonically increasing timestamp as the only partition key can concentrate new writes on a small part of the system.

30.11 Replication

Primary / | \ ↓ ↓ ↓ Replica Replica Replica | ↓ Read Traffic

Replication can improve read scalability and availability, but introduces replication lag and consistency considerations.

30.12 Leader-Based vs Leaderless Replication

Leader-basedLeaderless
Writes commonly coordinate through a leader.Multiple replicas can accept writes depending on design.
Clear write ordering can be easier.Can provide different availability characteristics.
Failover is an important concern.Conflict resolution becomes important.

30.13 Quorum

For a replicated system with N replicas, quorum-style designs often reason about read and write replica counts such that:

R + W > N

This is a conceptual consistency tool, not a universal guarantee. The actual semantics depend on the database and implementation.

30.14 Caching

Client ↓ API ↓ Cache ┌┴───────────┐ Hit Miss ↓ ↓ Response Database ↓ Cache ↓ Response

Common cache strategies:

PatternIdea
Cache-asideApplication reads cache, then database on miss and populates cache.
Read-throughCache abstraction loads missing data.
Write-throughWrites update cache and backing store according to implementation.
Write-behindCache acknowledges before persistence is completed; requires careful durability design.

30.15 Cache Failure Modes

  • Cache stampede
  • Thundering herd
  • Stale data
  • Hot keys
  • Eviction storms
  • Cache poisoning
  • Cold-start latency

Mitigations can include TTL jitter, request coalescing, locks/single-flight, prewarming, rate limiting and appropriate invalidation strategies.

30.16 Distributed Rate Limiting

API Gateway | ↓ Rate Limiter | Redis | ┌──────────┴──────────┐ ↓ ↓ Allowed Rejected ↓ ↓ Application 429

Know Fixed Window, Sliding Window, Token Bucket and Leaky Bucket. For distributed enforcement, state must be coordinated across application instances.

30.17 Messaging Fundamentals

Producer ↓ Topic ↓ Partition ↓ Consumer Group ↓ Consumer

Master:

  • Ordering
  • Partitioning
  • Offsets
  • Consumer lag
  • Retention
  • Replication
  • At-least-once delivery
  • Idempotent consumers
  • Retry topics / DLQs
  • Backpressure

30.18 At-Least-Once Delivery

If an event can be delivered more than once, the consumer must tolerate duplicates.

Event: PAYMENT_COMPLETED
Event ID: EVT-12345

Consumer receives EVT-12345
      ↓
Process
      ↓
Record processed event

Duplicate EVT-12345
      ↓
Already processed
      ↓
Ignore / return safely

30.19 Exactly-Once — Interview Nuance

Do not casually promise “exactly once” across an entire distributed business workflow. Discuss the guarantees provided by the messaging system, transaction boundary and application-level idempotency.

30.20 Backpressure

Producer Rate ↓ 100,000 events/sec Consumer Capacity ↓ 60,000 events/sec Difference ↓ Consumer Lag ↓ Need to scale / throttle / buffer / shed load

Backpressure prevents a fast producer from overwhelming slower consumers.

30.21 Load Balancing

Load Balancer / | \ ↓ ↓ ↓ App 1 App 2 App 3 | | | └───────┴───────┘ Backend

Know round-robin, least connections, weighted routing, health checks, sticky sessions and why stateless services make horizontal scaling easier.

30.22 Stateless vs Stateful Services

StatelessStateful
Request can usually hit any healthy instance.Instance may own important local state.
Easy horizontal scaling.Scaling and failover are more complex.
Session can live in external storage.State needs replication/persistence.

30.23 Database Bottleneck Analysis

Slow API ↓ Trace dependency time ↓ Database is slow ↓ Execution Plan ↓ Index / Join / Scan / Lock ↓ Connection Pool ↓ Query / Schema / Capacity ↓ Fix + Measure

Never assume “add an index” is the universal solution. An unnecessary index increases write cost and storage overhead.

30.24 Distributed Transaction Options

Local transactionBest when the operation can remain within one database boundary.
SagaUseful for long-running business workflows with compensating actions.
OutboxReliable bridge between database state and event publication.
2PCStrong coordination but adds latency, coupling and operational complexity.
Principal rule: Prefer reducing the distributed transaction boundary before reaching for a complex distributed transaction protocol.

30.25 Failure-Oriented Design

For every component, ask:

What if it is slow?Timeout, bulkhead, queueing.
What if it is down?Fallback, retry, failover.
What if it returns duplicates?Idempotency.
What if messages reorder?Versioning/order controls.
What if data is stale?Consistency policy and invalidation.
What if traffic spikes?Autoscaling, backpressure, rate limiting.

30.26 Multi-Region Architecture

Global DNS / Traffic Manager | ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐ ↓ ↓ Region A Region B ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ │ API │ │ API │ │ Services│ │ Services│ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ ↓ ↓ Data ←──── Replication ───→ Data

Discuss active-active vs active-passive, data replication, conflict resolution, RPO, RTO, traffic failover and operational complexity.

30.27 RPO and RTO

TermMeaning
RPOMaximum acceptable amount of data loss measured in time.
RTOMaximum acceptable time to restore service.

Example: RPO 5 minutes means the organization can tolerate losing up to approximately five minutes of recent data under the stated disaster scenario.

30.28 Disaster Recovery Strategies

Backup / RestoreLowest complexity, slower recovery.
Pilot LightMinimal infrastructure kept ready.
Warm StandbyReduced-capacity secondary environment.
Active-ActiveBoth regions serve traffic; highest complexity and cost.

30.29 Security in System Design

Internet ↓ WAF / Edge Protection ↓ API Gateway ↓ Authentication ↓ Authorization ↓ Services ↓ Encrypted Data ↓ Managed Secrets / Key Management

Include:

  • Least privilege
  • Network segmentation
  • TLS in transit
  • Encryption at rest
  • Secrets management
  • Token validation
  • Audit logging
  • PII protection
  • Dependency and container scanning
  • Threat modeling

30.30 Observability Architecture

Applications | ┌──┼───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Metrics Logs Traces ↓ ↓ ↓ Monitoring / OpenTelemetry / APM ↓ Dashboards ↓ Alerts ↓ Incident Response

Think in terms of the four golden signals:

  • Latency
  • Traffic
  • Errors
  • Saturation

30.31 System Design #1 — Banking Payment Platform

React | ↓ API Gateway | Authentication | ↓ Payment Service | ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Account Fraud Ledger Service Service Service | | | ↓ ↓ ↓ PostgreSQL Redis/ML PostgreSQL | ↓ Event Bus | ┌───────────────────┼─────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Notification Analytics Audit Service Pipeline Service | | | ↓ ↓ ↓ Email Warehouse Storage

30.32 Payment Requirements

Functional: validate account, validate funds, fraud decision, debit account, create ledger entry, publish events, notify customer and provide audit history.

Non-functional: high availability, low latency, strong correctness for financial state, no duplicate debit, security, auditability, horizontal scalability and disaster recovery.

30.33 Payment Transaction Boundary

Customer Request ↓ Idempotency Check ↓ Financial Validation ↓ Account + Ledger Transaction ↓ Commit ↓ Outbox Event ↓ Async Downstream Processing ┌──────────┼──────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Fraud* Notification Analytics *Fraud may need to be synchronous depending on business risk policy.

The critical point is to define exactly which checks must complete before accepting the financial transaction and which downstream activities can happen asynchronously.

30.34 Payment Idempotency

POST /payments
Idempotency-Key: 7f4a-payment-001

Store the key with the resulting business outcome. A repeated request with the same key should return the previously established result rather than creating a second debit.

30.35 Payment Failure Scenarios

FailureDesign Response
Client retriesIdempotency key and unique constraint.
Account service slowTimeout + circuit breaker + controlled failure.
Fraud service unavailableExplicit risk policy: fail closed or controlled fallback.
Notification failsQueue/event retry; do not undo a successful payment solely for notification failure.
Event broker unavailableOutbox persists event for later publication.
Consumer receives duplicateIdempotent consumer.
Region failsFailover according to RTO/RPO architecture.

30.36 System Design #2 — Enterprise SaaS Platform

Users ↓ Global Edge / WAF ↓ API Gateway ↓ Tenant-aware Services | ├── Identity ├── Customer ├── Billing ├── Workflow └── Reporting | ├── Relational DB ├── Cache ├── Object Storage └── Event Bus | ↓ Data Platform | ↓ Analytics / AI

Principal-level SaaS topics:

  • Tenant isolation
  • Tenant-aware authorization
  • Data partitioning
  • Noisy-neighbor protection
  • Per-tenant rate limits
  • Configuration isolation
  • Usage metering
  • Billing
  • Audit
  • Tenant-specific data retention

30.37 Multi-Tenancy Models

ModelIsolationOperational Cost
Shared DB / shared schemaLowestLowest
Shared DB / separate schemaMediumMedium
Separate database per tenantHighHigh
HybridDepends on tierFlexible

30.38 System Design #3 — Notification Platform

Producer Services | ↓ Notification API | ↓ Event Bus | ┌────┼─────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Email SMS Push ↓ ↓ ↓ Provider APIs / Delivery Workers | ↓ Delivery Status | ↓ Analytics

Discuss deduplication, user preferences, templates, provider failover, retry policies, rate limits, delivery status and dead-letter handling.

30.39 System Design #4 — Real-Time Fraud Platform

Payment Event ↓ Stream Processor ↓ Feature Lookup ┌───┴────────────┐ ↓ ↓ Redis Feature Store └───────┬────────┘ ↓ ML Model ↓ Fraud Score ↓ Decision Engine ┌─────┴─────┐ ↓ ↓ Approve Review / Reject ↓ Analytics

Discuss model latency, feature freshness, false positives, explainability, model versioning, online/offline feature consistency and safe degradation.

30.40 System Design #5 — Enterprise RAG Platform

User ↓ AI Gateway ↓ Authentication / Authorization ↓ Query Understanding ↓ Hybrid Retrieval ├── Keyword Search └── Vector Search ↓ Reranker ↓ LLM ↓ Guardrails / Policy ↓ Response ↓ Observability / Evaluation

Principal-level concerns:

  • Document-level authorization
  • PII and sensitive-data handling
  • Prompt injection
  • Retrieval quality
  • Hallucination evaluation
  • Latency and token cost
  • Model fallback
  • Prompt/version management
  • Human approval for high-risk actions

30.41 System Design Trade-Off Matrix

ChoiceQuestion to Ask
SQL vs NoSQLDo we need relational integrity, transactions or flexible schema?
REST vs gRPCPublic interoperability or efficient internal RPC?
Sync vs asyncDoes the caller need an immediate result?
Cache vs databaseCan stale data be tolerated?
Microservices vs monolithDo independent boundaries justify distributed complexity?
Kafka vs PulsarWhat ecosystem, storage, multi-tenancy and operational model fits?
Single region vs multi-regionWhat are the RTO/RPO and business cost requirements?

30.42 Principal Interview Questions

  1. Design a payment system handling 10,000 transactions/sec.

30.43 Whiteboard Checklist

RequirementsClarify users, workflows and business rules.
ScaleEstimate traffic, storage and growth.
ArchitectureDraw only the components needed to explain the solution.
DataDefine ownership, schema, consistency and partitioning.
ReliabilityExplain failure, retry, timeout and recovery.
SecurityIdentity, authorization, encryption and secrets.
ObservabilityMetrics, logs, traces and alerts.
Trade-offsState alternatives and why you chose one.

30.44 The Principal Engineer Closing Statement

When presenting a system design, finish by summarizing the architecture, the most important trade-offs, the largest risks and the next validation steps. A Principal Engineer should show that the design is not just technically elegant, but operable, secure, economically sensible and aligned with customer outcomes.

43. SQL Zero-to-Hero — Enterprise Banking Mastery

A complete SQL path from absolute beginner to Principal Engineer level: concepts, queries, joins, subqueries, CTEs, window functions, transactions, indexing, query optimization, security, database design, SQL interview questions and production banking scenarios.

Learning strategy: Learn SQL by asking three questions: What data do I need? How are the tables related? How can I retrieve it correctly and efficiently?
🧒 Kid analogy: A SQL database is a giant, perfectly organized filing cabinet of spreadsheets (tables). A JOIN is stapling two related spreadsheets together by a matching column, like matching each student's name to their locker number.

43.1 What Is SQL?

SQL stands for Structured Query Language. It is used to create, read, modify and control data in relational databases.

Application
    ↓
SQL
    ↓
Database
    ↓
Tables
    ↓
Rows + Columns

Banking Example

customers
accounts
transactions
beneficiaries
loans
employees

43.2 Database Fundamentals

ConceptLayman MeaningBanking Example
DatabaseContainer for related dataBanking database
TableStructured collection of recordsaccounts
RowOne recordOne customer
ColumnOne attributecustomer_name
Primary KeyUnique identitycustomer_id
Foreign KeyRelationship to another tableaccount.customer_id
IndexData structure that speeds lookupindex on account_number

43.3 Create Banking Tables

CREATE TABLE customers (
    customer_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    full_name VARCHAR(150) NOT NULL,
    email VARCHAR(200) UNIQUE,
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
);

CREATE TABLE accounts (
    account_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id BIGINT NOT NULL,
    account_number VARCHAR(30) UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    account_type VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,
    balance DECIMAL(18,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    status VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    FOREIGN KEY (customer_id)
        REFERENCES customers(customer_id)
);

CREATE TABLE transactions (
    transaction_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    account_id BIGINT NOT NULL,
    transaction_type VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    amount DECIMAL(18,2) NOT NULL,
    transaction_date TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    reference_no VARCHAR(100),
    FOREIGN KEY (account_id)
        REFERENCES accounts(account_id)
);

43.4 INSERT

INSERT INTO customers
(customer_id, full_name, email)
VALUES
(1, 'Ravi Kumar', 'ravi@example.com');

INSERT INTO accounts
(account_id, customer_id, account_number,
 account_type, balance, status)
VALUES
(101, 1, 'SB100001',
 'SAVINGS', 50000.00, 'ACTIVE');

43.5 SELECT

SELECT *
FROM accounts;

SELECT account_number, balance
FROM accounts;

SELECT account_number, balance
FROM accounts
WHERE status = 'ACTIVE';

43.6 Filtering

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE balance > 100000;

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE account_type = 'SAVINGS'
  AND balance > 50000;

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE account_type IN ('SAVINGS', 'CURRENT');

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE balance BETWEEN 10000 AND 100000;

43.7 Sorting

SELECT account_number, balance
FROM accounts
ORDER BY balance DESC;

43.8 DISTINCT

SELECT DISTINCT account_type
FROM accounts;

43.9 LIKE

SELECT *
FROM customers
WHERE full_name LIKE 'Ravi%';

43.10 NULL

SELECT *
FROM customers
WHERE email IS NULL;

SELECT *
FROM customers
WHERE email IS NOT NULL;

43.11 Aggregate Functions

SELECT COUNT(*) AS account_count
FROM accounts;

SELECT SUM(balance) AS total_balance
FROM accounts;

SELECT AVG(balance) AS average_balance
FROM accounts;

SELECT MAX(balance) AS highest_balance
FROM accounts;

SELECT MIN(balance) AS lowest_balance
FROM accounts;

43.12 GROUP BY

SELECT account_type,
       COUNT(*) AS total_accounts,
       SUM(balance) AS total_balance
FROM accounts
GROUP BY account_type;

43.13 HAVING

SELECT customer_id,
       COUNT(*) AS account_count
FROM accounts
GROUP BY customer_id
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;

Remember: WHERE filters rows before grouping. HAVING filters groups after aggregation.

43.14 INNER JOIN

SELECT
    c.full_name,
    a.account_number,
    a.balance
FROM customers c
INNER JOIN accounts a
    ON c.customer_id = a.customer_id;

43.15 LEFT JOIN

SELECT
    c.full_name,
    a.account_number
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN accounts a
    ON c.customer_id = a.customer_id;

A LEFT JOIN is useful when you want every customer, even customers who do not currently have an account matching the join condition.

43.16 Multiple Joins

SELECT
    c.full_name,
    a.account_number,
    t.transaction_id,
    t.amount,
    t.transaction_type
FROM customers c
JOIN accounts a
    ON c.customer_id = a.customer_id
JOIN transactions t
    ON a.account_id = t.account_id
WHERE c.customer_id = 1;

43.17 Self Join

CREATE TABLE employees (
    employee_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    employee_name VARCHAR(100),
    manager_id BIGINT
);

SELECT
    e.employee_name AS employee,
    m.employee_name AS manager
FROM employees e
LEFT JOIN employees m
    ON e.manager_id = m.employee_id;

43.18 Subqueries

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE balance >
(
    SELECT AVG(balance)
    FROM accounts
);

43.19 EXISTS

SELECT c.*
FROM customers c
WHERE EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM accounts a
    WHERE a.customer_id = c.customer_id
);

EXISTS is often useful when you only need to know whether a related record exists.

43.20 CASE

SELECT
    account_number,
    balance,
    CASE
        WHEN balance >= 1000000
            THEN 'PREMIUM'
        WHEN balance >= 100000
            THEN 'HIGH'
        ELSE 'STANDARD'
    END AS customer_segment
FROM accounts;

43.21 COALESCE

SELECT
    full_name,
    COALESCE(email, 'NO EMAIL') AS email
FROM customers;

43.22 UNION vs UNION ALL

SELECT customer_id
FROM accounts
WHERE status = 'ACTIVE'

UNION

SELECT customer_id
FROM loans
WHERE status = 'ACTIVE';

UNION removes duplicates. UNION ALL preserves them and is usually cheaper when duplicate removal is unnecessary.

43.23 Common Table Expressions — CTE

WITH customer_balances AS (
    SELECT
        customer_id,
        SUM(balance) AS total_balance
    FROM accounts
    GROUP BY customer_id
)
SELECT *
FROM customer_balances
WHERE total_balance > 500000;

43.24 Recursive CTE

WITH RECURSIVE employee_tree AS (
    SELECT employee_id, employee_name, manager_id
    FROM employees
    WHERE manager_id IS NULL

    UNION ALL

    SELECT e.employee_id,
           e.employee_name,
           e.manager_id
    FROM employees e
    JOIN employee_tree t
      ON e.manager_id = t.employee_id
)
SELECT *
FROM employee_tree;

43.25 Window Functions

Window functions calculate values across related rows without collapsing those rows like GROUP BY does.

SELECT
    account_id,
    transaction_id,
    amount,
    SUM(amount) OVER (
        PARTITION BY account_id
        ORDER BY transaction_date
    ) AS running_total
FROM transactions;

ROW_NUMBER

SELECT *
FROM (
    SELECT
        t.*,
        ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
            PARTITION BY account_id
            ORDER BY transaction_date DESC
        ) AS rn
    FROM transactions t
) x
WHERE rn <= 5;

This returns the five latest transactions for each account.

RANK vs DENSE_RANK

SELECT
    customer_id,
    balance,
    RANK() OVER (
        ORDER BY balance DESC
    ) AS rank_position,
    DENSE_RANK() OVER (
        ORDER BY balance DESC
    ) AS dense_rank_position
FROM customer_balances;

43.26 Transactions

A database transaction groups related operations so they behave as one logical unit.

BEGIN;

UPDATE accounts
SET balance = balance - 5000
WHERE account_id = 101
  AND balance >= 5000;

UPDATE accounts
SET balance = balance + 5000
WHERE account_id = 202;

INSERT INTO transactions
(transaction_id, account_id,
 transaction_type, amount)
VALUES
(90001, 101, 'DEBIT', 5000);

COMMIT;

If a critical step fails, the application may issue ROLLBACK according to the database and transaction design.

43.27 ACID

PropertyMeaningBanking Example
AtomicityAll or nothingDebit and credit succeed together.
ConsistencyRules remain validBalance constraints remain valid.
IsolationConcurrent transactions are controlledTwo transfers do not incorrectly overwrite each other.
DurabilityCommitted data survives failuresCompleted transfer remains recorded.

43.28 Isolation Levels

LevelHigh-Level Meaning
Read UncommittedMay see uncommitted changes.
Read CommittedReads committed data.
Repeatable ReadRepeated reads are more stable within a transaction.
SerializableStrongest isolation; may reduce concurrency.

The exact behavior depends on the database engine. Choose isolation based on business correctness and measured concurrency requirements.

43.29 Optimistic vs Pessimistic Locking

Optimistic

UPDATE accounts
SET balance = 45000,
    version = version + 1
WHERE account_id = 101
  AND version = 7;

If zero rows are updated, another transaction may have changed the record.

Pessimistic

SELECT *
FROM accounts
WHERE account_id = 101
FOR UPDATE;

The database locks the selected row according to the engine's locking semantics.

43.30 Indexes

CREATE INDEX idx_accounts_customer
ON accounts(customer_id);

CREATE INDEX idx_transactions_account_date
ON transactions(account_id, transaction_date);

An index can dramatically improve reads but consumes storage and adds work to writes. Never index every column blindly.

43.31 Composite Index

CREATE INDEX idx_transactions_account_type_date
ON transactions(
    account_id,
    transaction_type,
    transaction_date
);

Column order matters. Design indexes around real query patterns.

43.32 EXPLAIN

EXPLAIN
SELECT *
FROM transactions
WHERE account_id = 101
ORDER BY transaction_date DESC;

Use the database's execution-plan tools to understand scans, index usage, joins, row estimates and expensive operations.

43.33 SQL Performance Optimization

  1. Measure before optimizing.
  2. Inspect the execution plan.
  3. Verify indexes.
  4. Return only required columns.
  5. Filter early where appropriate.
  6. Avoid unnecessary joins.
  7. Use pagination.
  8. Watch for N+1 query patterns in application code.
  9. Review cardinality and statistics.
  10. Consider partitioning for very large datasets.
  11. Use caching carefully.
  12. Benchmark before and after.

43.34 Keyset Pagination

For very large transaction tables, keyset pagination can be more efficient than very deep OFFSET pagination.

SELECT
    transaction_id,
    amount,
    transaction_date
FROM transactions
WHERE account_id = 101
  AND transaction_id < 900500
ORDER BY transaction_id DESC
LIMIT 50;

43.35 OFFSET Pagination

SELECT *
FROM transactions
WHERE account_id = 101
ORDER BY transaction_date DESC
LIMIT 50 OFFSET 50000;

Deep offsets can become expensive because the database may still need to process many preceding rows.

43.36 Normalization

Normalization reduces unnecessary duplication and update anomalies.

Customer ↓ Account ↓ Transaction Instead of: Customer + Account + Transaction repeated in every row

Common Normal Forms

  • 1NF — atomic values.
  • 2NF — remove partial dependency on composite keys.
  • 3NF — remove inappropriate transitive dependencies.

43.37 Denormalization

Denormalization intentionally duplicates or precomputes data to improve read performance. Use it deliberately and define how consistency will be maintained.

43.38 Database Partitioning

transactions_2026_01
transactions_2026_02
transactions_2026_03
...

Large transaction datasets can sometimes benefit from partitioning by date or another appropriate business dimension, depending on database capabilities and query patterns.

43.39 SQL Security

Never Build SQL Like This

sql = "SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = '" + email + "'"

Use Parameterized Queries

sql = """
SELECT *
FROM users
WHERE email = ?
"""

cursor.execute(sql, (email,))

Parameterized queries help prevent SQL injection. Also use least-privilege database users, secret management, encryption and auditing.

43.40 SQL Injection

Interview answer: SQL injection occurs when untrusted input changes the meaning of a SQL statement. Prevent it primarily with parameterized queries/prepared statements, safe ORM/query APIs, input validation where appropriate and least-privilege database accounts.

43.41 SQL + Java Spring Boot Example

@Query("""
    SELECT a
    FROM Account a
    WHERE a.customerId = :customerId
      AND a.status = 'ACTIVE'
""")
List<Account> findActiveAccounts(
    @Param("customerId")
    Long customerId
);

43.42 SQL + Python Example

import psycopg

with psycopg.connect(DB_URL) as conn:
    with conn.cursor() as cur:
        cur.execute(
            """
            SELECT account_number, balance
            FROM accounts
            WHERE customer_id = %s
            """,
            (customer_id,)
        )

        rows = cur.fetchall()

43.43 SQL for Fraud Detection

SELECT
    account_id,
    COUNT(*) AS transaction_count,
    SUM(amount) AS total_amount
FROM transactions
WHERE transaction_date >=
      CURRENT_TIMESTAMP - INTERVAL '1 hour'
GROUP BY account_id
HAVING COUNT(*) >= 10
    OR SUM(amount) >= 100000;

This is a screening query, not a complete fraud model. Real fraud systems combine rules, historical behavior, streaming data and ML.

43.44 Find Duplicate Transactions

SELECT
    account_id,
    amount,
    reference_no,
    COUNT(*) AS duplicate_count
FROM transactions
GROUP BY
    account_id,
    amount,
    reference_no
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;

43.45 Find the Second-Highest Balance

SELECT MAX(balance) AS second_highest
FROM accounts
WHERE balance < (
    SELECT MAX(balance)
    FROM accounts
);

43.46 Top 3 Customers by Balance

SELECT *
FROM (
    SELECT
        customer_id,
        SUM(balance) AS total_balance,
        DENSE_RANK() OVER (
            ORDER BY SUM(balance) DESC
        ) AS rnk
    FROM accounts
    GROUP BY customer_id
) x
WHERE rnk <= 3;

43.47 Customers Without Transactions

SELECT c.customer_id, c.full_name
FROM customers c
JOIN accounts a
  ON a.customer_id = c.customer_id
LEFT JOIN transactions t
  ON t.account_id = a.account_id
WHERE t.transaction_id IS NULL;

43.48 Monthly Transaction Summary

SELECT
    DATE_TRUNC('month', transaction_date)
        AS month,
    transaction_type,
    COUNT(*) AS transaction_count,
    SUM(amount) AS total_amount
FROM transactions
GROUP BY
    DATE_TRUNC('month', transaction_date),
    transaction_type
ORDER BY month;

43.49 Running Account Balance

SELECT
    account_id,
    transaction_date,
    transaction_type,
    amount,
    SUM(
        CASE
            WHEN transaction_type = 'CREDIT'
                THEN amount
            ELSE -amount
        END
    ) OVER (
        PARTITION BY account_id
        ORDER BY transaction_date, transaction_id
    ) AS running_balance
FROM transactions;

43.50 SQL Interview Questions + Answers

Q1. DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP?

Answer: DELETE removes rows and can be filtered. TRUNCATE removes all rows using database-specific semantics and is generally intended for quickly clearing a table. DROP removes the table definition and its data. Transaction and recovery behavior varies by database.

Q2. WHERE vs HAVING?

Answer: WHERE filters individual rows before grouping. HAVING filters grouped results after aggregation.

Q3. INNER JOIN vs LEFT JOIN?

Answer: INNER JOIN returns matching rows from both sides. LEFT JOIN returns all rows from the left side and matching rows from the right side, with NULLs when no match exists.

Q4. Primary key vs unique key?

Answer: A primary key identifies the main identity of a row and is the table's primary entity key. A UNIQUE constraint enforces uniqueness for another candidate/business key. Exact NULL behavior depends on the database.

Q5. What is an index?

Answer: An index is an auxiliary data structure that can make searches and ordering faster. It costs storage and adds write overhead, so indexes should be designed from actual access patterns.

Q6. What is a composite index?

Answer: An index over multiple columns. Column order matters because it determines which query predicates and ordering patterns can efficiently use the index.

Q7. What is a covering index?

Answer: An index that contains enough information for a query to be answered without fetching additional table data, depending on the database engine and execution plan.

Q8. What is normalization?

Answer: A set of design principles that reduce unnecessary duplication and anomalies by structuring relational data appropriately.

Q9. What is denormalization?

Answer: Deliberate duplication or precomputation to improve read performance. It introduces consistency-management responsibilities.

Q10. What is a deadlock?

Answer: Two or more transactions wait for resources held by each other, so none can proceed. Databases usually detect and abort one transaction. Applications should use consistent lock ordering, short transactions and safe retry strategies.

Q11. How do you troubleshoot a slow query?

Answer: Reproduce and measure it, inspect the execution plan, check indexes/statistics, cardinality, joins, sorting, filtering, row counts and lock waits. Then change one thing, benchmark again and verify the improvement.

Q12. What is an N+1 query problem?

Answer: The application runs one query to retrieve parent records and then another query for each parent. One request can therefore generate hundreds or thousands of database calls. Fix it with appropriate joins, batch fetching, projections or carefully designed queries.

Q13. What is a transaction?

Answer: A logical unit of database work governed by transactional guarantees such as atomicity and consistency. In banking, transferring money often requires coordinated updates that must not partially succeed.

Q14. Optimistic vs pessimistic locking?

Answer: Optimistic locking assumes conflicts are uncommon and detects changes using a version/timestamp. Pessimistic locking explicitly locks data while the transaction operates. The correct choice depends on contention and business correctness.

Q15. How do you design a transaction table for billions of rows?

Answer: Start with workload analysis. Use appropriate keys and indexes, partition where supported and justified, archive/retain data according to policy, use efficient pagination, avoid unbounded queries, separate OLTP from analytical workloads when necessary, and measure query plans and storage growth continuously.

Q16. SQL database vs NoSQL?

Answer: SQL databases provide strong relational modeling and mature transactional capabilities. NoSQL systems can be advantageous for particular scale, access-pattern or schema-flexibility requirements. I choose based on consistency, query patterns, scale, operational requirements and business constraints—not fashion.

Q17. How do you prevent SQL injection?

Answer: Use parameterized queries/prepared statements, safe ORM/query APIs, least-privilege accounts and secure secret management. Never concatenate untrusted user input into SQL.

Q18. What is a window function?

Answer: A window function calculates a value across related rows while retaining individual rows. It is useful for rankings, running totals, latest-N records and comparisons with previous/next records.

Q19. CTE vs subquery?

Answer: Both can express intermediate query logic. CTEs often improve readability and can support recursive queries. Performance depends on the database engine and query; choose based on clarity and measured execution behavior.

Q20. How would you optimize a banking transaction API?

Answer: Keep transactions short, use proper indexes, retrieve only required columns, prevent N+1 queries, use connection pooling, inspect execution plans, use idempotency for retried writes, choose appropriate isolation/locking, monitor lock contention and latency, and separate analytical workloads from the OLTP path when necessary.

43.51 Principal Engineer SQL Design Questions

Q. Design a database for a banking system.

Model answer: I would begin with bounded business capabilities: Customer, Account, Transaction, Beneficiary, Payment, Loan and Audit. Define ownership and lifecycle for each entity, establish keys and constraints, model transactional invariants, identify high-volume access patterns, then design indexes and partitioning from measured workloads. I would keep the OLTP model normalized where consistency matters and introduce carefully controlled read models/materialized views for reporting. Security, encryption, auditing, retention and disaster recovery are architectural requirements, not afterthoughts.

Q. How would you handle account balance consistency?

Model answer: Treat balance-changing operations as transactional business operations. Use appropriate locking/versioning, enforce database constraints, make transfer requests idempotent and ensure debit/credit invariants. For distributed transfers across services, I would avoid pretending a single database transaction exists and instead design explicit workflow/state, durable events, reconciliation and compensating behavior where appropriate.

Q. How would you scale transaction reads?

Model answer: First identify the read workload. Use appropriate indexes and keyset pagination, read replicas where consistency permits, caching for safe repeated reads, partitioning for very large datasets and dedicated analytical stores for heavy reporting. I would preserve the transactional database as the source of truth and avoid using it as an unrestricted analytics engine.

43.52 SQL Capstone Project

Project: Build the SQL data layer for the Digital Banking platform.

Customer ↓ Accounts ↓ Transactions ↓ Beneficiaries ↓ Transfers ↓ Audit Events ↓ Reporting / Analytics

Capstone Requirements

  • Design normalized banking tables.
  • Create primary and foreign keys.
  • Add business constraints.
  • Create useful indexes.
  • Implement customer/account queries.
  • Implement transaction history.
  • Implement latest-N transactions.
  • Implement monthly reporting.
  • Implement balance calculations.
  • Implement duplicate detection.
  • Implement fraud-screening queries.
  • Implement transfer transaction handling.
  • Demonstrate optimistic/pessimistic locking.
  • Analyze slow queries using execution plans.
  • Write security-safe parameterized queries.

Capstone Interview Exercise

Given a transaction table containing 2 billion rows, explain how you would return the latest 50 transactions for one customer in under the required latency target.

Expected direction: Do not start by adding random indexes. Establish the query, cardinality and execution plan; model the access path; use appropriate composite indexes and/or partitioning; use keyset pagination; retrieve only required columns; validate with production-like data and measure p95/p99 latency.

Principal Engineer takeaway: SQL mastery is not memorizing syntax. It is understanding data modeling, correctness, concurrency, query plans, indexes, scale, security and the business consequences of every database decision.

44. NoSQL + MongoDB Zero-to-Hero — Enterprise Banking Mastery

Learn NoSQL and MongoDB from beginner to Principal Engineer level using the same Digital Banking case study: documents, collections, CRUD, schema design, embedding vs referencing, indexes, aggregation, transactions, replication, sharding, consistency, performance, security, Spring Boot/Python integration and interview Q&A.

Layman explanation: A relational database usually organizes information into tables and relationships. MongoDB stores information as flexible JSON-like documents. The right choice depends on access patterns, consistency requirements, scale and business needs.
🧒 Kid analogy: If SQL is a filing cabinet of identical index cards, MongoDB is a box of sticky notes — each note (document) can have its own shape: some have extra fields, some don't, and you can freely stuff related information into one note instead of stapling multiple cards together.

44.1 SQL vs NoSQL

SQLMongoDB / Document NoSQL
TablesCollections
RowsDocuments
ColumnsDocument fields
JOIN-centric relationshipsOften model around access patterns
Schema usually strongly structuredFlexible document schema
Excellent transactional/relational workloadsExcellent for appropriate document-oriented workloads

44.2 MongoDB Mental Model

MongoDB Database ↓ Collections ↓ Documents ↓ Fields ↓ Arrays / Nested Objects

44.3 Install / Connect

# Python
pip install pymongo

# Java
# Add the MongoDB Spring Data dependency
# to the Spring Boot project.
from pymongo import MongoClient

client = MongoClient(
    "mongodb://localhost:27017"
)

db = client["digital_banking"]

customers = db["customers"]
accounts = db["accounts"]

44.4 Create a Customer Document

{
  "_id": "C1001",
  "name": "Ravi Kumar",
  "email": "ravi@example.com",
  "phone": "+91XXXXXXXXXX",
  "status": "ACTIVE",
  "createdAt": "2026-08-24T10:00:00Z"
}

A MongoDB document can naturally contain nested objects and arrays.

44.5 Insert Documents

customers.insert_one({
    "_id": "C1001",
    "name": "Ravi Kumar",
    "email": "ravi@example.com",
    "status": "ACTIVE"
})

customers.insert_many([
    {
        "_id": "C1002",
        "name": "Alisha",
        "status": "ACTIVE"
    },
    {
        "_id": "C1003",
        "name": "Priya",
        "status": "ACTIVE"
    }
])

44.6 Find Documents

customers.find_one({
    "_id": "C1001"
})

customers.find({
    "status": "ACTIVE"
})

customers.find({
    "name": {
        "$regex": "^Ravi",
        "$options": "i"
    }
})

44.7 Projection

customers.find(
    {"status": "ACTIVE"},
    {
        "_id": 1,
        "name": 1,
        "email": 1
    }
)

Return only fields the application actually needs.

44.8 Update

customers.update_one(
    {"_id": "C1001"},
    {
        "$set": {
            "email": "new@example.com"
        }
    }
)

44.9 Update with $inc

accounts.update_one(
    {"_id": "A1001"},
    {
        "$inc": {
            "transactionCount": 1
        }
    }
)

44.10 Upsert

customers.update_one(
    {"_id": "C2000"},
    {
        "$set": {
            "name": "New Customer",
            "status": "ACTIVE"
        }
    },
    upsert=True
)

44.11 Delete

customers.delete_one({
    "_id": "C1003"
})

In enterprise banking, prefer business-state transitions such as status = CLOSED or retention workflows where audit/history requirements make physical deletion inappropriate.

44.12 Embedded Documents

{
  "_id": "C1001",
  "name": "Ravi Kumar",
  "address": {
    "city": "Bhubaneswar",
    "state": "Odisha",
    "country": "India"
  }
}

Embedding is useful when related information is normally read together and has a manageable lifecycle.

44.13 Arrays

{
  "_id": "C1001",
  "beneficiaries": [
    {
      "id": "B1",
      "name": "Home",
      "account": "XXXX1234"
    },
    {
      "id": "B2",
      "name": "Office",
      "account": "XXXX5678"
    }
  ]
}

44.14 Embedding vs Referencing

EmbedReference
Data read togetherData independently accessed
Small/controlled sizeLarge or growing related data
Single-document updateSeparate lifecycle
Fast read of complete aggregateUseful when duplication becomes expensive

Banking Rule of Thumb

Do not embed an unbounded transaction history inside a customer document. A customer's transactions can grow indefinitely. Keep large transaction streams as separate documents/collections and model queries around actual access patterns.

44.15 Account Document

{
  "_id": "A1001",
  "customerId": "C1001",
  "accountNumber": "SB100001",
  "type": "SAVINGS",
  "currency": "INR",
  "status": "ACTIVE",
  "balance": 50000.00,
  "limits": {
    "dailyTransfer": 100000
  }
}

  "merchant": {
    "name": "Online Store",

transactions.find({
    "merchant.category": "ECOMMERCE"
})

44.18 Query Arrays

customers.find({
    "beneficiaries": {
        "$elemMatch": {
            "name": "Home"
        }
    }
})

44.19 Indexes

accounts.create_index(
    [("customerId", 1)]
)

transactions.create_index(
    [
        ("accountId", 1),
        ("timestamp", -1)
    ]
)

For the query “latest transactions for an account,” the compound index on accountId and descending timestamp is a common access-pattern-driven design.

44.20 Unique Index

accounts.create_index(
    [("accountNumber", 1)],
    unique=True
)

44.21 Explain a MongoDB Query

transactions.find({
    "accountId": "A1001"
}).sort(
    "timestamp", -1
).limit(50).explain()

Use execution statistics to determine whether indexes are being used effectively and whether the query examines far more documents than it returns.

44.22 Aggregation Pipeline

pipeline = [
    {
        "$match": {
            "accountId": "A1001"
        }
    },
    {
        "$group": {
            "_id": "$type",
            "total": {
                "$sum": "$amount"
            },
            "count": {
                "$sum": 1
            }
        }
    }
]

result = transactions.aggregate(pipeline)

44.23 Monthly Banking Analytics

pipeline = [
    {
        "$match": {
            "accountId": "A1001"
        }
    },
    {
        "$group": {
            "_id": {
                "$dateToString": {
                    "format": "%Y-%m",
                    "date": "$timestamp"
                }
            },
            "totalAmount": {
                "$sum": "$amount"
            },
            "count": {
                "$sum": 1
            }
        }
    },
    {
        "$sort": {
            "_id": 1
        }
    }
]

44.24 Aggregation Stages

  • $match — filter documents.
  • $project — shape fields.
  • $group — aggregate.
  • $sort — order results.
  • $limit — restrict output.
  • $unwind — expand arrays.
  • $lookup — perform a join-like operation.
  • $facet — run multiple pipeline branches.

44.25 MongoDB $lookup

pipeline = [
    {
        "$lookup": {
            "from": "customers",
            "localField": "customerId",
            "foreignField": "_id",
            "as": "customer"
        }
    }
]

$lookup exists, but document modeling should not automatically reproduce a relational schema. First consider whether embedding or a different read model better matches the application's access pattern.

44.26 Transactions

with client.start_session() as session:
    with session.start_transaction():

        accounts.update_one(
            {
                "_id": "A1001",
                "balance": {"$gte": 5000}
            },
            {
                "$inc": {
                    "balance": -5000
                }
            },
            session=session
        )

        accounts.update_one(
            {"_id": "A2001"},
            {
                "$inc": {
                    "balance": 5000
                }
            },
            session=session
        )

MongoDB supports multi-document transactions, but transaction use should be deliberate. Good document modeling can often reduce the need for distributed transactional updates.

44.27 Atomic Single-Document Updates

accounts.update_one(
    {
        "_id": "A1001",
        "balance": {"$gte": 5000}
    },
    {
        "$inc": {
            "balance": -5000
        }
    }
)

Single-document atomic updates are a powerful MongoDB design primitive. Always verify the matched/modified result and build application-level idempotency for retried business operations.

44.28 Idempotent Banking Transactions

transactions.update_one(
    {
        "idempotencyKey": "REQ-123"
    },
    {
        "$setOnInsert": {
            "accountId": "A1001",
            "amount": 5000,
            "status": "COMPLETED"
        }
    },
    upsert=True
)

Create an appropriate unique index on the idempotency key so concurrent requests cannot accidentally create duplicate business operations.

44.29 Replication

A MongoDB replica set maintains multiple copies of data to improve availability and provide failover.

Application ↓ MongoDB Replica Set ├── Primary ├── Secondary └── Secondary

44.30 Read Preference

Applications can choose read preferences according to consistency and latency requirements. Financial correctness-sensitive operations generally require careful consistency design rather than blindly reading from secondary nodes.

44.31 Write Concern

Write concern controls how much acknowledgement the application requires for a write. Choose it based on durability and availability requirements rather than using the fastest setting by default.

44.32 Sharding

Application ↓ MongoDB Router ↓ Shard Key ├── Shard 1 ├── Shard 2 └── Shard 3

Sharding distributes data across nodes. The shard key is a major architectural decision because poor distribution can create hotspots and inefficient queries.

44.33 Choosing a Shard Key

  • Consider cardinality.
  • Consider query patterns.
  • Avoid predictable monotonically increasing keys when they create hotspots for the chosen architecture.
  • Consider distribution of writes.
  • Consider whether the most important queries can target the appropriate shard(s).
  • Test with production-like scale.

44.34 MongoDB Performance Checklist

  1. Design documents around access patterns.
  2. Keep documents within practical size limits.
  3. Index actual query patterns.
  4. Use compound indexes deliberately.
  5. Use projections.
  6. Limit result sets.
  7. Inspect execution plans.
  8. Avoid unbounded arrays.
  9. Avoid expensive aggregation pipelines without measurement.
  10. Use connection pooling.
  11. Monitor slow queries.
  12. Monitor memory, CPU, storage and replication lag.

44.35 MongoDB Security

  • Enable authentication.
  • Use TLS for network communication.
  • Apply least-privilege roles.
  • Never expose MongoDB directly to the public internet.
  • Protect credentials using a secret manager.
  • Audit sensitive operations.
  • Use encryption features appropriate to regulatory requirements.
  • Validate input and control query construction.
  • Apply network-level access controls.

44.36 Spring Boot + MongoDB

@Document(collection = "accounts")
public class Account {

    @Id
    private String id;

    private String customerId;
    private String accountNumber;
    private BigDecimal balance;
    private String status;
}

Repository

public interface AccountRepository
        extends MongoRepository<Account, String> {

    List<Account> findByCustomerId(
        String customerId
    );

    Optional<Account>
        findByAccountNumber(
            String accountNumber
        );
}

MongoTemplate

Query query = new Query(
    Criteria.where("customerId")
            .is(customerId)
);

query.fields()
     .include("accountNumber")
     .include("balance");

List<Account> accounts =
    mongoTemplate.find(
        query,
        Account.class
    );

44.37 Python + MongoDB

from pymongo import MongoClient

client = MongoClient(MONGO_URI)

db = client["digital_banking"]

cursor = db.transactions.find(
    {"accountId": "A1001"},
    {
        "_id": 1,
        "amount": 1,
        "type": 1,
        "timestamp": 1
    }
).sort(
    "timestamp", -1
).limit(50)

for transaction in cursor:
    print(transaction)

44.38 MongoDB in a Microservices Architecture

Customer Service ↓ Customer MongoDB Account Service ↓ Account MongoDB Transaction Service ↓ Transaction MongoDB Reporting / Analytics ↓ Data Platform

A common microservices principle is that a service owns its data. Do not create a shared MongoDB collection that every microservice freely modifies.

44.39 MongoDB + Event-Driven Architecture

Transfer Service ↓ MongoDB ↓ Outbox / Change Stream Pattern ↓ Event Bus ↓ Fraud / Notification / Analytics

For reliable event publishing, design explicitly for the consistency gap between database writes and message publication. An outbox-style architecture can be useful.

44.40 MongoDB Change Streams

with db.transactions.watch() as stream:
    for change in stream:
        print(change)

Change streams can support reactive workflows such as notifications, downstream processing and operational integrations. They are not a replacement for a durable enterprise event architecture in every use case.

44.41 MongoDB + RAG / GenAI

MongoDB can also participate in AI applications as a document store and, depending on the deployed MongoDB capabilities, as part of vector-search/RAG architectures.

Bank Documents ↓ MongoDB ├── Document Data ├── Metadata └── Vector / Search Capability ↓ Retriever ↓ LLM ↓ Grounded Banking Answer

Use ACL metadata with retrieval so the AI application never retrieves documents the caller is not authorized to see.

44.42 SQL vs MongoDB — Principal Decision

RequirementLikely Direction
Strong relational integrity and complex relational reportingSQL often fits naturally
Flexible document-shaped aggregateMongoDB can fit well
Highly connected relational queriesSQL often simpler
Large evolving document structuresMongoDB may fit well
Financial transaction correctnessEvaluate transactional model carefully; do not choose by hype
High-volume event/document workloadsEvaluate MongoDB and other specialized stores against actual access patterns

44.43 MongoDB Interview Questions + Answers

Q1. What is MongoDB?

Answer: MongoDB is a document-oriented NoSQL database that stores BSON documents in collections. It is designed for flexible document modeling, horizontal scaling and application access patterns that fit document storage.

Q2. Collection vs table?

Answer: A collection is broadly analogous to a relational table, but MongoDB documents can have flexible structures and nested fields.

Q3. Document vs row?

Answer: A document is broadly analogous to a row but can contain nested objects and arrays and does not require every document to have exactly the same field structure.

Q4. Embedding vs referencing?

Answer: Embed data when it belongs to the same aggregate, is bounded and commonly read together. Reference data when it has an independent lifecycle, grows significantly or is shared across many aggregates.

Q5. Does MongoDB support transactions?

Answer: Yes. MongoDB supports transactions including multi-document transactions. However, good document modeling and atomic single-document operations should be preferred when they satisfy the business requirement.

Q6. What is a replica set?

Answer: A group of MongoDB instances maintaining replicated data for availability and failover.

Q7. What is sharding?

Answer: Sharding distributes data across multiple servers using a shard key. The key must be chosen carefully to distribute load and support important query patterns.

Q8. What makes a good index?

Answer: An index should support an important query pattern, reduce the amount of data examined and provide an acceptable write/storage trade-off. Verify with execution plans and real workloads.

Q9. What is a compound index?

Answer: An index over multiple fields. Field ordering affects which predicates and sorting operations can efficiently use it.

Q10. What is an aggregation pipeline?

Answer: A sequence of stages that transforms and analyzes documents, such as filtering, grouping, sorting, projecting and joining-like lookups.

Q11. What is MongoDB's document size consideration?

Answer: MongoDB has a maximum BSON document size. More importantly, even before reaching the technical maximum, unbounded document growth can create performance and operational problems. Large transaction histories should generally not be embedded indefinitely in one customer document.

Q12. How do you prevent duplicate transfers?

Answer: Use an application-level idempotency key, enforce uniqueness with an appropriate index and make the operation safely retryable. Do not depend only on the LLM or UI to avoid duplicates.

Q13. MongoDB vs Redis?

Answer: MongoDB is a persistent document database. Redis is primarily an in-memory data platform commonly used for caching, counters, sessions and other low-latency workloads. They solve different problems and can be complementary.

Q14. MongoDB vs Cassandra?

Answer: Both are NoSQL systems but have different data models and operational/query trade-offs. Cassandra is strongly oriented around predictable distributed access patterns and high write availability, while MongoDB provides a rich document model and query/aggregation capabilities.

Q15. How would you troubleshoot slow MongoDB queries?

Answer: Inspect the query shape, execution statistics, indexes, documents examined versus returned, sorting, working-set size, server resource usage and lock/replication behavior. Then test an index or query/model change using production-like data.

Q16. How do you design MongoDB for banking?

Answer: Start from business aggregates and access patterns. Keep account and transaction data appropriately bounded, enforce uniqueness and authorization, use atomic updates/transactions where required, design idempotency, indexes and retention, replicate for availability and separate analytical workloads where appropriate.

Q17. Can MongoDB replace SQL everywhere?

Answer: No. Database selection is a workload decision. SQL remains excellent for strongly relational data and complex transactional/reporting workloads. MongoDB is powerful where document modeling and its operational/query characteristics fit the business requirements.

44.44 Principal Engineer MongoDB Design Exercise

Question: Design a MongoDB architecture for 100 million customers and billions of banking transactions.

Model answer: I would first define bounded aggregates and workload patterns rather than placing everything into one document. Customer/profile data would be separate from the unbounded transaction stream. I would choose keys and compound indexes from real queries, use replica sets for availability, evaluate sharding based on transaction distribution and query routing, implement retention/archival, enforce idempotency and uniqueness, and separate analytical workloads from transactional traffic. I would load-test with production-like cardinality before committing to the shard key and capacity model.

  • Customer document model.
  • Account document model.
  • Transaction document model.
  • Beneficiary model.

  • Latest transaction API.
  • Aggregation reporting.
  • Atomic balance update.

  • Python integration.
  • MongoDB security checklist.
  • MongoDB + event-driven integration.
Principal Engineer takeaway: MongoDB mastery is not memorizing commands. It is knowing when document modeling is appropriate, how to design around access patterns, how to guarantee business correctness, and how to scale, secure and operate the database in production.

45. Apache Kafka Zero-to-Hero — Spring Boot Event-Driven Architecture

Practical Kafka learning path from beginner to Principal Engineer: brokers, topics, partitions, offsets, producers, consumers, consumer groups, replication, ISR, retention, ordering, delivery semantics, retries, DLQ, idempotency, transactions, Schema Registry concepts, Kafka Streams, Spring Boot integration, observability, architecture patterns and interview Q&A.

Layman explanation: Kafka is like a highly scalable digital railway. Producers put events onto named tracks called topics. Consumers read those events independently. Kafka keeps events for a configured period so consumers can process them at their own speed.
🧒 Kid analogy: Kafka is like a row of numbered mailboxes on a street (topics split into partitions). Mail carriers (producers) drop letters into a mailbox. Neighbors (consumers) walk by and read the letters in order, at their own pace — and the letters stay in the mailbox for a while even after being read, so a second neighbor can also come read them later.

45.1 Kafka Mental Model

Banking Service ↓ Producer ↓ Kafka Topic ┌────┴─────┐ Partition 0 Partition 1 Partition 2 └────┬─────┘ ↓ Consumer Group ├── Consumer A ├── Consumer B └── Consumer C

45.2 Core Kafka Terminology

TermLayman MeaningBanking Example
BrokerKafka serverOne Kafka node
ClusterGroup of brokersProduction Kafka cluster
TopicNamed stream/category of eventstransaction-events
PartitionOrdered log within a topicPartition for transaction events
OffsetPosition of a messageEvent number 10293
ProducerPublishes eventsTransfer service
ConsumerReads eventsFraud service
Consumer GroupConsumers sharing workFraud-service instances
ReplicationCopies partitions across brokersAvailability
ISRIn-sync replicasHealthy replicas

45.3 Event-Driven Banking Architecture

Customer ↓ API Gateway ↓ Transfer Service ↓ Transaction DB ↓ Outbox ↓ Kafka ├── Fraud Service ├── Notification Service ├── Audit Service ├── Analytics └── Customer Activity Service

The key architectural idea is that consumers are decoupled from the producer. The transfer service does not need to synchronously call every downstream system.

45.4 Event vs Command

EventCommand
Something happenedRequest to perform something
Past tenseUsually imperative
transaction.completedcreate.transfer
Multiple consumers can reactUsually directed at a responsible service

45.5 Create Topic

kafka-topics.sh \
  --bootstrap-server localhost:9092 \
  --create \
  --topic transaction-events \
  --partitions 3 \
  --replication-factor 3

45.6 Spring Boot Dependencies

<dependency>
    <groupId>
        org.springframework.kafka
    </groupId>
    <artifactId>
        spring-kafka
    </artifactId>
</dependency>

45.7 Spring Boot Producer

@Service
public class TransactionEventPublisher {

    private final KafkaTemplate<String, TransactionEvent>
        kafkaTemplate;

    public void publish(TransactionEvent event) {

        kafkaTemplate.send(
            "transaction-events",
            event.accountId(),
            event
        );
    }
}

45.8 Kafka Producer Configuration

spring:
  kafka:
    bootstrap-servers:
      localhost:9092

    producer:
      key-serializer:
        org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer

      value-serializer:
        org.springframework.kafka.support.serializer.JsonSerializer

      acks: all
      retries: 5
      enable-idempotence: true

Important: Configuration names and defaults can vary with Kafka/Spring versions. Verify the settings for the exact production version.

45.9 Spring Boot Consumer

@KafkaListener(
    topics = "transaction-events",
    groupId = "fraud-service"
)
public void consume(
    TransactionEvent event
) {

    fraudService.analyze(event);
}

45.10 Consumer Groups

🧒 A consumer group is a team of friends splitting up a stack of numbered raffle tickets (partitions) so each ticket only gets read by ONE friend on the team — no double-reading within the team. But a totally different team (a different consumer group) can grab their own full copy of all the tickets and read every single one independently.
Topic: transaction-events Partitions: P0 → Consumer A P1 → Consumer B P2 → Consumer C Same group = work shared Different groups: fraud-group notification-group analytics-group Each group receives the event independently.

45.11 Ordering

Kafka ordering is guaranteed within a partition, not globally across all partitions.

kafkaTemplate.send(
    "transaction-events",
    accountId,     // stable key
    event
);

Using a stable key can route events for the same entity to the same partition, helping preserve per-key ordering.

45.12 Partitions and Scalability

Partitions enable parallel processing. More partitions can increase consumer parallelism, but partition count is an architectural decision with operational consequences.

45.13 Offset

An offset identifies a record's position in a partition. Consumer groups track offsets so consumers can resume processing.

45.14 Delivery Semantics

SemanticMeaning
At-most-onceMay lose messages but avoids duplicate processing.
At-least-onceMessages are retried, so duplicates are possible.
Exactly-onceRequires careful end-to-end design; do not treat a single Kafka setting as proof of business-level exactly-once behavior.

45.15 Idempotent Consumer

if (processedEventRepository
        .exists(event.eventId())) {

    return;
}

process(event);

processedEventRepository
    .save(event.eventId());

For production, make the deduplication mechanism itself concurrency-safe and durable.

45.16 Retry

@RetryableTopic(
    attempts = "4",
    backoff = @Backoff(
        delay = 1000,
        multiplier = 2
    )
)
@KafkaListener(
    topics = "transaction-events",
    groupId = "fraud-service"
)
public void consume(
    TransactionEvent event
) {
    fraudService.analyze(event);
}

45.17 Dead Letter Topic

Kafka ↓ Consumer ↓ Failure ↓ Retry ↓ Retry ↓ Retry ↓ DLT ↓ Operations / Investigation

45.18 Outbox Pattern

A common problem is: the database transaction succeeds but publishing the Kafka event fails. The outbox pattern writes the business change and an event record in the same database transaction, then publishes the outbox event asynchronously.

BEGIN;

UPDATE account
SET balance = balance - 5000
WHERE account_id = 101;

INSERT INTO outbox_event
(event_id, event_type, payload)
VALUES
('EV123',
 'TRANSFER_COMPLETED',
 '{...}');

COMMIT;
DB Transaction ├── Business Data └── Outbox Event ↓ Outbox Publisher ↓ Kafka

45.19 Kafka Transactions

Kafka transactions can provide atomicity across certain Kafka read/process/write patterns. They do not magically make a database update and Kafka publish one distributed transaction.

45.20 Schema Evolution

Events are contracts. Avoid breaking consumers by evolving schemas compatibly.

{
  "eventId": "EV1001",
  "eventType": "TRANSFER_COMPLETED",
  "version": 2,
  "accountId": "A1001",
  "amount": 5000,
  "currency": "INR"
}

For enterprise systems, teams often use Avro, Protobuf or JSON Schema with a schema registry and explicit compatibility policies.

45.21 Kafka Streams Concept

Kafka Topic ↓ Stream Processing ├── Filter ├── Map ├── Join ├── Aggregate └── Window ↓ Output Topic

Kafka Streams is useful when stream processing belongs close to the Kafka ecosystem, such as transaction aggregation or event-driven stateful processing.

45.22 Kafka Security

  • TLS for encryption in transit.
  • SASL/authentication as appropriate.
  • ACLs for topic access.
  • Separate producer and consumer privileges.
  • Do not allow every service to read/write every topic.
  • Protect credentials using a secret manager.
  • Audit administrative and sensitive operations.

45.23 Kafka Production Observability

  • Consumer lag.
  • Producer error rate.
  • Publish latency.
  • Consumer processing latency.
  • Under-replicated partitions.
  • Broker disk usage.
  • Network throughput.
  • Request latency.
  • DLT volume.
  • Retry volume.
