Build: A Spring Boot banking transfer platform using Kafka.

React / Angular ↓ Spring Boot API ↓ Transfer Service ↓ SQL Transaction ↓ Outbox ↓ Kafka ├── Fraud ├── Notification ├── Audit ├── Analytics └── Customer Activity

Capstone Requirements

  • Implement transfer API.
  • Persist transfer transaction.
  • Write outbox event.
  • Publish transaction.completed.
  • Consume with multiple consumer groups.
  • Preserve per-account ordering.
  • Implement retries.
  • Implement DLT.
  • Implement idempotent consumers.
  • Add schema versioning.
  • Secure Kafka.
  • Monitor consumer lag.
  • Load test producers and consumers.
  • Document failure/recovery scenarios.

45.25 Kafka Interview Questions + Answers

Q. What is Kafka?

Answer: Kafka is a distributed event-streaming platform based on durable partitioned logs. It supports high-throughput publication and consumption of event streams and allows multiple independent consumer groups to process the same events.

Q. Topic vs partition?

Answer: A topic is a logical stream. A partition is an ordered append-only log within that topic. Partitions provide parallelism and determine the scope of ordering.

Q. What is a consumer group?

Answer: A consumer group is a set of consumers that cooperate to process partitions of a topic. Within a group, a partition is normally assigned to one consumer at a time.

Q. How do you guarantee ordering?

Answer: Kafka guarantees ordering within a partition. For per-account ordering, use a stable account identifier as the record key so related events are routed consistently to the same partition.

Q. What is consumer lag?

Answer: Consumer lag represents how far a consumer group's processed position is behind the available records. Growing lag can indicate insufficient consumer capacity, slow downstream dependencies or failures.

Q. At-least-once vs exactly-once?

Answer: At-least-once processing can deliver duplicates and therefore requires idempotent consumers. Exactly-once semantics can be achieved for certain Kafka workflows with transactional processing, but business-level exactly-once across external databases/services requires additional design.

Q. Why use the outbox pattern?

Answer: It addresses the consistency gap between committing a database change and publishing an event. The business change and outbox record are committed together, after which a reliable publisher sends the event to Kafka.

Q. How do you handle poison messages?

Answer: Retry transient failures with backoff. After configured attempts, route the message to a dead-letter topic with sufficient metadata for investigation and replay. Avoid infinite retries.

Q. How do you scale Kafka consumers?

Answer: Increase partitions to provide parallelism and scale consumers within the group up to the useful partition count. Also optimize processing latency and downstream dependencies.

Q. What is ISR?

Answer: ISR means in-sync replicas: replicas sufficiently caught up with the leader according to Kafka's replication rules. Healthy ISR is important for durability and availability.

Q. Kafka vs RabbitMQ?

Answer: Kafka is optimized around durable distributed event logs and high-throughput streaming. RabbitMQ is commonly used for messaging/routing patterns with strong queue-oriented semantics. The choice depends on event retention, replay, throughput, routing, ordering and operational requirements.

Q. Kafka vs REST?

Answer: REST is synchronous request/response communication. Kafka enables asynchronous event-driven communication and temporal decoupling. Mature architectures often use both.

45.26 Principal Engineer Kafka Design Question

Question: Design a banking event platform handling 50,000 transaction events per second.

Model answer: Start from event contracts and access patterns, then estimate partition count from throughput and consumer parallelism. Use replication for durability, stable keys for ordering, producer idempotence where appropriate, consumer groups for horizontal scaling, retry/DLT policies, outbox for database-to-event consistency, schema compatibility rules, security ACLs and observability around lag and broker health. Load-test with realistic event sizes and failure scenarios before selecting production capacity.

46. Redis Cache Zero-to-Hero — Spring Boot + Production Caching

Learn Redis from beginner to Principal Engineer: key/value storage, TTL, eviction, cache-aside, read-through/write-through concepts, distributed cache, sessions, rate limiting, counters, locks, pub/sub, streams, data structures, serialization, Spring Boot integration, cache consistency, failure modes, security and interview Q&A.

Layman explanation: Redis is like a very fast memory shelf next to your application. Put frequently needed information there so the application does not repeatedly travel to the slower database.
🧒 Kid analogy: Redis is the snack drawer on your desk instead of walking to the kitchen (database) every time you're hungry. It's much faster to grab a snack from the drawer, but the drawer is small and snacks can go stale (TTL/expiration) — so you restock it from the kitchen when needed.

46.1 Why Redis?

Application ↓ Redis Cache ├── HIT → Return quickly └── MISS ↓ Database ↓ Store in Redis ↓ Return

46.2 Redis Data Structures

TypeExampleUse
Stringcustomer:1001:nameSimple values/counters
Hashcustomer:1001Object-like fields
Listrecent:transactionsOrdered items
Setcustomer:rolesUnique values
Sorted Setbank:leaderboardRanked data
Streamtransaction-eventsEvent-like streams

46.3 Basic Redis Commands

SET customer:1001:name "Ravi"

GET customer:1001:name

SET account:1001:balance 50000 EX 60

INCR login:attempts:1001

DEL customer:1001:name

46.4 TTL

SET otp:123456 "valid"
EXPIRE otp:123456 300

TTL otp:123456

TTL prevents temporary data from remaining indefinitely.

46.5 Cache-Aside Pattern

🧒 Cache-aside is: peek in the snack drawer first (check the cache); if it's empty, walk to the kitchen (query the database), grab a snack, and — importantly — restock the drawer for next time (write to cache) before you leave.
public Account getAccount(
    String accountId
) {

    Account cached =
        redis.get(accountId);

    if (cached != null) {
        return cached;
    }

    Account account =
        repository.findById(accountId)
                  .orElseThrow();

    redis.set(
        accountId,
        account,
        Duration.ofMinutes(5)
    );

    return account;
}

46.6 Spring Boot Redis Dependency

<dependency>
    <groupId>
        org.springframework.boot
    </groupId>
    <artifactId>
        spring-boot-starter-data-redis
    </artifactId>
</dependency>

46.7 Spring Boot Configuration

spring:
  data:
    redis:
      host: localhost
      port: 6379
      timeout: 2s

Production deployments should configure authentication/TLS, connection pools and network access according to the Redis deployment.

46.8 Spring Cache

@Service
public class AccountService {

    @Cacheable(
        value = "accounts",
        key = "#accountId"
    )
    public Account getAccount(
        String accountId
    ) {
        return repository
            .findById(accountId)
            .orElseThrow();
    }
}

46.9 Cache Invalidation

@CacheEvict(
    value = "accounts",
    key = "#accountId"
)
public void updateAccount(
    String accountId,
    AccountUpdate request
) {
    repository.update(
        accountId,
        request
    );
}

Cache invalidation is one of the hardest parts of caching. Define the source of truth and invalidation strategy explicitly.

46.10 Cache Stampede

A cache stampede happens when a popular key expires and many requests simultaneously hit the database.

Popular Key Expires ↓ 1,000 Requests ↓ 1,000 DB Calls ↓ Database Overload

Mitigations include jittered TTLs, request coalescing, locks, background refresh and carefully chosen stale-while-revalidate strategies.

46.11 Cache Penetration

Repeated requests for data that does not exist can bypass the cache and repeatedly hit the database. Negative caching, validation and appropriate request controls can help.

46.12 Cache Avalanche

If a large number of keys expire at the same time, the database may receive a sudden load spike. Use TTL jitter, staggered expiration and capacity planning.

46.13 Redis Distributed Lock

SET lock:transfer:123 \
    token-abc \
    NX \
    EX 30

Important: Distributed locks are subtle. Do not implement a production locking protocol from a simplistic SET/DEL example without understanding ownership tokens, expiry, failure and the specific Redis deployment guarantees.

46.14 Rate Limiting

String key =
    "rate:user:" + userId;

Long count =
    redisTemplate.opsForValue()
        .increment(key);

if (count == 1) {
    redisTemplate.expire(
        key,
        Duration.ofMinutes(1)
    );
}

if (count > 100) {
    throw new RateLimitException();
}

46.15 Session Storage

Redis can store distributed session state so multiple application instances can access the same session data.

Browser ↓ Load Balancer ├── App 1 ─┐ ├── App 2 ─┼── Redis Session Store └── App 3 ─┘

46.16 Redis Pub/Sub

PUBLISH notifications
        "transaction completed"

Pub/Sub is useful for transient fan-out notifications but does not provide the same durable replay model as Kafka.

46.17 Redis Streams

XADD transaction-stream *
    accountId A1001
    amount 5000
    type DEBIT

Redis Streams can support stream processing patterns, but choose between Redis Streams and Kafka based on durability, replay, scale, ecosystem and operational requirements.

46.18 Redis vs Kafka

RedisKafka
Excellent low-latency cacheDurable event streaming
In-memory-first architectureDistributed persistent log
TTL is central to many cache use casesRetention/replay is central
Great for sessions/rate limits/countersGreat for event-driven integration

46.19 Cache Consistency Strategies

  • Cache-aside.
  • Write-through.
  • Write-behind/write-back.
  • Read-through.
  • Explicit invalidation.
  • TTL-based expiration.
  • Event-driven invalidation.

Choose according to freshness requirements. A bank account balance should not be casually cached with a long TTL if the business requires near-real-time correctness.

46.20 What Should You Cache?

Good CandidatePotentially Bad Candidate
Reference dataHighly volatile balance without freshness design
Product catalogUncontrolled sensitive information
Short-lived session stateData requiring strict source-of-truth semantics
Rate-limit countersLarge objects with low reuse
Expensive read resultsEverything by default

46.21 Redis Security

  • Never expose Redis directly to the public internet.
  • Use authentication and TLS where supported/required.
  • Use network isolation.
  • Restrict commands/permissions where the deployment supports it.
  • Do not store secrets casually in cache keys or values.
  • Define data retention/TTL.
  • Consider encryption and compliance requirements.

46.22 Redis Production Monitoring

  • Cache hit ratio.
  • Miss ratio.
  • Memory usage.
  • Evictions.
  • Latency.
  • Connection count.
  • Command throughput.
  • Replication health.
  • Persistence health.
  • Hot keys.

46.23 Redis Capstone — Banking Performance Layer

Build: Add Redis to the Digital Banking platform.

  • Cache customer profile/reference data.
  • Cache safe read-heavy API responses.
  • Implement TTL.

  • Secure Redis.
  • Compare Redis with Kafka for event workloads.

46.24 Redis Interview Questions + Answers

Q. Why Redis?

Answer: Redis provides very low-latency access to in-memory data structures and is useful for caching, sessions, counters, rate limiting, distributed coordination and selected streaming use cases.

Q. What is cache-aside?

Answer: The application first checks the cache. On a miss, it reads the source of truth and stores the result in the cache. The application controls when data is populated and invalidated.

Q. What is cache stampede?

Answer: Many requests simultaneously miss or expire the same popular cache entry and overwhelm the underlying database. Mitigations include request coalescing, jittered TTLs, locks and background refresh.

Q. Redis vs database?

Answer: Redis is commonly used as a low-latency data layer or cache, while the primary database usually remains the system of record. Redis should not be introduced merely to hide a poorly designed database query.

Q. Redis vs Kafka?

Answer: Redis is excellent for low-latency caching and data structures; Kafka is designed around durable distributed event logs and replayable event streams. They often complement each other.

Q. How do you cache banking data safely?

Answer: Classify data by freshness and sensitivity. Define the authoritative source, TTL and invalidation strategy. Avoid caching highly sensitive or rapidly changing values unless the consistency model is explicitly designed and approved.

Q. How do you prevent stale data?

Answer: Use appropriate TTLs, explicit invalidation, versioning or event-driven invalidation. For critical data, read from the source of truth when required by business correctness.

Q. What is a hot key?

Answer: A key receiving disproportionately high traffic. It can overload one Redis shard or create contention. Solutions depend on the workload and may include replication, local caching, key distribution or redesign.

Q. How do you troubleshoot Redis latency?

Answer: Check command latency, slow commands, memory pressure, evictions, network latency, hot keys, connection pools, serialization cost and server resource usage. Then reproduce with representative traffic.

46.25 Principal Engineer Redis Design Question

Question: Design a Redis caching layer for a banking API serving 100,000 requests per second.

Model answer: Start by classifying endpoints by freshness, sensitivity and reuse. Define cache keys and TTLs, choose cache-aside or another appropriate pattern, design invalidation, handle stampedes and hot keys, size memory and connections, configure high availability, secure the network, monitor hit ratio/p99 latency/evictions and keep the database as the authoritative source where appropriate. Load-test failure scenarios including Redis unavailability.

47. JWT + OAuth 2.0 + OpenID Connect — Zero-to-Hero Security Mastery

Understand authentication and authorization from beginner to Principal Engineer: sessions, tokens, JWT, access tokens, refresh tokens, OAuth 2.0 roles and flows, OIDC, scopes, claims, PKCE, client credentials, Spring Security, resource servers, token validation, key rotation, security pitfalls and interview Q&A.

Critical distinction: OAuth 2.0 is primarily an authorization framework. OpenID Connect adds an identity layer on top of OAuth 2.0. JWT is a token format, not an authentication protocol by itself.
🧒 Kid analogy: A JWT is like a wristband at a theme park: once security checks you at the gate and clips it on, every ride operator can just glance at the wristband (verify the signature) instead of re-checking your ID at every single ride. OAuth2 is the rulebook for how you're allowed to hand your wristband — or a limited "guest pass" version of it — to someone else on your behalf.

47.1 Authentication vs Authorization

AuthenticationAuthorization
Who are you?What are you allowed to do?
Login / identity verificationPermissions / policies
OIDC can provide identityOAuth scopes/roles/policies can help express access

47.2 Session vs Token

Session Browser ↓ Session ID ↓ Server-side session store Token Client ↓ Access Token ↓ Resource Server ↓ Validate token ↓ Authorize request

47.3 JWT Structure

header.payload.signature

A JWT commonly contains a header, payload claims and a signature. The payload is encoded, not automatically encrypted. Do not put secrets into ordinary JWT claims.

47.4 JWT Example

{
  "sub": "user-1001",
  "iss": "https://identity.example.com",
  "aud": "banking-api",
  "exp": 1787560000,
  "scope": "accounts:read transfers:create"
}

47.5 JWT Validation

A resource server should validate at minimum the signature and appropriate issuer, audience, expiry and relevant claims according to the application's security policy.

47.6 OAuth 2.0 Roles

RoleMeaning
Resource OwnerEntity that owns the protected resource
ClientApplication requesting access
Authorization ServerIssues tokens
Resource ServerHosts protected APIs

47.7 Authorization Code + PKCE

User ↓ Client App ↓ Authorization Server ↓ Login / Consent ↓ Authorization Code ↓ Client + PKCE verifier ↓ Access Token ↓ Banking API
🧒 PKCE is like a coat-check ticket you rip in half before handing off: you keep one half (the "verifier," generated fresh each time and never shared upfront) and give the desk only a stamped copy of the pattern on it (the "challenge"). When you come back for your coat (exchange the code for a token), you must show the matching torn half — so even if someone intercepts the claim ticket in between, they can't collect your coat without your exact torn half.

Authorization Code with PKCE is the standard direction for many public-client scenarios. Do not embed client secrets in browser/mobile applications.

47.8 Client Credentials

Client Credentials is appropriate for machine-to-machine authorization where there is no end-user authorization step.

Service A ↓ Authorization Server ↓ Access Token ↓ Service B

47.9 Scopes

accounts:read
accounts:write
transfers:create
transfers:approve

Scopes should express meaningful permissions and should be enforced by the resource server.

47.10 Roles vs Scopes

Roles often describe organizational/application responsibilities. Scopes describe delegated permissions. Large enterprise systems may use roles, scopes, claims and policy engines together.

47.11 OAuth 2.0 vs OIDC

OAuth 2.0OIDC
AuthorizationAuthentication/identity layer
Access tokenID token plus OAuth capabilities
API accessLogin/identity information

47.12 ID Token vs Access Token

ID token: intended for the client to understand authentication/identity information.

Access token: intended to authorize access to a resource server.

Do not casually send an ID token to an API as though it were an access token.

47.13 Spring Boot Resource Server

@Configuration
@EnableWebSecurity
public class SecurityConfig {

    @Bean
    SecurityFilterChain security(
        HttpSecurity http
    ) throws Exception {

        return http
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                .requestMatchers(
                    "/public/**"
                ).permitAll()
                .requestMatchers(
                    "/accounts/**"
                ).hasAuthority(
                    "SCOPE_accounts:read"
                )
                .requestMatchers(
                    "/transfers/**"
                ).hasAuthority(
                    "SCOPE_transfers:create"
                )
                .anyRequest()
                .authenticated()
            )
            .oauth2ResourceServer(
                oauth2 ->
                    oauth2.jwt()
            )
            .build();
    }
}

47.14 Spring Boot OAuth2 Configuration

spring:
  security:
    oauth2:
      resourceserver:
        jwt:
          issuer-uri:
            https://identity.example.com/issuer

The resource server can use the issuer metadata to discover the authorization server configuration and signing keys according to the provider's implementation.

47.15 Method-Level Authorization

@PreAuthorize(
    "hasAuthority('SCOPE_transfers:create')"
)
@PostMapping("/transfers")
public TransferResponse createTransfer(
    @RequestBody TransferRequest request
) {
    return transferService.create(request);
}

47.16 Security Architecture

User ↓ Frontend ↓ Authorization Server / OIDC ↓ Access Token ↓ API Gateway ↓ Spring Boot Resource Server ↓ JWT Validation ↓ Scope / Role / Policy Authorization ↓ Banking Service ↓ Database

47.17 Refresh Tokens

Refresh tokens allow a client to obtain new access tokens without requiring the user to authenticate every time. Protect them carefully and follow the authorization server's rotation/reuse-detection strategy.

47.18 JWT Expiration

Use short-lived access tokens appropriate to the risk profile. Do not solve every security problem by making access tokens valid for days or months.

47.19 Key Rotation

Authorization servers should rotate signing keys according to security policy. Resource servers should support discovery/key sets and key rollover rather than hard-coding one permanent public key.

47.20 JWT Common Mistakes

  • Trusting JWT claims without signature validation.
  • Not checking issuer.
  • Not checking audience.
  • Accepting expired tokens.
  • Putting secrets in claims.
  • Using ID tokens as API access tokens.
  • Long-lived access tokens.
  • Hard-coded signing keys.
  • Putting authorization only in the UI.
  • Logging full tokens.

47.21 Banking Authorization

Customer
  ├── Can read own account
  ├── Can create permitted transfer
  └── Cannot approve own high-risk transfer

Bank Employee
  ├── Can access permitted customer records
  └── Cannot bypass audit controls

Operations
  └── Can perform operational actions
      according to explicit policy

47.22 JWT + API Gateway

An API gateway can perform coarse-grained authentication/routing controls, but downstream services should still enforce authorization appropriate to their responsibilities. Do not assume “the request came through the gateway” means every internal caller is trusted.

47.23 OAuth2 for Microservices

User ↓ Frontend ↓ OIDC Login ↓ Access Token ↓ API Gateway ↓ Service A ↓ Service B ↓ Service C

For service-to-service calls, use an appropriate machine identity/client-credentials pattern or another workload identity mechanism. Avoid forwarding end-user tokens blindly across every internal service.

47.24 JWT vs Opaque Tokens

JWTOpaque Token
Self-contained claimsReference-like token
Can be validated locallyOften requires introspection
Potentially lower validation latencyCentralized control can simplify revocation
Revocation requires careful designIntrospection can provide current status

47.25 JWT Interview Questions + Answers

Q. What is JWT?

Answer: JWT is a compact token format for carrying claims, commonly signed so recipients can verify integrity and authenticity. JWT itself is not OAuth or OIDC.

Q. Is JWT encrypted?

Answer: A typical signed JWT is encoded and signed, not encrypted. Anyone who receives it can generally decode the payload. Sensitive secrets should not be placed in it unless an appropriate encrypted-token mechanism is deliberately used.

Q. OAuth2 vs OIDC?

Answer: OAuth 2.0 is an authorization framework. OIDC builds an identity/authentication layer on top of OAuth 2.0 and introduces concepts such as the ID token.

Q. Access token vs ID token?

Answer: An access token authorizes access to a resource server. An ID token communicates authentication/identity information to the client. They serve different audiences and purposes.

Q. What is PKCE?

Answer: Proof Key for Code Exchange adds a verifier/challenge mechanism to the authorization-code flow, helping protect public clients such as mobile and browser applications from authorization-code interception.

Q. How do you validate a JWT?

Answer: Verify its cryptographic signature and validate issuer, audience, expiration and application-specific claims/scopes. Also enforce authorization after authentication.

Q. How do you revoke JWTs?

Answer: Prefer short-lived access tokens and carefully managed refresh-token rotation/revocation. For immediate revocation requirements, use server-side state/introspection or an appropriate revocation strategy.

Q. Where should authorization happen?

Answer: At the resource server/business-service boundary where the system has enough context to decide whether the requested operation is permitted. UI checks are only convenience controls, not security boundaries.

Q. How would you secure a banking transfer API?

Answer: Authenticate with an approved identity system, validate the access token, enforce scopes/roles and resource ownership, validate business rules, use idempotency, apply transaction limits and step-up authentication where required, audit the operation and keep the final authorization decision in trusted backend code.

47.26 Principal Engineer Security Design Question

Question: Design authentication and authorization for a multi-channel banking platform with web, mobile, partner APIs and microservices.

Model answer: I would establish a central identity/authorization platform using OIDC for user authentication and OAuth 2.0 access delegation. Web/mobile public clients would use Authorization Code with PKCE. Machine-to-machine clients would use an appropriate workload/client-credentials pattern. APIs would act as resource servers validating issuer, audience, signature, expiry and scopes. Business services would enforce resource-level authorization. High-risk transactions would use step-up controls, idempotency and audit. Key rotation, secret management, token lifetime, logging, threat detection and incident response would be part of the platform design.

47.27 Security Capstone

Build: Secure the Digital Banking platform using OIDC/OAuth2/JWT.

  • OIDC login.
  • Authorization Code + PKCE.
  • JWT access token validation.
  • Scope-based authorization.
  • Role/policy authorization.
  • Spring Security resource server.
  • Service-to-service authentication.
  • Refresh-token protection.
  • Key rotation.
  • Audit logging.
  • Step-up authentication for high-risk transfers.
  • API security testing.
Principal Engineer takeaway: Authentication tells you who the caller is; authorization determines what they can do; business authorization determines whether they can perform this specific operation on this specific resource at this specific time.

31. Module 4 — Cloud Architecture + DevOps + Azure Kubernetes Mastery

This module prepares you for Principal Engineer discussions around Azure architecture, cloud-native Java platforms, Kubernetes, CI/CD, infrastructure as code, security, observability, reliability and cost optimization.

Principal Engineer mindset: Cloud architecture is not “which Azure service do I know?” It is the disciplined selection of compute, networking, identity, storage, data, deployment and operational capabilities based on availability, latency, security, compliance, scalability and cost requirements.
🧒 Kid analogy: Kubernetes is a strict but caring school principal for a building full of classrooms (pods/containers). If a classroom's teacher doesn't show up (a container crashes), the principal immediately assigns a substitute (restarts it) — and if a class gets too crowded, the principal opens up new classrooms automatically (autoscaling).

31.1 Azure Enterprise Reference Architecture

Internet | Azure Front Door / WAF / CDN | ↓ API Management | ↓ Application Gateway | ┌───────┴────────┐ ↓ ↓ AKS App Service | ┌────────────┼────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Spring Boot Workers AI / APIs | ├── Azure Database ├── Azure Cache for Redis ├── Service Bus / Event Hubs ├── Blob Storage └── Key Vault | ↓ Azure Monitor Application Insights | ↓ Log Analytics

A Principal Engineer should understand how enterprise cloud environments are governed before application workloads are deployed.

Know the difference between the managed control plane and worker node pools, and how Kubernetes objects interact.

31.6 Production Spring Boot Deployment on AKS

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: payment-service
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: payment-service
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: payment-service
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: payment
          image: myregistry.azurecr.io/payment:1.0.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: "500m"
              memory: "512Mi"
            limits:
              cpu: "1"
              memory: "1Gi"
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /actuator/health/readiness
              port: 8080
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /actuator/health/liveness
              port: 8080

Do not blindly copy resource values. Use load testing and production telemetry to establish realistic requests and limits.

31.7 Readiness vs Liveness vs Startup Probes

ProbeQuestionTypical Action
ReadinessCan this instance receive traffic?Remove from Service endpoints if false.
LivenessIs the process unhealthy enough to restart?Restart container if failure threshold is reached.
StartupHas a slow-starting application finished initialization?Protect slow startup from premature liveness failures.
Common production mistake: Using a deep dependency check for liveness can cause Kubernetes to restart every pod when a database is temporarily unavailable. Liveness should generally indicate whether the application process itself is unhealthy enough to restart.

31.8 Kubernetes Service Types

TypeUse
ClusterIPInternal service-to-service communication.
NodePortExposes a port on each node; usually not the preferred production ingress approach.
LoadBalancerRequests an external load-balancer integration.
HeadlessDirect pod discovery, often useful for stateful systems.

31.9 AKS Networking

Internet ↓ Azure Front Door / WAF ↓ Application Gateway ↓ AKS Ingress ↓ Kubernetes Service ↓ Pods ↓ Private Azure Services | ├── Azure SQL / PostgreSQL ├── Redis ├── Key Vault └── Storage

Principal-level topics include VNet integration, subnet planning, private endpoints, DNS, network security groups, egress control, ingress security and service-to-service traffic.

31.10 Private Endpoint

A private endpoint provides private network access to supported Azure services through a private IP address in a VNet. Discuss DNS resolution and network routing, not just the phrase “private endpoint.”

31.11 Azure Identity — Managed Identity

AKS Workload ↓ Managed Identity / Workload Identity ↓ Microsoft Entra ID ↓ Azure Resource ↓ Key Vault / Storage / DB / Service

Prefer identity-based access over embedding long-lived cloud credentials inside container images or Kubernetes manifests.

31.12 Azure Key Vault

  • Store secrets, keys and certificates where appropriate.
  • Use managed identity/workload identity to access secrets.
  • Rotate credentials.
  • Audit access.
  • Avoid putting secrets in Git.
  • Avoid baking secrets into container images.

31.13 Azure Container Registry

Developer ↓ Git ↓ CI Pipeline ↓ Build Image ↓ Security Scan ↓ Azure Container Registry ↓ AKS Deployment

Use immutable image tags or digests for controlled deployments. Avoid relying on a mutable latest tag in production.

31.14 CI/CD Reference Architecture

Developer ↓ Git Repository ↓ Pull Request ↓ Code Review ↓ CI ┌─┼──────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Build Test Security Scan └─┼──────────────────┘ ↓ Container Build ↓ Push to ACR ↓ Deploy to Dev ↓ Integration / Security Tests ↓ Approval / Policy ↓ Deploy to Production ↓ Monitor ↓ Rollback if needed

31.15 CI Pipeline Quality Gates

CompileBuild reproducibly.
Unit TestsFast feedback on business logic.
Static AnalysisQuality and maintainability.
Dependency ScanKnown vulnerable libraries.
Container ScanImage vulnerabilities and policy.
Integration TestsVerify service/database behavior.
API TestsValidate contracts.
Performance TestsValidate critical latency/throughput assumptions.

31.16 Deployment Strategies

StrategyIdeaRisk
RollingGradually replace old pods.Old/new versions coexist.
Blue-GreenMaintain two environments and switch traffic.Higher temporary capacity.
CanarySend a small percentage of traffic to new version.Requires strong telemetry and routing.

31.17 Canary Release

100% Traffic | ↓ Router ┌───┴───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ 95% Stable 5% Canary ↓ ↓ Metrics / Errors / Latency ↓ Decision / \ Promote Rollback

Canary decisions should use objective signals such as error rate, p95/p99 latency, saturation and business KPIs.

31.18 GitOps Concept

Git | | desired state ↓ GitOps Controller | ↓ Kubernetes | ↓ Actual State Drift ↓ Controller reconciles

The core idea is declarative desired state stored in version control and continuously reconciled with the cluster.

31.19 Infrastructure as Code with Terraform

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "app" {
  name     = "rg-payment-prod"
  location = "East US"
}

resource "azurerm_container_registry" "acr" {
  name                = "paymentprodacr"
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.app.name
  location            = azurerm_resource_group.app.location
  sku                 = "Premium"
}

Principal Terraform topics:

  • State management
  • Remote state and locking
  • Modules
  • Variables and outputs
  • Plan/apply workflow
  • Drift detection
  • Secrets handling
  • Environment separation
  • Policy and approval

31.20 Terraform State

Important: Terraform state is part of the infrastructure control plane. Protect it like sensitive infrastructure metadata. Use appropriate remote storage, locking, access controls and encryption.

31.21 Kubernetes Autoscaling

Traffic ↑ ↓ CPU / Memory / Custom Metric ↓ HPA ↓ Pod count ↑ ↓ Cluster capacity insufficient? ↓ Cluster Autoscaler ↓ Node count ↑

Understand the difference between HPA, cluster autoscaling and vertical resource changes. Autoscaling is not a substitute for good capacity planning.

31.22 Kubernetes Resource Requests and Limits

resources:
  requests:
    cpu: "500m"
    memory: "512Mi"
  limits:
    cpu: "1"
    memory: "1Gi"

Requests influence scheduling; limits constrain resource consumption according to Kubernetes/runtime behavior. Poorly chosen values can cause inefficient bin packing, throttling or out-of-memory kills.

31.23 Pod Disruption and High Availability

For critical services, consider:

  • Multiple replicas
  • Pod anti-affinity/topology spread
  • PodDisruptionBudget
  • Readiness probes
  • Multi-zone node pools
  • Graceful shutdown
  • Connection draining

31.24 Graceful Shutdown for Spring Boot

SIGTERM ↓ Application stops accepting new work ↓ Readiness becomes false ↓ Traffic drains ↓ In-flight requests complete ↓ Resources close ↓ Container exits

Design the application and deployment together. Kubernetes cannot provide graceful behavior if the application does not handle termination correctly.

31.25 Kubernetes Troubleshooting Framework

Incident ↓ kubectl get pods ↓ Pod status? | ├── Pending → scheduling/resources ├── CrashLoopBackOff → application/startup/config ├── ImagePullBackOff → registry/image/auth └── Running but failing → logs/probes/network/dependency ↓ describe pod ↓ logs ↓ events / metrics ↓ trace dependencies

31.26 Common AKS Incidents

SymptomLikely Areas
CrashLoopBackOffApplication crash, config, secret, dependency startup.
ImagePullBackOffImage name/tag, ACR auth, network access.
Pending podInsufficient resources, affinity, taints, node constraints.
503 from ServiceNo ready endpoints, selector mismatch, ingress/service configuration.
High latencyCPU throttling, DB, network, downstream services, GC.
OOMKilledMemory pressure, leak, undersized limit, excessive heap.
DNS failuresCluster DNS, service name, network policy or upstream resolution.

31.27 Azure Monitor + Application Insights

AKS / App / Azure Services | ↓ Telemetry | ┌──────┼──────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Metrics Logs Traces └──────┼──────┘ ↓ Azure Monitor ↓ Alert / Dashboard ↓ Incident Response

Correlate application traces with infrastructure metrics and dependency telemetry. A CPU dashboard alone rarely explains a distributed incident.

31.28 DevOps DORA Metrics

Deployment FrequencyHow often valuable changes reach production.
Lead Time for ChangesTime from code change to production.
Change Failure RatePercentage of deployments causing failure/remediation.
Time to RestoreHow quickly service recovers from incidents.

31.29 DevSecOps Pipeline

Code ↓ SAST ↓ Dependency Scan ↓ Unit Tests ↓ Build ↓ Container Scan ↓ IaC Scan ↓ Deploy ↓ DAST / Integration Tests ↓ Production ↓ Runtime Security / Monitoring

Security should be integrated throughout the delivery lifecycle rather than treated as a final approval step.

31.30 Supply Chain Security

  • Pin dependencies where appropriate.
  • Scan third-party libraries.
  • Use trusted base images.
  • Scan container images.
  • Restrict CI credentials.
  • Sign/verify artifacts where supported.
  • Generate and retain software bill of materials where required.
  • Use least-privilege identities.

31.31 Azure Cost Optimization

Right-sizeUse actual utilization rather than guesses.
AutoscaleMatch capacity to demand.
Storage tiersMove infrequently accessed data to appropriate tiers.
Reserved capacityUse committed purchasing where utilization is predictable.
ArchitectureUse managed services where operational savings justify cost.
Observability costControl log/metric retention and ingestion volume.

31.32 Cost Is an Architecture Requirement

A Principal Engineer should be able to explain not only why a system works, but what it costs and what drives the cost. At scale, network egress, storage, logging, database capacity and always-on compute can dominate application costs.

31.33 Azure + AKS Production Security Model

Identity ↓ Entra ID / Workload Identity ↓ RBAC ↓ AKS | ├── Network Policies ├── Private Cluster / Private Access ├── Image Security ├── Secrets via Key Vault ├── Pod Security Controls └── Audit / Monitoring

31.34 Zero-Downtime Architecture

Load Balancer ↓ Stable Version Pods ← traffic | └── rollout ↓ New Version ↓ Readiness succeeds ↓ Gradual traffic ↓ Old Pods drain ↓ Old Pods removed

Zero downtime is a system property involving application compatibility, database migrations, traffic routing, readiness, graceful shutdown and rollback—not merely a Kubernetes deployment setting.

31.35 Backward-Compatible Database Migration

Version N ↓ Add nullable/new column ↓ Deploy code that can read/write old + new ↓ Backfill data ↓ Switch reads ↓ Stop old writes ↓ Remove old field later

Avoid destructive schema changes in the same release as application code that still depends on the old schema.

31.36 Blue-Green vs Canary — Principal Trade-Off

Blue-GreenCanary
Fast traffic switchGradual exposure
Simple rollbackMore sophisticated telemetry
Higher temporary capacityCan reduce blast radius
Useful for major releasesUseful for risk-controlled incremental rollout

31.37 Architecture Decision Record

# ADR-001: Use AKS for the Core Java Platform

## Context
Need scalable container orchestration for multiple
Spring Boot services with independent deployment.

## Decision
Use Azure Kubernetes Service with managed identity,
private networking and automated CI/CD.

## Alternatives
- Azure App Service
- Azure Container Apps
- Virtual Machines

## Consequences
+ Kubernetes ecosystem and control
+ Standardized deployment model
- Higher operational complexity
- Requires platform engineering maturity

Principal engineers should document significant architectural decisions and their consequences.

31.38 Production Incident — AKS API Latency Spike

Scenario

At 10:05 AM, p99 latency for a Spring Boot payment API jumps from 250 ms to 4 seconds. CPU is 80%, pods are healthy, and there has been no application deployment.

What do you investigate?

Strong investigation path

  1. Check traffic volume and request distribution.
  2. Compare p50, p95 and p99 to identify tail-only vs broad degradation.
  3. Inspect distributed traces for the slowest span.
  4. Check database latency, connection pool utilization and lock waits.
  5. Check downstream service latency.
  6. Check GC, CPU throttling and memory pressure.
  7. Check node health and network telemetry.
  8. Check cache hit ratio and hot keys.
  9. Check message/queue lag if asynchronous dependencies exist.
  10. Compare infrastructure and business metrics against the incident start time.
  11. Mitigate first if customer impact is severe; root-cause second.
Principal behavior: Separate mitigation from diagnosis. Scaling out may reduce impact, but it does not prove CPU was the root cause.

31.41 Capstone Acceptance Criteria

32. Module 5 — AI / GenAI + Agentic Engineering + Modernization Leadership

This module prepares you for the AI modernization portion of the Principal Engineer role: LLM architecture, RAG, tool calling, agentic systems, evaluation, guardrails, AI engineering productivity, legacy modernization and executive-level AI strategy.

Principal Engineer mindset: Do not treat GenAI as a chatbot feature. Design it as an engineered production capability with measurable business outcomes, security boundaries, evaluation, observability, cost controls, human oversight and a clear failure model.
🧒 Kid analogy: An LLM alone is like a super well-read friend who can only talk from memory. RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) is that same friend, but now they're allowed to quickly flip through your actual notebook before answering — so they answer using YOUR real facts instead of guessing from memory.

32.1 The Enterprise AI Architecture

Users / Applications | ↓ AI Gateway / API | ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ AuthN/AuthZ Guardrails Rate Limits | | | └─────────────┼─────────────┘ ↓ AI Orchestration | ┌───────────────────┼──────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ RAG Tools Agents | | | ┌──────┼──────┐ | ┌─────┴─────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ ↓ ↓ ↓ Search Vector Rerank APIs / DBs Planner Specialist | Store | Systems Agent Agents └──────┬────────┘ ↓ LLM | ┌──────┴────────┐ ↓ ↓ Evaluation Safety / Policy | | └──────┬────────┘ ↓ Response | Observability Metrics / Logs / Traces / Cost / Quality

32.2 AI Engineering Layers

Model LayerFoundation models, embeddings, rerankers and specialized models.
Knowledge LayerDocuments, structured data, vector stores, search and knowledge graphs.
OrchestrationPrompting, workflows, agents, tool calls and state.
SafetyAuthorization, guardrails, validation and human approval.
EvaluationQuality, groundedness, relevance, safety and task success.
OperationsLatency, tokens, cost, tracing, model failures and drift.

32.3 LLM Fundamentals You Must Explain

At Principal level, be able to explain:

  • Tokens and context windows
  • Transformer-based language modeling at a high level
  • Temperature and sampling
  • System/user/developer instructions
  • Structured output
  • Function/tool calling
  • Embeddings
  • Retrieval-Augmented Generation
  • Fine-tuning vs prompting vs RAG
  • Latency and token economics

32.4 Prompt Engineering vs RAG vs Fine-Tuning

ApproachBest FitMain Trade-Off
PromptingBehavior/instruction changesLimited by model knowledge and context.
RAGPrivate/current knowledgeRetrieval quality and context management.
Fine-tuningSpecialized behavior/style/task patternsTraining complexity and lifecycle management.
Tool CallingTaking actions or accessing live systemsSecurity and authorization complexity.
Interview answer: I would generally prefer RAG for changing enterprise knowledge and tool calling for controlled access to live systems. Fine-tuning is considered when behavior or task performance cannot be achieved reliably through prompting, retrieval and orchestration alone.

32.5 RAG Architecture

Documents ↓ Ingestion ↓ Parsing / Cleaning ↓ Chunking ↓ Embeddings ↓ Vector Store | ├───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ Keyword Search Vector Search | | └───────┬───────┘ ↓ Hybrid Rank ↓ Reranker ↓ Context Builder ↓ LLM ↓ Grounded Answer

32.6 RAG Ingestion Pipeline

PDF / DOCX / HTML / DB ↓ Extract Text ↓ Normalize ↓ Metadata Enrich ↓ Chunking ↓ Embeddings ↓ Vector / Search Index ↓ Version + Audit

Metadata should include document ID, source, version, timestamp, tenant, access permissions and other fields required for filtering and authorization.

32.7 Chunking Strategy

Chunk size is not a universal magic number. Evaluate:

  • Semantic boundaries
  • Document structure
  • Query patterns
  • Context window
  • Retrieval precision
  • Retrieval recall
  • Duplicate context
  • Latency and token cost
Security requirement: Document-level authorization must be enforced before restricted content reaches the model context. Hiding unauthorized documents only after retrieval is too late.

32.8 Hybrid Retrieval

User Query | ├───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ Keyword Search Vector Search | | └───────┬───────┘ ↓ Fusion / Rank ↓ Reranker ↓ Top Context ↓ LLM

Keyword retrieval is useful for exact terms, identifiers and names. Vector retrieval is useful for semantic similarity. Combining both can improve robustness.

32.9 RAG Evaluation

MetricQuestion
Retrieval RecallDid we retrieve the information needed?
PrecisionHow much retrieved context is relevant?
GroundednessIs the answer supported by retrieved evidence?
Answer RelevanceDoes the response answer the actual question?
Task SuccessDid the workflow accomplish the business goal?
SafetyDid the system avoid prohibited or unsafe behavior?

32.10 Agentic Engineering

User Goal ↓ Agent ↓ Understand ↓ Plan ↓ Select Tool ↓ Execute ↓ Observe Result ↓ Evaluate ↓ Continue / Stop ↓ Final Answer

An agent is not simply an LLM with a long prompt. Agentic systems combine model reasoning with tools, state, policies, execution and feedback loops.

32.11 Workflow vs Agent

Deterministic WorkflowAgentic Workflow
Known sequence of stepsModel selects next action
Predictable behaviorMore flexible behavior
Easier testingRequires stronger evaluation
Lower varianceHigher variance
Preferred for stable processesUseful for ambiguous tasks
Principal design rule: Use deterministic workflows wherever the business process is known. Introduce agentic decision-making only where flexibility creates meaningful value.

32.12 Tool Calling

{
  "name": "get_account_balance",
  "arguments": {
    "customerId": "C12345"
  }
}

Never treat model-generated tool arguments as inherently trusted input.

32.13 Tool Authorization Architecture

User ↓ Agent ↓ Policy Engine ↓ Tool Authorization ↓ Allowed? ┌────┴────┐ Yes No ↓ ↓ Tool Reject ↓ Validate Inputs ↓ Execute ↓ Audit Result

Authorization must be enforced outside the model. The model can request an action; the policy layer decides whether the action is allowed.

32.14 High-Risk Agent Actions

Money MovementRequire explicit authorization and potentially human approval.
Production ChangesUse change controls and approval workflows.
Data DeletionRequire strong policy and confirmation.
External CommunicationControl who/what the agent can contact.
Credential AccessNever expose secrets directly to the model.
Code ExecutionSandbox, restrict and audit execution.

32.15 Agent State

Conversation State + Task State + Tool Results + Workflow State + Audit State ↓ Agent Runtime State

Persist only what is required. Separate conversational memory from authoritative business state. Never make the LLM transcript the system of record for financial or security-critical data.

32.16 Multi-Agent Architecture

Supervisor Agent | ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Research Agent Coding Agent Data Agent | | | Tools Tools Tools | | | └───────────────┼────────────────┘ ↓ Policy / Review ↓ Result

Use multiple agents only when specialized roles provide measurable benefits. Multi-agent architectures increase latency, token cost, state complexity and failure modes.

32.17 Agent Guardrails

Input GuardrailsValidate user intent, authorization and sensitive data.
Prompt GuardrailsProtect system instructions and context boundaries.
Tool GuardrailsAllowlist tools, arguments, scopes and resources.
Output GuardrailsValidate structure, policy and sensitive content.
Runtime GuardrailsTimeouts, budgets, iteration limits and circuit breakers.
Human GuardrailsApproval for high-risk actions.

32.18 Prompt Injection

Untrusted Document ↓ "Ignore previous instructions..." ↓ Retrieved Context ↓ LLM ↓ Potential Unsafe Action

Treat retrieved documents, web content, emails and tool outputs as untrusted data. Do not allow data content to override system policy.

32.19 PII and Sensitive Data Protection

User Input ↓ Sensitive Data Detection ↓ Redact / Tokenize / Policy ↓ LLM Context ↓ Response Validation ↓ Redaction ↓ User

Design data minimization, access control, retention, audit and redaction into the architecture rather than relying on the model to “remember not to leak data.”

32.20 LLM Cost Engineering

Prompt SizeReduce unnecessary context.
Model RoutingUse cheaper models for simpler tasks.
CachingCache deterministic/reusable results where safe.
RetrievalSend only relevant context.
Output LimitsControl maximum generated tokens.
Agent BudgetsLimit iterations, tool calls and total spend.

32.21 LLM Latency Engineering

Total Latency = Gateway + Retrieval + Reranking + Model Time-to-First-Token + Generation + Tool Calls + Post-processing

Optimize the slowest components. Streaming improves perceived latency but does not necessarily reduce total compute time.

32.22 Model Routing

Request ↓ Classifier / Policy | ├── Simple → Small / Fast Model ├── Complex → Strong Model ├── Retrieval → RAG Model └── High Risk → Human Review ↓ Response

Routing can optimize cost and latency while reserving expensive models for cases where they create measurable value.

32.23 LLM Reliability

  • Model timeout
  • Provider outage
  • Rate limit
  • Malformed output
  • Tool failure
  • Retrieval failure
  • Context overflow
  • Safety refusal
  • Hallucination

Use fallback models, structured output validation, retries with limits, circuit breakers, cached responses and graceful degradation where appropriate.

32.24 Structured Outputs

{
  "decision": "APPROVE",
  "riskScore": 0.08,
  "reasons": [
    "Customer history consistent",
    "Transaction within normal range"
  ]
}

Validate model output against a schema before downstream systems use it.

32.25 AI Evaluation Pipeline

Prompt / Model / RAG Change ↓ Evaluation Set ↓ Automated Evaluation ┌──────┼────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Quality Safety Regression └──────┼────────┘ ↓ Human Review ↓ Release Gate ↓ Production ↓ Online Monitoring

32.26 Golden Dataset

Create representative test cases covering normal, edge, adversarial and high-risk scenarios.

  • Expected answer or acceptable answer characteristics
  • Required citations/grounding
  • Expected tool usage
  • Forbidden actions
  • Latency budget
  • Cost budget

32.27 AI Observability

AI Request | ├── Model ├── Prompt Version ├── Retrieval ├── Tool Calls ├── Tokens ├── Latency ├── Cost └── Safety Result ↓ Trace ↓ AI Dashboard

Never log raw prompts or responses blindly when they may contain sensitive customer information. Apply privacy-aware logging and redaction.

32.28 AI Governance

AreaPrincipal Responsibility

32.29 AI Modernization Strategy

Legacy Portfolio ↓ Assess ↓ ┌─────┼────────────────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Keep Modernize Replace ↓ ┌─────┼───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ APIs Data AI ↓ ↓ Automation Copilot / Agent ↓ ↓ Business Outcomes

Do not “AI-enable everything.” Prioritize processes where AI can materially improve revenue, cost, speed, quality, customer experience or engineering productivity.

32.30 Legacy Java Modernization with AI

Code DiscoveryUse AI to map dependencies, modules and undocumented behavior.

OperationsSummarize incidents, logs and traces for engineers.

32.31 AI-Assisted Software Development Lifecycle

Requirements ↓ AI-assisted Analysis ↓ Specification ↓ Architecture ↓ AI-assisted Coding ↓ Automated Tests ↓ Security Review ↓ CI/CD ↓ Production ↓ Observability ↓ AI-assisted Incident Analysis ↓ Continuous Improvement

AI should augment engineering judgment, not bypass review, security controls or production governance.

32.32 Spec-Driven AI Development

Business Requirement ↓ Acceptance Criteria ↓ Technical Specification ↓ Architecture Decision ↓ AI Agent / Developer ↓ Implementation ↓ Automated Validation ↓ Human Review ↓ Release

A precise specification reduces ambiguity, improves agent performance and provides a measurable basis for validating generated changes.

32.33 Agentic SDLC Guardrails

32.34 Tool Authorization for Coding Agents

Coding Agent | ↓ Policy Engine | ┌──┼───────────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Read Repo Run Tests Edit Files Allowed Allowed Scoped | ↓ Commit / PR | ↓ Human Review | ↓ Merge Gate

Separate read, write, execute, deploy and production permissions. The agent should receive the minimum permissions necessary for its task.

32.35 Multi-Agent Engineering Architecture

Engineering Supervisor | ┌───────────────────┼──────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Requirements Agent Architecture Agent Coding Agent | | | ↓ ↓ ↓ Spec Store ADR / Design Repository | | | └───────────────────┼──────────────────┘ ↓ Test Agent ↓ Security Agent ↓ Review Gate ↓ PR

Agent specialization should correspond to clear responsibilities. Avoid a swarm of agents where one deterministic workflow would be simpler and cheaper.

32.42 Principal Scenario — Enterprise AI Copilot

Interviewer

Your organization wants an AI engineering copilot that can read repositories, analyze requirements, create code, run tests, open pull requests and explain production incidents. How would you design it safely?

Strong answer structure

  1. Define allowed use cases and prohibited actions.
  2. Separate read-only analysis from write/execute permissions.
  3. Use repository-scoped authorization.
  4. Use a policy engine outside the LLM.
  5. Use tools with explicit schemas and allowlists.
  6. Sandbox code execution.
  7. Require tests before creating a PR.
  8. Run security and dependency scans.
  9. Require human approval for merge and production deployment.
  10. Record tool calls, decisions and artifacts for audit.
  11. Measure productivity, defect rate and review rework.
  12. Set token, time and tool-call budgets.
Principal-level insight: The LLM should be treated as an untrusted reasoning component inside a trusted engineering control plane. Authorization, validation, policy and deployment permissions must remain deterministic.

32.43 Principal Scenario — Modernize a Legacy Java Platform

Interviewer

You inherit a 15-year-old Java monolith with limited tests, tightly coupled modules, a shared database and frequent production incidents. Leadership wants “AI modernization” in six months. What do you do?

Strong approach

  1. Establish business-critical flows and production baselines.
  2. Map dependencies, modules and runtime behavior.
  3. Use AI-assisted code discovery, but validate results against runtime evidence.
  4. Characterize the system with characterization tests before major refactoring.
  5. Identify bounded contexts and high-value seams.
  6. Introduce APIs around selected capabilities.
  7. Modernize incrementally using the Strangler pattern.
  8. Automate regression tests and CI/CD.
  9. Introduce observability before large architectural changes.
  10. Use AI for documentation, test generation, migration assistance and incident analysis.
  11. Measure delivery speed, defect rate, incident rate and cost.
  12. Avoid a six-month big-bang rewrite.

32.44 AI Modernization Roadmap

0–30 Days Discovery + Baseline + Governance ↓ 30–60 Days Observability + Test Foundation ↓ 60–90 Days AI-assisted Engineering Pilot ↓ 3–6 Months High-value Modernization Seams ↓ 6–12 Months Platform / Agent Expansion ↓ Continuous Measure ROI + Improve

32.47 Capstone Acceptance Criteria

  • Repository-scoped authentication and authorization.
  • RAG over engineering documentation with access filtering.
  • Tool calling with schema validation.
  • Policy engine outside the LLM.
  • Read-only and write/execute permissions separated.
  • Automated test execution.
  • Security and dependency scanning.
  • PR generation with human review.
  • Production deployment blocked without explicit approval.
  • Prompt/model/tool versions tracked.
  • Evaluation dataset and regression gate implemented.
  • Token, time and tool-call budgets implemented.
  • Audit trail for material agent actions.
  • Metrics for productivity, quality, cost and safety.

5. How measured?Quality, productivity, revenue, cost and risk metrics.

32.49 Principal Engineer Closing Framework

Business Outcome ↓ Use Case ↓ Architecture ↓ Data + Models + Tools ↓ Security + Guardrails ↓ Evaluation ↓ Production Operations ↓ ROI Measurement ↓ Scale / Stop Decision

The Principal-level AI answer: “I would start with the business outcome, establish a measurable baseline, choose the simplest architecture that can achieve the target, protect data and tools with deterministic controls, evaluate continuously, instrument production behavior and scale only after proving quality, safety and ROI.”

38. Production AI / GenAI / Agentic Engineering — Zero-to-Hero

A complete beginner-to-Principal Engineer guide to LLMs, token economics, production RAG, agents, MCP, skills, instructions, guardrails, Spec-Driven Development, AI-assisted engineering and enterprise AI modernization using one continuous banking case study.

Production mindset: A production AI system is not “just an LLM call.” It is a software system with identity, authorization, validation, observability, evaluation, cost controls, reliability, data governance and human accountability.
🧒 Kid analogy: An AI "agent" is like giving your smart friend not just a notebook to read (RAG) but also a set of tools — a phone to make calls, a calculator, a key to certain doors — and clear rules about which doors they're allowed to open by themselves versus which ones need your permission first (guardrails/human approval).

38.1 The Banking AI Platform

Customer ↓ Web / Mobile ↓ AI Assistant ↓ Input Guardrails ↓ Agent Orchestrator ├── RAG ├── Banking Tools ├── MCP └── Specialist Agents ↓ Authorization ↓ Bank APIs / Microservices ↓ Audit + Observability ↓ Human Approval for High-Risk Actions

38.2 LLM Fundamentals

An LLM predicts the next token based on context. A token is a small unit of text; it may be a whole word, part of a word or punctuation.

Prompt
  ↓
Tokenizer
  ↓
Tokens
  ↓
Model
  ↓
Output Tokens
  ↓
Detokenizer
  ↓
Text

Interview: What is an LLM?

Answer: An LLM is a neural language model trained on large datasets to model sequences of tokens. In applications, we provide instructions and context and ask it to generate or transform information. It does not automatically become a trusted source of truth; production systems must ground important answers in authoritative data.

38.3 Token Optimization — How to Save LLM / Copilot Costs

Layman explanation: Tokens are like the number of words a taxi carries. If you send unnecessary passengers every time, every trip becomes expensive. Send only what the model needs.

Bad Design

Huge system prompt + Entire chat history + Entire source repository + All documents + All tool definitions ↓ LLM ↓ High token cost + latency

Optimized Design

Small stable instructions + Relevant context + Compressed history + Only required tools + Cached repeated context ↓ Model Router ├── Small model → simple work └── Large model → difficult work

Practical Token-Saving Techniques

  1. Prompt compression: remove repeated prose and unnecessary examples.
  2. Context selection: retrieve only relevant documents/code.
  3. Conversation summarization: replace old conversation with a compact state summary.
  4. Tool selection: expose only tools needed for the current task.
  5. Output limits: constrain maximum output when a short answer is enough.
  6. Caching: reuse stable instructions/results when the platform supports it.
  7. Model routing: use smaller/cheaper models for classification, extraction and simple transformations.
  8. Batch work: combine compatible operations when latency/business semantics allow.
  9. Structured output: request only required fields instead of verbose prose.
  10. RAG: retrieve relevant chunks instead of sending an entire knowledge base.

Token Cost Estimation

monthly_input_tokens =
    requests_per_month
    * average_input_tokens;

monthly_output_tokens =
    requests_per_month
    * average_output_tokens;

monthly_cost =
    input_tokens_cost
    + output_tokens_cost;

For real deployments, use the current provider pricing rather than hard-coding prices into architecture documents.

GitHub Copilot / AI Coding Cost Optimization

  • Keep repository instructions concise and hierarchical.
  • Give an agent the exact files relevant to the task.
  • Use repository search instead of dumping entire repositories into context.
  • Break large tasks into specifications and focused implementation steps.
  • Ask for targeted diffs rather than rewriting unrelated files.
  • Use smaller models for straightforward transformations where available.
  • Run tests locally/automatically and provide only failure context back to the agent.
  • Store stable architectural rules in reusable instructions rather than repeating them in every prompt.

38.4 Context Engineering

Context engineering means deliberately deciding what information an AI system receives, in what format, at what time and for what purpose.

User Request ↓ Intent ↓ Retrieve Relevant Context ↓ Filter by Authorization ↓ Compress ↓ Prioritize ↓ Prompt / Tool Context ↓ Model

Context Priority

  1. Safety and authorization constraints.
  2. Task-specific instructions.
  3. Authoritative business data.
  4. Relevant conversation state.
  5. Useful examples.
  6. Optional background.

38.5 Production RAG — High Performance + Low Cost

RAG means Retrieval-Augmented Generation. Instead of asking the model to remember every bank policy, retrieve the relevant policy and give it to the model.

Bank Policy PDFs ↓ Parse ↓ Clean ↓ Chunk ↓ Metadata ↓ Embeddings ↓ Vector / Search Index ↓ User Question ↓ Retrieve ↓ Filter / Rerank ↓ Context ↓ LLM ↓ Grounded Answer + Citations

Chunking

CHUNK_SIZE = 800
CHUNK_OVERLAP = 120

These are starting points, not universal constants. Evaluate retrieval quality on real documents.

Metadata

{
  "document": "loan-policy.pdf",
  "department": "loans",
  "country": "IN",
  "version": "2026-08",
  "accessLevel": "INTERNAL"
}

ACL-Aware RAG

A user must never retrieve a document merely because it is semantically relevant. Retrieval must also respect identity and authorization.

Question ↓ Identity ↓ Authorization Filter ↓ Semantic Retrieval ↓ Reranking ↓ Allowed Context Only ↓ LLM

Hybrid Retrieval

Combine keyword search and semantic/vector search when both exact terms and conceptual meaning matter.

Reranking

Retrieve a wider candidate set cheaply, then use a stronger reranker to select the most relevant passages before calling the expensive generation model.

Production RAG Cost Controls

  • Use metadata filtering before expensive retrieval steps.
  • Keep chunks focused.
  • Retrieve a small top-K and rerank.
  • Cache repeated questions/results where safe.
  • Use smaller embedding models when evaluation shows acceptable quality.
  • Compress retrieved context.
  • Use a smaller generation model for low-risk FAQ tasks.
  • Measure retrieval recall, answer quality, latency and cost together.

RAG Evaluation

MetricQuestion
Retrieval RecallDid we retrieve the relevant source?
PrecisionAre retrieved passages actually useful?
GroundednessIs the answer supported by retrieved evidence?
Answer QualityDid the answer solve the user's problem?
LatencyHow quickly did the answer arrive?
CostHow expensive is each successful answer?

38.6 What Is an AI Agent?

An agent is an AI-driven software loop that can interpret a goal, decide which actions are needed, call tools, inspect results and continue until the task reaches an appropriate stopping condition.

Goal ↓ Plan ↓ Choose Tool ↓ Authorization ↓ Tool Call ↓ Observe Result ↓ Reason ↓ Next Action / Finish

Agent vs Chatbot

ChatbotAgent
Mostly responds with textCan execute controlled actions
Usually one interaction pathCan perform multi-step workflows
Limited toolsTool-enabled
Lower operational riskRequires authorization and guardrails

38.7 Tool Calling

{
  "tool": "get_account_balance",
  "arguments": {
    "accountId": "ACC1001"
  }
}

The model should not directly access a database just because it asks to. A controlled tool layer validates the request and enforces authorization.

Banking Tool

async function getAccountBalance(
  user,
  accountId
) {

  authorize(
    user,
    "ACCOUNT_READ",
    accountId
  );

  return accountService
    .getBalance(accountId);
}

38.8 MCP — Model Context Protocol

Layman explanation: MCP provides a standardized way for AI applications to discover and use external tools/resources through a controlled protocol instead of creating a completely different integration mechanism for every system.

AI Application ↓ MCP Client ↓ MCP Server ├── Tools ├── Resources └── Prompts ↓ Banking Systems

MCP Tool Example

{
  "name": "get_account_balance",
  "description": "Get balance for an authorized account",
  "inputSchema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {
      "accountId": {
        "type": "string"
      }
    },
    "required": ["accountId"]
  }
}

Production MCP Security

  • Authenticate the caller.
  • Authorize every tool invocation.
  • Validate tool arguments.
  • Apply least privilege.
  • Never trust model-generated account IDs.
  • Audit every sensitive action.
  • Rate-limit dangerous operations.
  • Require human approval for high-impact actions.

38.9 Agent Skills vs Tools vs Instructions

ConceptSimple MeaningBanking Example
InstructionRule for how the agent should behaveNever reveal another customer's data.
SkillReusable capability/workflowAnalyze monthly spending.
ToolExecutable operationGet account balance.
ResourceInformation exposed to the applicationBank policy document.

38.10 Agent Guardrails

Guardrails constrain what the AI system can receive, decide, output and execute.

User Input ↓ Input Guardrail ├── Injection Detection ├── Sensitive Data Rules └── Policy Check ↓ Agent ↓ Tool Authorization ↓ Tool ↓ Output Guardrail ├── Sensitive Data Check ├── Policy Check └── Grounding Check ↓ User

Prompt Injection

An attacker may attempt to manipulate an agent through user input or retrieved content. Treat external content as untrusted data and keep authority in explicit application controls rather than model instructions alone.

Tool Authorization

if (!authorizationService.can(
    user,
    "TRANSFER_CREATE",
    fromAccount
)) {
  throw new ForbiddenError();
}

Risk-Based Approval

ActionRiskControl
Read public FAQLowAutomatic
Read customer's own balanceMediumAuthenticated + authorized
Create beneficiaryHighStrong auth + policy checks
Transfer moneyVery highExplicit confirmation / step-up auth / approval as required

38.11 Agent Observability

Log enough to debug the system without logging secrets or unnecessary sensitive banking information.

  • Request/correlation ID.
  • Agent workflow ID.
  • Tool selected.
  • Tool latency.
  • Success/failure.
  • Model and version.
  • Token usage.
  • Estimated cost.
  • Guardrail decisions.
  • Human approval events.

38.12 Agent Evaluation

Golden Dataset ↓ Agent Run ↓ Evaluate ├── Correctness ├── Tool Selection ├── Safety ├── Groundedness ├── Latency └── Cost ↓ Release Gate

Do not evaluate agents only by asking whether the final sentence sounds good. Test tool selection, authorization behavior, failure recovery and unsafe requests.

38.13 Spec-Driven Development

Layman explanation: Instead of telling an AI coding agent “build a banking transfer feature,” first write down exactly what the feature must do, what it must not do, the API contract, acceptance criteria and tests.

Business Requirement ↓ Functional Specification ↓ Technical Specification ↓ API Contract ↓ Acceptance Criteria ↓ Test Cases ↓ AI Coding Agent ↓ Implementation ↓ Automated Tests ↓ Code Review ↓ Deployment

Example Specification

Feature: Create Transfer

Actors:
Authenticated customer

Rules:
1. Source account must belong to customer.
2. Amount must be greater than zero.
3. Destination must be valid.
4. Idempotency key is required.
5. Every transfer creates an audit event.
6. High-risk transfers require configured approval.

Acceptance:
Given a valid customer
When a valid transfer is submitted
Then the API returns a transaction ID
And duplicate submission with the same
idempotency key does not create a second transfer.

Why SDD Helps AI Coding

  • Reduces ambiguity.
  • Reduces repeated prompting.
  • Improves generated-code consistency.
  • Creates testable acceptance criteria.
  • Makes code review easier.
  • Creates durable engineering knowledge.

38.14 AI-Assisted vs AI-Augmented vs AI-Native

ModeMeaningExample
AI-AssistedAI helps an engineer perform a task.Generate a unit test.
AI-AugmentedAI is integrated into the engineering workflow.Agent analyzes requirements, proposes code and runs tests under human review.
AI-NativeThe product/workflow is designed around AI capabilities.Banking operations assistant that safely orchestrates authorized workflows.

38.15 Production Agent Architecture

User ↓ API Gateway ↓ Identity ↓ Agent Gateway ↓ Input Guardrails ↓ Orchestrator ├── RAG ├── MCP ├── Tools └── Specialist Agents ↓ Authorization ↓ Enterprise APIs ↓ Databases / Event Bus ↓ Audit / Observability Cross-cutting: Security Cost Evaluation Rate Limits Human Approval Resilience

38.16 Agent Reliability Patterns

  • Timeout: never allow an agent to run indefinitely.
  • Retry: retry only safe/idempotent operations.
  • Circuit breaker: stop repeatedly calling a failing dependency.
  • Budget: limit tokens, tool calls and execution time.
  • Maximum steps: prevent runaway agent loops.
  • Fallback: provide a deterministic workflow or human handoff.
  • Idempotency: prevent duplicate financial operations.

38.17 AI Modernization

AI modernization is not “put an LLM in front of a legacy system.” Start with business outcomes and modernize safely.

Legacy Banking System ↓ Characterize ↓ API Facade ↓ Observability ↓ Extract Bounded Capability ↓ Modern Service ↓ AI-Assisted Engineering ↓ Automated Tests ↓ Gradual Migration ↓ Retire Legacy Capability

SecurityIdentify likely vulnerabilitiesSecurity tooling + expert review

38.19 Production Checklist

  • Identity and authorization.
  • Prompt injection defenses.
  • Input/output validation.
  • PII/sensitive-data controls.
  • Tool-level authorization.
  • Human approval for high-risk actions.
  • Token/cost budgets.
  • Latency budgets.
  • Maximum agent steps.
  • Timeouts and retries.
  • Idempotency for financial actions.
  • RAG evaluation.
  • Agent evaluation.
  • Observability and audit.
  • Model/version governance.
  • Dependency and supply-chain security.
  • CI/CD quality gates.
  • Rollback strategy.

38.20 AI / Agentic Interview Q&A

Q. How would you reduce LLM cost by 50%?

Answer: First establish a baseline by measuring input tokens, output tokens, requests, model mix, latency and cost per successful task. Then reduce unnecessary context, compress history, cache stable context, retrieve only relevant RAG chunks, route simple tasks to smaller models, constrain outputs and eliminate redundant agent/tool calls. Finally, validate quality so cost savings do not simply reduce business value.

Q. How do you secure an AI agent that can transfer money?

Answer: The model never receives unrestricted financial authority. Put the agent behind an authorization layer, validate arguments against authenticated user identity, enforce account ownership and transaction limits, require idempotency, apply risk-based step-up authentication or human approval, audit every action and provide deterministic fallback behavior. The backend remains the final security boundary.

Q. What is MCP and why use it?

Answer: MCP standardizes how AI applications can interact with external capabilities and context providers. It can reduce bespoke integration work, but MCP does not replace authentication, authorization, validation or governance. Every exposed capability still needs enterprise security controls.

Q. RAG vs fine-tuning?

Answer: Use RAG when the model needs current/private/traceable information. Fine-tuning is more appropriate for changing model behavior or specialization rather than simply injecting frequently changing enterprise facts. They can also be combined.

Q. What is Spec-Driven Development?

Answer: It makes an explicit specification the contract between business intent, engineers and AI coding agents. The specification defines behavior, constraints, interfaces and acceptance criteria before implementation. This reduces ambiguity and makes AI-generated code easier to validate.

Q. What is the biggest mistake companies make with agents?

Answer: Giving an LLM too much authority before establishing deterministic authorization, observability, evaluation and failure controls. An agent should be treated as an untrusted decision-making component operating inside a trusted software control plane.

38.21 Production Agent Capstone

Project: Banking Operations Copilot.

  • Answer banking policy questions using ACL-aware RAG.
  • Retrieve a customer's own account information.
  • Explain transaction history.
  • Create a draft transfer.
  • Require explicit confirmation and configured approval for high-risk operations.
  • Use MCP to expose controlled banking tools.
  • Apply input/output guardrails.
  • Track token usage and cost.
  • Record audit events.
  • Evaluate the agent against a golden dataset.

Capstone Agent Flow

"Transfer ₹5,000 to my saved beneficiary" ↓ Identity ↓ Input Guardrail ↓ Agent ↓ Beneficiary Tool ↓ Authorization ↓ Transfer Draft Tool ↓ Risk Policy ↓ Customer Confirmation ↓ Idempotent Transfer Tool ↓ Audit Event ↓ Notification ↓ Final Response

Capstone Acceptance Tests

  1. Customer cannot access another customer's account.
  2. Prompt injection cannot bypass authorization.
  3. Transfer tool rejects unauthorized account IDs.
  4. Duplicate idempotency keys do not create duplicate transfers.
  5. High-risk action requires configured approval.
  6. RAG answers cite authorized source documents.
  7. Agent stops after maximum configured steps.
  8. Token budget is enforced.
  9. All sensitive actions produce audit records.
  10. Failure results in safe fallback/human handoff.
Principal Engineer takeaway: The goal is not to build the smartest agent. The goal is to build the most useful, measurable, secure, reliable and economically sustainable system that can safely produce the required business outcome.

38.22 Python + LangChain + LangGraph + LangSmith Production Implementation

All AI/GenAI/Agent/RAG/MCP examples in this module should be learned primarily through Python. Java remains useful for enterprise backend integration, but Python is the main implementation language for the AI layer because of its ecosystem around LLMs, RAG, agents and evaluation.

Learning rule: First understand the plain Python implementation. Then use LangChain when reusable abstractions help, LangGraph when the workflow needs explicit state/control, and LangSmith when you need tracing, evaluation and production observability.
🧒 Kid analogy: If your AI agent were a school project, plain Python is doing the assignment by hand, LangChain is a box of pre-made craft supplies (reusable building blocks) so you don't cut every shape from scratch, LangGraph is the step-by-step project plan pinned to the wall (explicit workflow/state), and LangSmith is the teacher's grading sheet that tracks how every step went.

38.22.1 Python AI Technology Stack

Python ↓ LLM Provider ↓ LangChain ├── Models ├── Prompts ├── Documents ├── Retrievers └── Tools ↓ LangGraph ├── State ├── Nodes ├── Edges └── Human Approval ↓ LangSmith ├── Tracing ├── Evaluation ├── Datasets └── Monitoring
