LeadershipCan influence without authority and grow technical leaders.
ExecutiveCan communicate risk, cost, options and outcomes concisely.

33.52 Final 30-Second Principal Engineer Philosophy

“I solve business problems through technology. I establish clarity before complexity, make architecture decisions based on explicit trade-offs, build for reliability and security, automate repeatable work, develop people and platforms, and measure engineering success through customer and business outcomes.”

33.53 Final Interview Strategy

Listen Carefully ↓ Clarify Ambiguity ↓ Structure the Answer ↓ State Assumptions ↓ Explain Trade-offs ↓ Discuss Failure Modes ↓ Connect to Business ↓ Communicate Clearly ↓ Recommend ↓ Close with Outcome
Remember: For a Principal Engineer interview, the interviewer is evaluating not only whether you can design the system, but whether they can trust you to make difficult decisions, influence strong engineers, communicate with executives, protect customers and move the organization forward.

36. Zero-to-Hero Learning Layer — Explain Every Topic Like a 12th-Pass Beginner

This section is deliberately beginner-friendly. It starts with everyday analogies, then introduces terminology, then shows small code examples, then builds toward enterprise implementation. Use it before the Principal-level sections when a topic feels unfamiliar.

Learning rule: Never memorize architecture diagrams. First understand the problem, then the simplest solution, then why the simple solution stops working at scale, and finally the enterprise solution.
🧒 Kid analogy: This whole chapter is written like explaining a video game to someone who's never played before: what's the goal, what are the basic controls, and only then, the advanced combo moves.

36.1 What Is Software?

Software is simply a set of instructions that tells a computer what to do.

Human Problem ↓ Rules / Instructions ↓ Program ↓ Computer ↓ Result

Example: A banking application receives “transfer ₹1,000 from A to B”. The software checks the user, checks the balance, records the transaction and returns the result.

36.2 What Is a Programming Language?

A programming language is a structured way for humans to write instructions that a computer can execute.

public class Hello {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello!");
    }
}

Java is popular in large organizations because it has a mature ecosystem, strong tooling, good performance and a large enterprise community.

36.3 What Is Java?

Java lets us write application code once and run it on systems that have a compatible Java runtime.

Java Source Code ↓ Java Compiler ↓ Bytecode (.class) ↓ JVM ↓ Operating System / Hardware

36.4 Your First Java Program

public class Main {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        String name = "Ranjan";
        int age = 18;

        System.out.println("Name: " + name);
        System.out.println("Age: " + age);
    }
}

Understand: String stores text, int stores whole numbers, and System.out.println() prints information.

36.5 Variables

int age = 20;
double salary = 50000.50;
boolean active = true;
String name = "Amit";

Think of a variable as a labelled box that stores a value.

36.6 Conditions

if (age >= 18) {
    System.out.println("Adult");
} else {
    System.out.println("Minor");
}

A condition allows software to make decisions.

36.7 Loops

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
    System.out.println(i);
}

A loop means “repeat this instruction”.

36.8 Methods

static int add(int a, int b) {
    return a + b;
}

public static void main(String[] args) {
    int result = add(10, 20);
    System.out.println(result);
}

36.9 Classes and Objects

A class is a blueprint. An object is a real thing created from that blueprint.

class BankAccount {

    String accountNumber;
    double balance;

    void deposit(double amount) {
        balance += amount;
    }
}

BankAccount account = new BankAccount();
account.accountNumber = "ACC100";
account.deposit(5000);

36.10 Encapsulation

Encapsulation means keeping data protected and allowing controlled operations.

class BankAccount {

    private double balance;

    public void deposit(double amount) {
        if (amount > 0) {
            balance += amount;
        }
    }

    public double getBalance() {
        return balance;
    }
}

36.11 Interface — Think of a Contract

interface PaymentGateway {
    void pay(double amount);
}

class UpiGateway implements PaymentGateway {
    public void pay(double amount) {
        System.out.println("Paid using UPI");
    }
}

class CardGateway implements PaymentGateway {
    public void pay(double amount) {
        System.out.println("Paid using card");
    }
}

The application depends on the contract, not the specific payment provider.

36.12 Exception Handling

try {
    int result = 10 / 0;
} catch (ArithmeticException e) {
    System.out.println("Cannot divide by zero");
}

Production applications should also log useful context and return safe, meaningful errors to users.

36.13 Collections

List<String> names =
    List.of("Amit", "Priya", "Rahul");

for (String name : names) {
    System.out.println(name);
}

Collections are containers for multiple values.

36.14 What Is SQL?

SQL is a language used to work with relational databases.

CREATE TABLE customers (
    id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    name VARCHAR(100),
    email VARCHAR(200)
);

INSERT INTO customers(id, name, email)
VALUES (1, 'Amit', 'amit@example.com');

SELECT * FROM customers;

36.15 Primary Key and Foreign Key

A primary key uniquely identifies a row. A foreign key connects one table to another.

CREATE TABLE orders (
    id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    customer_id BIGINT,
    amount DECIMAL(12,2),
    FOREIGN KEY (customer_id)
        REFERENCES customers(id)
);

36.16 What Is an API?

An API is a defined way for one software system to communicate with another.

Mobile App ↓ HTTP REST API ↓ Java Application ↓ Database

36.17 Your First REST API

@RestController
@RequestMapping("/hello")
public class HelloController {

    @GetMapping
    public String hello() {
        return "Hello World";
    }
}

Calling GET /hello returns the message.

36.18 What Is JSON?

{
  "name": "Amit",
  "age": 25,
  "active": true
}

JSON is a common format for sending structured data between applications.

36.19 What Is HTTP?

MethodTypical Meaning
GETRead
POSTCreate / submit
PUTReplace/update
PATCHPartial update
DELETEDelete

36.20 HTTP Status Codes

CodeMeaning
200Success
201Created
400Bad request
401Not authenticated
403Not authorized
404Not found
409Conflict
500Server error

36.21 What Is Spring?

Spring is a Java framework that helps us build large applications without manually creating and connecting every object.

Without Spring Class A → manually creates → Class B → manually creates → Class C With Spring Application → Spring Container → creates and connects A, B, C

36.22 Dependency Injection — Beginner Explanation

@Service
class EmailService {
    void send(String message) {
        System.out.println(message);
    }
}

@Service
class OrderService {

    private final EmailService emailService;

    OrderService(EmailService emailService) {
        this.emailService = emailService;
    }

    void createOrder() {
        emailService.send("Order created");
    }
}

Spring creates EmailService and gives it to OrderService. This is dependency injection.

36.23 What Is Spring Boot?

Spring Boot makes Spring applications easier to start by providing sensible defaults, auto-configuration and embedded servers.

@SpringBootApplication
public class Application {

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(
            Application.class,
            args
        );
    }
}

36.24 Typical Spring Boot Architecture

Controller ↓ Service ↓ Repository ↓ Database Controller = HTTP communication Service = Business rules Repository = Data access

36.25 Complete CRUD Example

@Entity
class Product {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue
    private Long id;

    private String name;
    private BigDecimal price;
}
public interface ProductRepository
        extends JpaRepository<Product, Long> {
}
@Service
class ProductService {

    private final ProductRepository repository;

    ProductService(ProductRepository repository) {
        this.repository = repository;
    }

    Product create(Product product) {
        return repository.save(product);
    }

    List<Product> findAll() {
        return repository.findAll();
    }
}
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/products")
class ProductController {

    private final ProductService service;

    ProductController(ProductService service) {
        this.service = service;
    }

    @PostMapping
    Product create(@RequestBody Product product) {
        return service.create(product);
    }

    @GetMapping
    List<Product> all() {
        return service.findAll();
    }
}

36.26 What Is a Database Transaction?

Imagine transferring ₹1,000 between two accounts. Debit and credit should behave as one business operation.

@Transactional
public void transfer(
        Account from,
        Account to,
        BigDecimal amount) {

    from.debit(amount);
    to.credit(amount);
}

If an unexpected database failure occurs, the transaction can be rolled back according to the configured transaction behavior.

36.27 What Is a Microservice?

A microservice is a separately deployable application focused on a business capability.

One Big Application ↓ Customer + Order + Payment + Shipping Microservices ↓ Customer Service Order Service Payment Service Shipping Service

Microservices are not automatically better. They make sense when independent ownership, deployment, scaling or domain boundaries justify their operational cost.

36.28 What Is a Distributed System?

A distributed system is a system where multiple computers cooperate to provide one larger service.

Example: when you use a banking application, your request may travel through a gateway, authentication service, account service, payment service, database and notification service.

36.29 Why Distributed Systems Are Difficult

  • Networks can fail.
  • Messages can be delayed.
  • Servers can crash.
  • Clocks differ.
  • Data can become temporarily inconsistent.
  • One slow dependency can slow many services.

36.30 Timeout — The First Resilience Rule

HTTP request
   ↓
Service A
   ↓  timeout = 2 seconds
Service B

If B does not respond:
A stops waiting and applies fallback/error logic.

Never allow remote calls to wait forever.

36.31 Retry — Beginner Example

try {
    callPaymentProvider();
} catch (TemporaryException e) {
    wait();
    callPaymentProvider();
}

Real systems need bounded retries, exponential backoff and jitter.

36.32 Circuit Breaker — Electricity Analogy

An electrical circuit breaker stops sending electricity through a dangerous circuit. A software circuit breaker similarly stops repeatedly calling an unhealthy dependency.

Healthy → Calls allowed ↓ failure threshold Open → Calls blocked ↓ wait period Half Open → Test call ↓ Healthy / Open

36.33 What Is a Message Queue?

A queue stores work so that the producer and consumer do not need to work at exactly the same speed.

Order Service ↓ Queue ↓ Notification Worker

This is useful when notification processing is slower than order creation.

36.34 What Is Kafka/Pulsar/Event Bus?

An event platform allows applications to publish events and consumers to process them independently.

OrderCreated
PaymentCompleted
ShipmentCreated
CustomerUpdated

Think of an event as a message saying “something happened”.

36.35 What Is Idempotency?

If the same request arrives twice, the final business result should not accidentally happen twice.

Example: clicking “Pay” twice should not charge the customer twice.

if (paymentAlreadyProcessed(idempotencyKey)) {
    return previousResult;
}

processPayment();
storeResult(idempotencyKey);

36.36 What Is Redis?

Redis is a fast in-memory data store commonly used for caching, counters, distributed locks and short-lived state.

SET user:100:name "Amit"
GET user:100:name

36.37 What Is Docker?

Docker packages an application and its dependencies into a portable container image.

FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
COPY target/app.jar app.jar
ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","app.jar"]

36.38 What Is Kubernetes?

Kubernetes manages containers across machines. It can restart failed containers, scale applications and route traffic.

Kubernetes Cluster ├── Node 1 → Pod → Java App ├── Node 2 → Pod → Java App └── Node 3 → Pod → Java App Service → distributes traffic

36.39 Pod vs Deployment vs Service

ObjectSimple Meaning
PodSmallest running unit containing containers.
DeploymentMaintains the desired number/version of pods.
ServiceStable network access to pods.
IngressHTTP routing from outside into services.
ConfigMapNon-secret configuration.
SecretSecret values, subject to appropriate protection.

36.40 Your First Kubernetes Deployment

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: hello
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: hello
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: hello
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: hello
          image: hello:1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080

36.41 What Is Azure?

Azure is Microsoft's cloud platform. Instead of buying physical servers, organizations rent computing, storage, databases, networking and managed services.

36.42 Azure Beginner Mental Model

Azure ├── Compute → VM / App Service / AKS ├── Storage → Blob / Files ├── Database → Azure SQL / PostgreSQL ├── Network → VNet / Load Balancer ├── Security → Entra ID / Key Vault ├── Integration → Service Bus / Event Hubs └── Monitoring → Azure Monitor

36.43 What Is AKS?

AKS is Azure Kubernetes Service. Azure manages much of the Kubernetes control-plane experience while you operate your workloads and cluster configuration.

36.44 What Is CI/CD?

CI means continuously integrating code changes and testing them. CD means continuously delivering/deploying validated software.

Developer ↓ Git ↓ Build ↓ Test ↓ Security Scan ↓ Package ↓ Deploy ↓ Monitor

36.45 What Is DevOps?

DevOps is a way of working that brings development and operations closer together through automation, shared ownership and fast feedback.

36.46 What Is DevSecOps?

DevSecOps means security is integrated into development and delivery rather than being a final manual checkpoint.

36.47 What Is Terraform?

Terraform describes infrastructure as code.

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "example" {
  name     = "rg-demo"
  location = "East US"
}

Instead of manually clicking through cloud portals, the desired infrastructure can be version-controlled and reviewed.

36.48 What Is Monitoring?

Monitoring answers: “Is the system healthy?”

  • CPU
  • Memory
  • Error rate
  • Request rate
  • Latency
  • Database health

36.49 What Is Observability?

Observability helps answer: “Why is the system behaving this way?”

LogsDetailed events.
MetricsNumbers over time.
TracesJourney of one request across services.

36.50 Distributed Trace Example

traceId=abc123

Gateway
  ↓ 12ms
Order Service
  ↓ 30ms
Payment Service
  ↓ 900ms
Bank Provider

Without tracing, you may only know that the order request is slow. With tracing, you can identify the slow dependency.

36.51 What Is AI?

Artificial intelligence refers broadly to systems that perform tasks that normally require human-like reasoning, perception, prediction or decision support.

36.52 What Is Machine Learning?

Machine learning learns patterns from examples rather than relying only on manually written rules.

Example: given historical transactions labelled fraudulent/not fraudulent, a model can learn patterns associated with fraud.

36.53 What Is an LLM?

A Large Language Model predicts and generates language based on patterns learned from large datasets.

Prompt ↓ LLM ↓ Generated Response

36.54 What Is a Prompt?

System:
You are a helpful banking assistant.

User:
Explain why my payment failed.

Context:
Payment status = DECLINED

A production prompt should clearly separate trusted instructions, user input and retrieved data.

36.55 What Is an Embedding?

An embedding converts content into a numerical vector that captures semantic characteristics.

"How do I reset my password?" ↓ Embedding Model ↓ [0.12, -0.31, 0.77, ...]

36.56 What Is a Vector Database?

It stores vectors and lets us search for vectors that are semantically similar.

36.57 What Is RAG?

Retrieval-Augmented Generation means retrieving relevant information first and giving it to the language model as context.

Question ↓ Search ↓ Relevant Documents ↓ Prompt + Context ↓ LLM ↓ Answer

36.58 Simple RAG Pseudocode

question = userQuestion();

documents = vectorStore.search(question);

context = combine(documents);

answer = llm.generate(
    question,
    context
);

36.59 Why RAG Instead of Giving Every Document to the LLM?

Large document collections may exceed context limits, increase cost and introduce irrelevant information. Retrieval narrows the evidence set.

36.60 What Is an AI Agent?

An agent is an AI-driven system that can reason about a goal, choose actions and use tools within defined boundaries.

Goal ↓ Reason ↓ Choose Tool ↓ Execute ↓ Observe Result ↓ Reason Again ↓ Finish

36.61 Workflow vs Agent

WorkflowAgent
Known sequenceDynamic next action
PredictableMore flexible
Easier to testRequires stronger evaluation
Usually cheaperCan cost more

36.62 Agent Tool Calling

{
  "tool": "get_order",
  "arguments": {
    "orderId": "ORD-1001"
  }
}

The tool gateway should validate the request before execution.

36.63 Why AI Agents Need Guardrails

A model may produce an unsafe or incorrect action. Therefore:

LLM ↓ Policy ↓ Authorization ↓ Validation ↓ Human Approval if Required ↓ Tool ↓ Audit

36.64 What Is Prompt Injection?

Prompt injection occurs when untrusted content attempts to manipulate an AI system into ignoring its intended rules or taking unintended actions.

Beginner rule: Treat user text, documents and web content as untrusted data. Do not allow them to rewrite security policy.

36.65 What Is AI Evaluation?

AI evaluation means systematically checking whether an AI application gives useful, correct, grounded and safe results.

MetricQuestion
RelevanceDid it answer the question?
GroundednessIs the answer supported by evidence?
SafetyDid it avoid unsafe behavior?
Tool correctnessDid it select/use the right tool?

36.66 What Is Legacy Modernization?

Modernization means improving an older system's technology, architecture, deployment or operational model while preserving business value.

36.67 Strangler Pattern — Beginner Explanation

Instead of destroying an old house and rebuilding it overnight, build new rooms around it and gradually move people into the new rooms. In software, new services gradually replace old capabilities.

36.68 What Is a Principal Engineer?

A Principal Engineer is a senior technical leader who solves problems beyond one team. They make architecture decisions, mentor engineers, influence strategy and connect technical choices to business outcomes.

36.69 Principal Engineer Daily Thinking

What problem are we solving? ↓ What does the customer need? ↓ What constraints exist? ↓ What options do we have? ↓ What are the risks? ↓ What should we decide? ↓ How will we measure success?

36.70 How to Answer Any Technical Interview Question

1. Define the concept.
2. Give a simple analogy.
3. Explain why it exists.
4. Give a small example.
5. Explain production concerns.
6. Discuss trade-offs.
7. Mention failure modes.
8. Connect it to business impact.

36.71 How to Learn Each Project

  1. Run the smallest possible version.
  2. Change one line and observe the result.
  3. Break it deliberately.
  4. Read the error.
  5. Fix it.
  6. Add a test.
  7. Containerize it.
  8. Deploy it locally.
  9. Deploy it to Kubernetes.
  10. Deploy it to Azure.
  11. Add monitoring.
  12. Explain the architecture aloud.

36.72 Beginner Project — Student Management API

@Entity
class Student {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue
    Long id;

    String name;
    String course;
}
interface StudentRepository
        extends JpaRepository<Student, Long> {
}
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/students")
class StudentController {

    private final StudentRepository repository;

    StudentController(StudentRepository repository) {
        this.repository = repository;
    }

    @PostMapping
    Student create(@RequestBody Student student) {
        return repository.save(student);
    }

    @GetMapping
    List<Student> all() {
        return repository.findAll();
    }
}

36.73 Beginner Project — Banking API

Build:

  • Create account.
  • Deposit money.
  • Withdraw money.
  • Transfer money.
  • View balance.
  • View transaction history.
@PostMapping("/deposit")
public Account deposit(
        @RequestParam BigDecimal amount) {

    account.setBalance(
        account.getBalance().add(amount)
    );

    return repository.save(account);
}

Then improve it: validation → transactions → authentication → idempotency → audit → tests → Docker → Kubernetes.

36.74 Beginner Project — Order Application

Student / User ↓ Order API ↓ Order Service ↓ Database ↓ Notification

First build it as one application. Then split notification into a separate service. Then introduce an event bus. This teaches why architecture becomes more complex.

36.75 Beginner Project — Simple RAG Assistant

  1. Create three text documents.
  2. Split them into chunks.
  3. Create embeddings.
  4. Store vectors.
  5. Search using a question.
  6. Send retrieved text to an LLM.
  7. Return the answer with sources.

36.76 Beginner Project — AI Agent

Start with only two tools:

getStudent(id)
getCourse(id)

Then add a policy layer:

READ_STUDENT = ALLOW
READ_COURSE  = ALLOW
DELETE_STUDENT = DENY

36.77 Beginner Project — Dockerize a Spring Boot App

docker build -t student-api:1.0 .
docker run -p 8080:8080 student-api:1.0

Open http://localhost:8080 and test the API.

36.78 Beginner Project — Kubernetes

kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
kubectl get pods
kubectl get services
kubectl logs <pod-name>

36.79 Beginner Project — Azure Journey

Laptop ↓ GitHub ↓ CI/CD ↓ Container Registry ↓ AKS ↓ Database ↓ Monitoring

Learn each box separately before connecting them.

36.82 The “Explain It to a Child” Test

If you cannot explain a technology without jargon, you probably do not understand it deeply enough yet.

For every topic ask yourself:

  1. What problem does it solve?
  2. What happens without it?
  3. What is the simplest example?
  4. What breaks at scale?
  5. How does the enterprise solution fix that?
  6. What are the trade-offs?
Final learning principle: Start simple, build it yourself, break it intentionally, observe the failure, improve the design, then explain the trade-off. That progression takes you from “I know the definition” to “I can engineer the system.”

39. Banking Zero-to-Hero — Complete Layman Explanation of the Entire Guide

This is the unified learning layer. Every major technology is explained first in everyday language, then through a banking example, then through code, then through production considerations and interview questions.

How to use this chapter: Imagine you are building a digital bank. A customer opens an account, logs in, checks balance, transfers money, receives notifications, and asks an AI assistant questions. Every technology in this guide exists to solve one or more problems in that journey.
🧒 Kid analogy: Think of the whole banking app like a school cafeteria line: you (the customer) walk up (login), tell the server what you want (API request), the server checks the menu board and kitchen stock (services and database), and hands you your tray (response) — every technology in this guide is just a different part of making that line fast, fair and safe.

39.1 The Banking Story We Will Build

Customer ↓ Web / Mobile App ↓ API Gateway ↓ Authentication ↓ Customer Service ↓ Account Service ↓ Payment / Transfer Service ↓ Fraud Service ↓ Database Events ↓ Kafka / Pulsar ├── Notification ├── Audit ├── Analytics └── Fraud Cloud ↓ Azure ↓ AKS ↓ Monitoring / Security / CI-CD AI ↓ RAG ↓ Agents ↓ MCP Tools

39.2 What Is a Banking Application?

At the simplest level, it is software that stores information and applies business rules safely.

Example rule: “A customer cannot transfer more money than the available balance.”

if (account.balance().compareTo(amount) < 0) {
    throw new InsufficientFundsException();
}

account.debit(amount);
receiver.credit(amount);

The code is easy. The difficult part is making this correct when thousands of customers perform transfers simultaneously, networks fail, duplicate requests arrive, databases fail, and attackers try to exploit the system.

39.3 Java From Zero

What is Java?

Java is a programming language used to tell computers what to do. In enterprise systems, Java is commonly used for backend APIs, business logic, integrations and distributed services.

Variable = labelled box

String customerName = "Amit";
BigDecimal balance = new BigDecimal("50000");
boolean active = true;

Condition = decision

if (balance.compareTo(amount) >= 0) {
    System.out.println("Transfer allowed");
} else {
    System.out.println("Insufficient funds");
}

Loop = repeat

for (Transaction tx : transactions) {
    System.out.println(tx.amount());
}

Method = reusable action

BigDecimal calculateFee(BigDecimal amount) {
    return amount.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.01"));
}

Class = blueprint

class Account {

    private final String accountNumber;
    private BigDecimal balance;

    Account(String accountNumber,
            BigDecimal balance) {
        this.accountNumber = accountNumber;
        this.balance = balance;
    }

    void deposit(BigDecimal amount) {
        balance = balance.add(amount);
    }
}

39.4 OOP Explained Through Banking

OOP ConceptBanking ExampleSimple Meaning
ClassAccountBlueprint
ObjectAccount ACC100Real instance
EncapsulationPrivate balanceProtect internal data
InheritanceAccount typesReuse/generalize carefully
PolymorphismDifferent payment providersSame contract, different behavior
AbstractionPaymentGatewayHide implementation details

39.5 SOLID — Full Explanation

S — Single Responsibility Principle

Layman: One worker should not be responsible for cooking, collecting payment, cleaning the restaurant and delivering food if those responsibilities grow independently.

Bank: Do not make AccountService calculate interest, send emails, write audit logs, call fraud APIs and generate PDF statements all in one class.

Bad

class AccountService {

    void transfer() {}
    void sendEmail() {}
    void generateStatement() {}
    void saveAuditLog() {}
    void callFraudApi() {}
}

Good

class TransferService {}
class NotificationService {}
class StatementService {}
class AuditService {}
class FraudService {}

Interview: SRP is about one primary reason to change, not literally one method per class.

O — Open/Closed Principle

Layman: Add a new payment method without rewriting stable transfer logic.

Bad

if (method.equals("UPI")) {
    // UPI
} else if (method.equals("CARD")) {
    // Card
} else if (method.equals("WIRE")) {
    // Wire
}

Good

interface PaymentMethod {
    void pay(Money amount);
}

class UpiPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(Money amount) {}
}

class CardPayment implements PaymentMethod {
    public void pay(Money amount) {}
}

Adding WalletPayment should not require rewriting the core payment algorithm.

L — Liskov Substitution Principle

Layman: If a rule says “all objects of this type can perform X”, a subtype must not unexpectedly break X.

Bank: If PaymentGateway promises refund(), do not create a subtype that always throws “refund not supported” unless the abstraction explicitly allows that behavior.

Bad

interface PaymentGateway {
    void pay();
    void refund();
}

class NoRefundGateway implements PaymentGateway {
    public void pay() {}
    public void refund() {
        throw new UnsupportedOperationException();
    }
}

Better

interface PaymentGateway {
    void pay();
}

interface RefundablePaymentGateway
        extends PaymentGateway {
    void refund();
}

Remember: LSP is about behavioral correctness, not merely method signatures.

I — Interface Segregation Principle

Layman: Do not give every employee a giant form containing fields they never use.

Bank: A read-only reporting service should not depend on account deletion, account creation and update methods.

Bad

interface AccountOperations {
    void create();
    void update();
    void delete();
    Account read();
}

Good

interface AccountReader {
    Account read();
}

interface AccountWriter {
    void create();
    void update();
}

interface AccountDeleter {
    void delete();
}

D — Dependency Inversion Principle

Layman: A bank employee should depend on the bank's approved process, not on one specific brand of printer.

Bad

class TransferService {
    private final MySqlRepository repo =
        new MySqlRepository();
}

Good

interface AccountRepository {
    Account find(AccountId id);
}

class TransferService {

    private final AccountRepository repo;

    TransferService(AccountRepository repo) {
        this.repo = repo;
    }
}

Spring Dependency Injection makes this style natural.

SOLID in One Banking Flow

TransferController
        ↓
TransferService
        ↓
AccountRepository
        ↓
Database

TransferService
   ├── FraudChecker
   ├── PaymentGateway
   ├── AuditService
   └── EventPublisher

39.6 Design Patterns — Complete Banking Cookbook

Factory Pattern

Problem: We support UPI, card, wire transfer and wallet payments.

interface PaymentProcessor {
    void process(Money money);
}

class UpiProcessor implements PaymentProcessor {
    public void process(Money money) {}
}

class CardProcessor implements PaymentProcessor {
    public void process(Money money) {}
}

class PaymentProcessorFactory {

    static PaymentProcessor get(String type) {
        return switch (type) {
            case "UPI" -> new UpiProcessor();
            case "CARD" -> new CardProcessor();
            default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException();
        };
    }
}

Why: Callers do not need to know how each processor is constructed.

Builder Pattern

Problem: A banking transaction may have many optional fields.

Transaction tx =
    new Transaction.Builder()
        .customerId("C100")
        .amount(new BigDecimal("5000"))
        .currency("INR")
        .channel("MOBILE")
        .description("Rent")
        .build();

Strategy Pattern

Problem: Different customers have different fee rules.

interface FeeStrategy {
    Money calculate(Money amount);
}

class StandardFee implements FeeStrategy {
    public Money calculate(Money amount) {
        return amount.multiply("0.01");
    }
}

class PremiumFee implements FeeStrategy {
    public Money calculate(Money amount) {
        return amount.multiply("0.002");
    }
}

Adapter Pattern

Problem: A modern bank application uses PaymentGateway, but a legacy bank exposes a completely different API.

class LegacyBankClient {
    void makeTransfer(double amount) {}
}

class LegacyBankAdapter
        implements PaymentGateway {

    private final LegacyBankClient client;

    LegacyBankAdapter(LegacyBankClient client) {
        this.client = client;
    }

    public void pay(Money money) {
        client.makeTransfer(
            money.amount().doubleValue()
        );
    }
}

Facade Pattern

Layman: The customer asks one bank employee to “make a transfer”; the employee coordinates identity, fraud, balance, payment and audit behind the scenes.

class TransferFacade {

    void transfer(TransferRequest request) {
        authenticate(request);
        checkFraud(request);
        debit(request);
        credit(request);
        recordAudit(request);
        publishEvent(request);
    }
}

Decorator Pattern

Add behavior without changing the original service.

interface AccountService {
    Account get(String id);
}

class CachedAccountService
        implements AccountService {

    private final AccountService target;

    CachedAccountService(AccountService target) {
        this.target = target;
    }

    public Account get(String id) {
        // check cache
        // call target when needed
        return target.get(id);
    }
}

Proxy Pattern

Put a controlled gate around another component.

class SecurePaymentProxy
        implements PaymentGateway {

    private final PaymentGateway target;

    public void pay(Money amount) {
        authorize();
        target.pay(amount);
    }
}

Observer / Event Pattern

When payment succeeds, several systems may care.

PaymentCompleted
   ├── Notification
   ├── Ledger
   ├── Analytics
   └── Fraud
publisher.publish(
    new PaymentCompleted(paymentId)
);

Chain of Responsibility

A transfer may pass through authentication → fraud → limit → compliance → execution.

AuthenticationHandler
        ↓
FraudHandler
        ↓
LimitHandler
        ↓
ComplianceHandler
        ↓
TransferHandler

State Pattern

An account or payment behaves differently depending on state.

enum PaymentState {
    CREATED, PROCESSING, SUCCESS, FAILED
}

Template Method

All payment flows share a common skeleton, while provider-specific steps differ.

abstract class PaymentFlow {

    final void execute() {
        validate();
        authenticate();
        process();
        audit();
    }

    abstract void process();

    void validate() {}
    void authenticate() {}
    void audit() {}
}

Command Pattern

Represent “transfer ₹5,000” as an object that can be queued, audited or retried.

record TransferCommand(
    String from,
    String to,
    Money amount
) {}

Repository Pattern

Hide database details from business logic.

interface AccountRepository {
    Optional<Account> findById(AccountId id);
    Account save(Account account);
}

CQRS

Layman: The system used for changing bank records does not have to be the same model optimized for displaying dashboards.

Transfer Command ↓ Write Database ↓ Event ↓ Read Model ↓ Customer Dashboard

Saga

Imagine an international payment requires multiple independent services. There may be no single database transaction covering everything.

Payment Created
 ↓
Reserve Funds
 ↓
Fraud Check
 ↓
Compliance
 ↓
Settlement

Failure:
Compensate previous successful steps

Transactional Outbox

Problem: Save payment and publish event reliably.

@Transactional
void createPayment(Payment payment) {

    paymentRepository.save(payment);

    outboxRepository.save(
        new OutboxEvent(
            "PaymentCreated",
            payment.id()
        )
    );
}

Circuit Breaker

If an external bank API is failing, stop hammering it.

if (circuitBreaker.isOpen()) {
    return fallback();
}

return bankClient.pay(payment);

Bulkhead

Keep reporting traffic from consuming all payment resources.

Application ├── Payment Thread Pool ├── Customer Thread Pool └── Reporting Thread Pool Reporting failure ↓ Payment remains available

Strangler Pattern

Replace a legacy banking monolith gradually instead of rewriting everything in one risky project.

Old Monolith
   ↓
Routing Layer
   ├── Old Customer Module
   ├── New Customer Service
   ├── Old Payment Module
   └── New Payment Service

39.7 Spring Boot — From Beginner to Production

What is Spring Boot?

Think of Spring Boot as a ready-made construction framework. Instead of manually wiring every component and server, Spring Boot provides conventions and infrastructure.

Controller → Service → Repository

@RestController
class AccountController {

    private final AccountService service;

    AccountController(AccountService service) {
        this.service = service;
    }

    @GetMapping("/accounts/{id}")
    AccountResponse get(
            @PathVariable String id) {

        return service.get(id);
    }
}
@Service
class AccountService {

    private final AccountRepository repository;

    AccountService(AccountRepository repository) {
        this.repository = repository;
    }

    AccountResponse get(String id) {
        return repository.findById(id)
            .map(AccountResponse::from)
            .orElseThrow();
    }
}

Why separate layers?

  • Controller handles HTTP.
  • Service handles business decisions.
  • Repository handles persistence.
  • Tests can isolate each layer.
  • Changes become easier to manage.

39.8 Spring Security

Authentication: Who are you?

Authorization: What are you allowed to do?

@PreAuthorize("hasRole('TRANSFER')")
public void transfer(...) {
}

Banking example: A customer can view their own account but cannot access another customer's account merely by changing the URL.

39.9 Database + JPA/Hibernate

Entity

@Entity
class Account {

    @Id
    private Long id;

    @Column(unique = true)
    private String accountNumber;

    private BigDecimal balance;
}

Repository

interface AccountRepository
        extends JpaRepository<Account, Long> {
}

Transaction

@Transactional
public void transfer(
        Long fromId,
        Long toId,
        BigDecimal amount) {

    Account from = repository.findById(fromId)
        .orElseThrow();

    Account to = repository.findById(toId)
        .orElseThrow();

    from.withdraw(amount);
    to.deposit(amount);
}

In a real banking system, concurrency control, isolation, idempotency, audit and reconciliation must also be addressed.

39.10 SQL for a Beginner

SELECT account_number, balance
FROM accounts
WHERE customer_id = 100;

Index

An index is like the index at the back of a textbook. It helps find information without scanning every page.

CREATE INDEX idx_account_customer
ON accounts(customer_id);

Database Questions to Ask

  • How many rows?
  • Which queries are frequent?
  • Which columns are selective?
  • What indexes exist?
  • What is the transaction boundary?
  • What consistency is required?

39.11 Microservices — Banking Example

Customer Service | Account Service | Transfer Service | Payment Service | Fraud Service

Do not split services merely because a diagram looks modern. Split around business boundaries, ownership, scaling and independent deployment needs.

Service-to-Service Communication

Synchronous: REST/gRPC. The caller waits.

Asynchronous: Event/message. The caller does not need the consumer to finish immediately.

39.12 Kafka / Pulsar

Think of an event bus as a very reliable notice board. A payment service posts “PaymentCompleted”. Different teams can subscribe without the payment service knowing all consumers.

PaymentCompleted {
    paymentId,
    customerId,
    amount,
    timestamp
}

Important Production Questions

  • Can messages be duplicated?
  • Can consumers process out of order?
  • What is the retry policy?
  • Where do failed messages go?
  • Is the consumer idempotent?
  • How is schema evolution handled?

39.13 Distributed Systems

Imagine 20 bank services running on 100 servers. The network between them can fail. A service can become slow. A machine can disappear.

Essential protections

TimeoutNever wait forever.
RetryRetry only suitable transient failures.
BackoffWait progressively longer.
Circuit BreakerStop calling a broken dependency.
BulkheadIsolate resources.
IdempotencyPrevent duplicate business effects.

39.14 System Design — Bank Transfer

Mobile App ↓ API Gateway ↓ Auth ↓ Transfer API ↓ Validation ↓ Fraud ↓ Account DB Transaction ↓ Outbox ↓ Event Bus ├── Notification ├── Ledger ├── Analytics └── Audit

Principal-Level Discussion

  • How many transactions per second?
  • What is the availability target?
  • What happens if fraud service is unavailable?
  • How are duplicate requests handled?
  • How is reconciliation performed?
  • What data is strongly consistent?
  • What can be eventually consistent?
  • How are secrets protected?
  • How is every transaction audited?

39.15 Docker

Docker is like putting the application and its required runtime into a standardized box.

FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre

COPY target/bank.jar /bank.jar

ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "/bank.jar"]

39.16 Kubernetes / AKS

Kubernetes is a manager for containerized applications. AKS is Azure's managed Kubernetes offering.

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: transfer-service
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: transfer
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: transfer
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: transfer
          image: bank/transfer:1.0

Production Kubernetes Checklist

  • Requests and limits.
  • Readiness/liveness/startup probes.
  • Horizontal Pod Autoscaling.
  • Pod disruption strategy.
  • Network policies.
  • Secrets management.
  • Identity.
  • Centralized logging.
  • Metrics and traces.
  • Safe rollout and rollback.

39.20 AI / LLM / RAG

Suppose employees ask: “What is our policy for international transfers?” The model should not guess. It should retrieve the approved policy and answer from that evidence.

Question ↓ Permission Check ↓ Hybrid Search ↓ Reranking ↓ Relevant Policy ↓ LLM ↓ Answer + Citation

39.21 Production RAG Optimization

ProblemFix
Too expensiveSmaller context, caching, model routing.
Too slowParallel retrieval, smaller top-K, reranking optimization.
Wrong answerBetter chunks, metadata, hybrid retrieval, evaluation.
Data leakACL-aware retrieval before model context.
HallucinationGrounding + refusal when evidence is insufficient.
Huge promptsCompression and relevant excerpts.

39.22 Agentic Banking Assistant

An assistant might have tools such as:

getAccountBalance()
getRecentTransactions()
searchPolicy()
createSupportTicket()

But it should not automatically have:

deleteCustomer()
transferUnlimitedMoney()
modifyInterestRate()

Agent Permission Model

SEARCH_POLICY      = ALLOW
READ_BALANCE       = ALLOW
READ_TRANSACTIONS  = ALLOW

CREATE_TICKET      = ALLOW
TRANSFER_MONEY     = APPROVAL_REQUIRED
CHANGE_LIMIT       = DENY
DELETE_ACCOUNT     = DENY

39.23 MCP in Banking

An MCP server could expose approved banking tools to an AI client.

AI Assistant ↓ MCP Client ↓ Banking MCP Server ├── search_policy ├── get_account_summary ├── get_transaction_history └── create_support_ticket ↓ Authorization ↓ Banking APIs

The MCP layer should not bypass existing enterprise authorization. It should strengthen and centralize controlled access.

39.24 Token Optimization for Banking AI

Do not send the customer's entire transaction history to the model when the question is “What was my latest ATM transaction?”

Bad

LLM context =
10 years of transactions
+ all policies
+ entire chat history

Better

Retrieve:
- last 10 relevant transactions
- relevant ATM policy
- current user question

Cost Strategy

Simple FAQ → cheap model
Complex analysis → stronger model
Repeated question → cache
Large document → retrieve/rerank
Huge history → summarize

39.25 Agent Guardrails

User ↓ Input Validation ↓ Agent ↓ Tool Allowlist ↓ Authorization ↓ Argument Validation ↓ Risk Check ↓ Human Approval ↓ Tool ↓ Audit

Never do this

if (agentSaysApproved) {
    executeProductionAction();
}

Do this

if (!authorizationService.allowed(
        user,
        action)) {
    throw new AccessDeniedException();
}

if (risk.isHigh(action) &&
    !approvalService.approved(action)) {
    throw new ApprovalRequiredException();
}

execute(action);

39.26 Skills

A skill is a repeatable recipe for doing a task.

Banking Incident Investigation Skill

Purpose:
Investigate failed transfers.

Steps:
1. Get incident ID.
2. Retrieve trace.
3. Check transfer status.
4. Check dependent services.
5. Check recent deployments.
6. Check relevant logs.
7. Form hypotheses.
8. Produce evidence-backed report.

Never:
- change production configuration
- restart production services
- modify database records

39.27 Spec-Driven Development

Before asking an AI coding agent to implement “transfer API”, write precisely what transfer means.

Feature: Money Transfer

Given:
source account has ₹10,000

When:
customer transfers ₹2,000

Then:
source = ₹8,000
destination increases by ₹2,000
audit event exists
duplicate request does not transfer twice

Then ask AI to generate tests and implementation against the specification.

39.28 AI-Assisted vs AI-Augmented Banking Engineering

AI-Assisted

Developer asks AI: “Explain this stack trace.”

AI-Augmented

AI automatically analyzes failed CI, identifies likely root cause, proposes a patch, creates tests and opens a PR. The engineer reviews it.

Agentic

An approved engineering agent can investigate a ticket, modify scoped files, run tests, update documentation and create a PR while operating under explicit permissions and budgets.

39.29 Legacy Banking Modernization

Imagine a 20-year-old Java monolith containing customer, account, payment and reporting functionality.

Do not immediately rewrite everything.

Legacy Monolith ↓ Identify Business Boundary ↓ Characterization Tests ↓ Create New Service ↓ Route Small Capability ↓ Measure ↓ Move Next Capability ↓ Retire Old Code

39.30 Observability

When a customer says “my transfer is slow”, logs alone may not be enough.

Trace ID: TX123 Gateway 10 ms Auth 15 ms Transfer 40 ms Fraud 900 ms ← bottleneck Database 20 ms

Now the engineer knows where to investigate.

39.31 Security

  • Never store passwords in source code.
  • Use managed identity where possible.
  • Protect secrets with a secret manager.
  • Validate input.
  • Use least privilege.
  • Encrypt data in transit.
  • Encrypt sensitive data at rest.
  • Audit privileged actions.
  • Patch dependencies.
  • Scan containers and infrastructure.

34. Complete Q&A Solutions + Implementations — Java, Spring Boot & Distributed Systems

This section upgrades the interview questions from Modules 1–3 from question lists into answer-ready Principal Engineer material with concise answers, deep explanations, implementation patterns, follow-ups and project exercises.

How to use this section: First practice the 60-second answer. Then expand to the deep-dive answer. Finally implement the project. A Principal Engineer should be able to move fluidly from business context → architecture → code → failure modes → operations → trade-offs.
🧒 Kid analogy: Think of this section as flashcards after a big lesson — short answer first (like the front of the card), then the full explanation (the back of the card), then a mini project so the idea sticks.

34.1 Java Core — Q&A

Q1. Explain the Java Memory Model and why it matters in concurrent systems.

60-second answer: The Java Memory Model defines how threads interact through memory, including visibility, ordering and atomicity guarantees. Without proper synchronization, one thread may not observe another thread's updates reliably. volatile provides visibility and ordering guarantees for a variable, while locks and atomic classes provide stronger coordination for compound operations.

Principal deep dive: I reason about shared state first, then choose the smallest synchronization mechanism that gives the required correctness. I avoid assuming that visibility implies atomicity. For example, count++ is not made atomic merely because count is volatile.

private final AtomicInteger counter = new AtomicInteger();

public int increment() {
    return counter.incrementAndGet();
}

Follow-up: Why not use synchronized everywhere? Because synchronization has a coordination cost and can reduce concurrency. The goal is correctness first, then measured optimization.

Q2. HashMap vs ConcurrentHashMap?

Answer: HashMap is not safe for concurrent structural modification. ConcurrentHashMap provides thread-safe concurrent access with significantly better concurrency than synchronizing the entire map. It is appropriate for shared mutable maps when compound operations can be expressed through atomic APIs such as computeIfAbsent.

ConcurrentHashMap<String, User> cache = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

User user = cache.computeIfAbsent(
    userId,
    id -> loadUser(id)
);

Principal consideration: Thread-safe collection operations do not automatically make a multi-step business workflow atomic. The business transaction may still require explicit coordination.

Q3. Explain equals() and hashCode().

Answer: If two objects are equal according to equals(), they must return the same hashCode(). Violating this contract can make objects behave incorrectly in hash-based collections.

public record CustomerId(String value) {}

Map<CustomerId, Customer> customers = new HashMap<>();

For entities, be particularly careful when identifiers are assigned after persistence. Equality semantics should match the domain model.

Q4. Explain immutability and why Principal Engineers prefer it in concurrent systems.

Answer: Immutable objects cannot change after construction. They are easier to reason about, naturally safer to share between threads and reduce synchronization requirements.

public record Money(BigDecimal amount, Currency currency) {
    public Money {
        Objects.requireNonNull(amount);
        Objects.requireNonNull(currency);
    }
}

Q5. Explain JVM memory and garbage collection at a high level.

Answer: Java applications allocate objects primarily on the heap, while each thread has its own stack. Garbage collectors reclaim objects that are no longer reachable. In production I monitor allocation rate, heap occupancy, GC pause behavior, CPU and application latency rather than tuning blindly.

Principal answer: GC is an operational concern. A latency-sensitive service needs an explicit latency budget and evidence-based tuning. If the application creates excessive temporary objects, increasing the heap may only postpone the underlying problem.

34.2 Object-Oriented Design — Q&A

Q6. SOLID principles in a real enterprise system?

Answer: SOLID is useful when it reduces coupling and improves changeability, not when applied mechanically.

  • S: One reason to change.
  • O: Extend behavior without repeatedly modifying stable code.
  • L: Subtypes preserve expected behavior.
  • I: Prefer focused interfaces.
  • D: Depend on abstractions rather than concrete implementation details.
public interface PaymentGateway {
    PaymentResult charge(Money amount);
}

public final class PaymentService {
    private final PaymentGateway gateway;

    public PaymentService(PaymentGateway gateway) {
        this.gateway = gateway;
    }
}

Q7. Composition vs inheritance?

Answer: Prefer composition when behavior varies independently or when inheritance would create tight coupling. Inheritance is appropriate when there is a genuine substitutable type relationship and the base abstraction has stable semantics.

34.3 Spring Boot — Q&A + Implementation

Q8. What happens when a Spring Boot application starts?

Answer: The application creates the Spring ApplicationContext, performs component scanning and configuration processing, creates beans, applies dependency injection and post-processors, configures embedded infrastructure and starts the application runtime such as the embedded web server.

@SpringBootApplication
public class PaymentApplication {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SpringApplication.run(PaymentApplication.class, args);
    }
}

Principal follow-up: Discuss auto-configuration, conditional beans, bean lifecycle, proxy creation, profiles, configuration properties and startup diagnostics.

Q9. How does Spring dependency injection work?

Answer: The container manages object creation and dependency wiring. Constructor injection makes required dependencies explicit and makes classes easier to test.

@Service
public class PaymentService {
    private final PaymentRepository repository;

    public PaymentService(PaymentRepository repository) {
        this.repository = repository;
    }
}

Q10. Explain @Transactional.

Answer: Spring typically uses a proxy around the transactional component. The proxy starts/joins a transaction before method execution and commits or rolls back according to transaction rules.

@Transactional
public PaymentResponse transfer(TransferRequest request) {
    debit(request.from());
    credit(request.to());
    return PaymentResponse.success();
}

Important: @Transactional does not automatically make external HTTP calls transactional. Database transactions and distributed operations must be designed separately.

Q11. Why can self-invocation break Spring proxy behavior?

Answer: A method calling another method on the same object may bypass the proxy, so proxy-based annotations such as @Transactional or certain aspects may not be applied to the internal call.

Solution: Move the transactional boundary to another bean or redesign the service boundary instead of depending on self-proxy tricks.

Q12. How would you secure a Spring Boot REST API?

@Bean
SecurityFilterChain security(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
    return http
        .csrf(csrf -> csrf.disable())
        .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
            .requestMatchers("/actuator/health/**").permitAll()
            .requestMatchers("/api/admin/**").hasRole("ADMIN")
            .anyRequest().authenticated())
        .oauth2ResourceServer(oauth -> oauth.jwt())
        .build();
}

Principal discussion: Authentication establishes identity. Authorization determines what that identity can do. Do not confuse JWT validation with business authorization.

Q13. How do you make a Spring Boot service production-ready?

  • Externalized configuration.
  • Health/readiness/liveness endpoints.
  • Structured logs.
  • Distributed tracing.
  • Metrics.
  • Timeouts.
  • Connection-pool limits.
  • Graceful shutdown.
  • Secure secrets.
  • Automated tests.
  • Container resource requests/limits.
  • CI/CD and rollback.

34.4 Microservices — Q&A

Q14. When should you use microservices?

Answer: Microservices are useful when independent scaling, ownership, deployment or domain boundaries justify the operational complexity. They should not be chosen merely because the organization wants “modern architecture.”

Principal trade-off: Microservices introduce network failure, distributed tracing, data consistency challenges, deployment complexity and operational overhead.

Q15. REST vs asynchronous messaging?

RESTMessaging
Immediate request/responseDecoupled asynchronous processing
Simple client semanticsBetter buffering and resilience
Caller waitsConsumer processes independently
Useful for queries/commands requiring immediate responseUseful for events and long-running workflows

Q16. How do you make REST APIs idempotent?

Answer: Use an idempotency key or business identifier and persist the result associated with the request. Repeated requests return the original outcome instead of creating duplicate business effects.

@PostMapping("/payments")
public PaymentResponse create(
        @RequestHeader("Idempotency-Key") String key,
        @RequestBody PaymentRequest request) {
    return paymentService.processIdempotently(key, request);
}

Q17. Explain circuit breaker, timeout and retry.

Answer: A timeout prevents indefinite waiting. A retry handles transient failures. A circuit breaker prevents repeated calls to an unhealthy dependency. They work together, but retries must be bounded and carefully applied.

@CircuitBreaker(name = "fraudService", fallbackMethod = "fallback")
@Retry(name = "fraudService")
@TimeLimiter(name = "fraudService")
public CompletableFuture<FraudResult> check(FraudRequest request) {
    return client.check(request);
}

Warning: Blind retries can amplify an outage. Use exponential backoff, jitter and retry budgets.

34.5 Distributed Systems — Q&A

Q18. Explain CAP theorem.

Answer: When a network partition occurs, a distributed system must choose between stronger consistency and availability for the affected operation path. CAP is about partitioned distributed systems; it does not mean a system can simply “pick two” under all circumstances.

Q19. Explain eventual consistency.

Answer: Replicas may temporarily disagree but converge when updates propagate successfully. The design must define which operations require strong consistency and which can tolerate stale reads.

Example: A product catalog view may tolerate seconds of staleness; a payment authorization balance may require much stronger guarantees.

Q20. How do you design distributed transactions?

Answer: Prefer local transactions combined with explicit workflow patterns such as Saga and transactional outbox. Avoid distributed two-phase commit unless the business and infrastructure genuinely require it and the operational cost is understood.

Order DB Transaction ↓ Transactional Outbox ↓ Event Bus ↓ Payment ↓ Inventory ↓ Shipment

Q21. Explain the transactional outbox.

@Transactional
public void createOrder(Order order) {
    orderRepository.save(order);

    outboxRepository.save(
        OutboxEvent.of(
            "OrderCreated",
            order.id()
        )
    );
}

A separate publisher reads the outbox and publishes the event. This avoids the classic failure where the database commits but event publication fails.

Q22. How do you make event consumers idempotent?

@Transactional
public void consume(PaymentCompleted event) {

    if (processedEventRepository.existsById(event.id())) {
        return;
    }

    updateCustomerBalance(event);

    processedEventRepository.save(
        ProcessedEvent.of(event.id())
    );
}

Use a unique event ID or business idempotency key and enforce uniqueness at the database level.

Q23. How do you handle distributed caching?

Answer: Define cache ownership, TTL, invalidation, stale-data tolerance and failure behavior. Redis can be used for shared caching, but the database remains the source of truth unless the architecture explicitly says otherwise.

@Cacheable(cacheNames = "customers", key = "#id")
public Customer getCustomer(String id) {
    return repository.findById(id).orElseThrow();
}

Q24. How do you design rate limiting?

Answer: Token bucket is a useful model for controlling sustained rate while permitting limited bursts.

tokens = min(capacity,
             tokens + elapsedTime * refillRate)

if tokens >= cost:
    tokens -= cost
    allow()
else:
    reject()

For distributed APIs, store shared state in a suitable low-latency system such as Redis or use a gateway/provider capability.

Q25. How do you design for failure?

  • Timeout every remote call.
  • Bound retries.
  • Use circuit breakers where appropriate.
  • Use bulkheads to isolate resources.
  • Make operations idempotent.
  • Provide graceful degradation.
  • Use queues for buffering where appropriate.
  • Monitor saturation.
  • Define recovery procedures.

34.6 Complete Project 1 — Spring Boot Enterprise Payment Platform

Goal: Implement a production-style payment platform demonstrating Java, Spring Boot, PostgreSQL, Redis, Kafka/Pulsar-compatible eventing, OAuth2, idempotency, outbox, observability, Docker and Kubernetes.

Architecture

Repository Structure

Payment Entity

@Entity
@Table(name = "payments")
public class Payment {
    @Id
    private UUID id;

    @Column(nullable = false, unique = true)
    private String idempotencyKey;

    @Column(nullable = false)
    private BigDecimal amount;

    @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING)
    private PaymentStatus status;

    protected Payment() {}

    public Payment(String key, BigDecimal amount) {
        this.id = UUID.randomUUID();
        this.idempotencyKey = key;
        this.amount = amount;
        this.status = PaymentStatus.CREATED;
    }
}

Service

@Service
public class PaymentService {

    private final PaymentRepository payments;
    private final OutboxRepository outbox;

    @Transactional
    public PaymentResponse create(
            String idempotencyKey,
            PaymentRequest request) {

        return payments
            .findByIdempotencyKey(idempotencyKey)
            .map(PaymentResponse::from)
            .orElseGet(() -> {

                Payment payment =
                    new Payment(
                        idempotencyKey,
                        request.amount()
                    );

                payments.save(payment);

                outbox.save(
                    OutboxEvent.paymentCreated(payment)
                );

                return PaymentResponse.from(payment);
            });
    }
}

REST API

POST /api/v1/payments

Headers:
Idempotency-Key: 7f2e...

{
  "accountId": "ACC-100",
  "amount": 2500.00,
  "currency": "INR"
}

34.9 Complete Project 4 — Distributed Rate Limiter

@RestController
public class RateLimitController {

    @GetMapping("/api/resource")
    public ResponseEntity<?> get() {

        if (!rateLimiter.allow("customer-123")) {
            return ResponseEntity.status(429).build();
        }

        return ResponseEntity.ok("allowed");
    }
}

Production implementation should move the shared state into a distributed store or gateway and define policies per tenant, user, IP, API and business operation.

35. Complete Q&A + Implementations — Azure/AKS + DevOps + GenAI/Agents + Modernization + Principal Leadership

This module converts the remaining Principal Engineer topics into interview-ready answers and practical implementation patterns.

35.1 Azure Architecture — Complete Q&A

Q1. Design a highly available Java platform on Azure.

60-second answer: I would start with availability, latency, security, compliance and scale requirements. For a containerized Java platform I would use Front Door/WAF at the edge, API Management where API governance is needed, AKS for container orchestration, managed database services, Redis for caching, Service Bus/Event Hubs for asynchronous communication, Key Vault for secrets, managed/workload identity for Azure access, and Azure Monitor/Application Insights for observability.

Internet ↓ Front Door + WAF ↓ API Management ↓ AKS ├── Spring Boot APIs ├── Workers └── AI Services ↓ Private Azure Services ├── SQL/PostgreSQL ├── Redis ├── Service Bus ├── Storage └── Key Vault ↓ Azure Monitor + App Insights
🧒 Kid analogy: Same flashcard idea as before, this time for the "running things in the cloud and leading a team" topics — short answer, then the deep explanation, then how it plays out in a real project.

Principal trade-off: AKS gives control and a common platform but adds operational complexity. The architecture should use AKS only when that control and ecosystem justify it.

Q2. AKS vs App Service vs Container Apps?

OptionBest FitTrade-Off
App ServiceWeb/API workloads with low platform-management needsLess orchestration control
Container AppsManaged container workloads and event-driven servicesLess Kubernetes control
AKSComplex container platform, Kubernetes ecosystem, advanced scheduling/networkingHigher operational/platform complexity

Q3. How do you secure AKS?

  • Use Entra ID and workload/managed identity.
  • Use least-privilege RBAC.
  • Prefer private connectivity for sensitive Azure resources.
  • Control ingress and egress.
  • Scan and control container images.
  • Use Key Vault for secrets.
  • Apply Kubernetes network/security policies.
  • Use policy-as-code where appropriate.
  • Audit cluster and application activity.
  • Continuously monitor vulnerabilities and configuration drift.

Q4. Explain managed identity/workload identity.

Answer: The workload obtains an Azure identity through the platform instead of embedding long-lived credentials in source code or container images. Authorization is then controlled through Azure RBAC and resource policies.

Spring Boot Pod ↓ Workload Identity ↓ Entra ID ↓ Azure RBAC ↓ Key Vault / Storage / Azure Service

Q5. How do you troubleshoot CrashLoopBackOff?

kubectl get pods -n production
kubectl describe pod <pod> -n production
kubectl logs <pod> -n production
kubectl logs <pod> --previous -n production
kubectl get events -n production --sort-by=.lastTimestamp

Then classify the failure: application startup, configuration, secret, dependency, resource limit, probe failure or image/runtime mismatch.

Q6. How do you troubleshoot a Pending pod?

kubectl describe pod <pod>
kubectl get nodes
kubectl describe node <node>
kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp

Investigate resource requests, node capacity, taints, tolerations, affinity, topology constraints and scheduling policies.

Q7. How do readiness and liveness probes differ?

Readiness: should this pod receive traffic?

Liveness: is the process unhealthy enough to restart?

Startup: has a slow-starting application finished initialization?

management:
  endpoint:
    health:
      probes:
        enabled: true

35.3 Terraform — Azure Foundation

    azurerm = {
      source  = "hashicorp/azurerm"
      version = "~> 4.0"
    }
  }
}

provider "azurerm" {
  features {}
}

resource "azurerm_resource_group" "platform" {
  name     = "rg-principal-platform"
  location = "East US"
}

resource "azurerm_container_registry" "acr" {
  name                = "principalplatformacr"
  resource_group_name = azurerm_resource_group.platform.name
  location            = azurerm_resource_group.platform.location
  sku                 = "Premium"
}

Interview follow-up: Explain remote state, locking, module boundaries, environment isolation, secret handling and plan/apply approvals.

35.4 DevOps — Complete Q&A

Q8. Design a production CI/CD pipeline.

Pull Request ↓ Build ↓ Unit Tests ↓ Static Analysis ↓ Dependency Scan ↓ Container Build ↓ Image Scan ↓ Push ACR ↓ Deploy Dev ↓ Integration Tests ↓ Deploy Staging ↓ Approval / Policy ↓ Canary Production ↓ Monitor ↓ Promote / Rollback

Principal answer: The pipeline is a risk-control system. Every gate should exist because it prevents a known failure mode. Avoid adding gates merely because “DevOps requires them.”

Q9. Blue-green vs canary deployment?

Blue-green switches traffic between two environments and enables rapid rollback.

Canary gradually exposes a new version to a subset of traffic and uses production telemetry as a release signal.

Q10. How do you implement rollback?

Rollback must be designed before deployment. Keep immutable artifacts, version configuration, preserve the previous release, use progressive deployment and ensure database changes remain backward compatible.

35.5 GitHub Actions CI/CD Implementation

name: build-and-deploy

on:
  pull_request:
  push:
    branches: [ main ]

jobs:
  build-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Set up Java
        uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: '21'

      - name: Test
        run: ./mvnw verify

      - name: Build image
        run: docker build -t $IMAGE_TAG .

      - name: Security checks
        run: echo "Run SAST, dependency and image scanning"

      - name: Deploy
        if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
        run: echo "Deploy to AKS"

35.6 DevSecOps Q&A

Q11. Where should security occur in CI/CD?

Answer: Throughout the lifecycle: source scanning, secret detection, dependency scanning, SAST, IaC scanning, container scanning, deployment policy and runtime monitoring.

Q12. How do you secure CI credentials?

Prefer workload identity/federated credentials where supported. Avoid permanent cloud access keys in repository secrets. Scope identities to the minimum deployment permissions.

35.7 GenAI — Complete Q&A

Q13. RAG vs fine-tuning?

Answer: Use RAG when the problem is access to private, current or frequently changing knowledge. Use fine-tuning when specialized behavior or task patterns need to be learned. They can be combined, but neither automatically solves authorization or hallucination.

Q14. How do you prevent hallucination?

  • Retrieve authoritative context.
  • Require citations where appropriate.
  • Constrain output formats.
  • Use retrieval and answer evaluation.
  • Allow the model to abstain when evidence is insufficient.
  • Validate critical outputs independently.
  • Never use generated text as trusted authorization.

Q15. How do you prevent prompt injection?

Untrusted Content ↓ Treat as DATA ↓ Retrieve ↓ Policy Layer ↓ LLM ↓ Tool Authorization ↓ Validation ↓ Execution

Instructions contained inside documents or tool results must not automatically gain the authority of system policy.

Q16. How do you secure LLM tool calling?

Agent Request
   ↓
Tool Schema Validation
   ↓
Authorization Policy
   ↓
Resource Scope Check
   ↓
Human Approval if High Risk
   ↓
Execute
   ↓
Audit

Model intent is not authorization.

Q17. When should you use an agent instead of a workflow?

Use deterministic workflows for known sequences. Use agents when selecting the next action requires flexible reasoning and the uncertainty justifies the added cost and risk.

Q18. How do you control runaway agents?

  • Maximum iterations.
  • Maximum tool calls.
  • Token budget.
  • Time budget.
  • Allowed tool list.
  • State limits.
  • Circuit breaker.
  • Human approval for high-impact actions.

35.8 Agent Implementation — Secure Tool Runtime

public final class ToolPolicy {

    public boolean allowed(
            String principal,
            String tool,
            Map<String, Object> args) {

        if ("production.deploy".equals(tool)) {
            return hasRole(principal, "RELEASE_APPROVER");
        }

        if ("repository.read".equals(tool)) {
            return true;
        }

        return false;
    }
}
public ToolResult execute(
        String principal,
        ToolRequest request) {

    if (!policy.allowed(
            principal,
            request.name(),
            request.arguments())) {
        throw new AccessDeniedException(
            "Tool not authorized"
        );
    }

    validateSchema(request);
    audit(request);

    return toolRegistry
        .get(request.name())
        .execute(request.arguments());
}

35.9 RAG Implementation Pattern

public Answer ask(String question, String tenant) {

    List<Document> docs =
        retriever.search(
            question,
            Filter.tenant(tenant)
        );

    List<Document> ranked =
        reranker.rank(question, docs);

    String context =
        contextBuilder.build(ranked);

    return llm.answer(
        promptTemplate.render(
            question,
            context
        )
    );
}

Critical control: Filter.tenant(tenant) represents authorization-aware retrieval, not merely metadata tagging.

35.10 AI Evaluation Implementation

record EvalCase(
    String question,
    String expectedEvidence,
    String expectedBehavior
) {}

record EvalResult(
    double groundedness,
    double relevance,
    double safety,
    boolean toolPolicyPassed
) {}

Build a regression dataset and run it whenever the prompt, model, retrieval logic or tool policy changes.

35.11 AI Cost-Control Implementation

public Model select(ModelRequest request) {

    if (request.complexity() == Complexity.SIMPLE) {
        return FAST_MODEL;
    }

    if (request.highRisk()) {
        return STRONG_MODEL;
    }

    return DEFAULT_MODEL;
}

35.12 Modernization — Complete Q&A

Q19. How would you modernize a 15-year-old Java monolith?

  1. Baseline business-critical flows.
  2. Map dependencies and runtime behavior.
  3. Improve observability.
  4. Create characterization/regression tests.
  5. Identify domain boundaries.
  6. Introduce APIs around high-value seams.
  7. Use the Strangler pattern incrementally.
  8. Modernize deployment and CI/CD.
  9. Measure reliability, delivery speed and cost.
  10. Only then expand the modernization scope.

Q20. Why not rewrite everything?

A big-bang rewrite concentrates unknown requirements, migration risk and schedule risk. Incremental modernization creates working checkpoints, preserves business continuity and allows learning from production.

Q21. How can AI help legacy modernization?

  • Codebase discovery.
  • Dependency mapping.
  • Documentation generation.
  • Test generation.
  • Refactoring suggestions.
  • Migration assistance.
  • Incident analysis.
  • API extraction support.

AI output must be validated against tests, runtime evidence and architecture constraints.

35.13 Modernization Implementation — Strangler Pattern

Legacy Monolith | ↓ API Gateway / Routing | ┌────┴───────────────┐ ↓ ↓ Legacy Capability New Service | | └──────────┬─────────┘ ↓ Shared Events ↓ Gradual Migration

Migration Sequence

Phase 1: Observe
Phase 2: Characterize
Phase 3: Isolate
Phase 4: Extract
Phase 5: Route
Phase 6: Stabilize
Phase 7: Remove legacy path

35.14 Principal Leadership — Complete Q&A

Q22. What distinguishes a Principal Engineer?

Answer: Scope, leverage and decision quality. A Principal Engineer influences multiple teams, establishes technical direction, handles ambiguous problems, reduces systemic risk and develops other technical leaders.

Q23. Tell me about a time you disagreed with leadership.

Strong structure: State the business objective, explain the disagreement factually, show alternatives, provide evidence, make a recommendation, accept the decision and explain the outcome. Avoid framing the story as “I was right and leadership was wrong.”

Q24. How do you influence without authority?

  1. Build credibility through technical evidence.
  2. Understand each stakeholder's goals.
  3. Make trade-offs explicit.
  4. Create reusable decision artifacts.
  5. Find early adopters.
  6. Demonstrate value through a pilot.
  7. Scale through standards/platforms rather than mandates.

Q25. How do you handle technical debt?

Translate debt into business impact: delivery delay, incidents, security exposure, operational cost or lost capability. Prioritize debt alongside product work rather than creating a separate unlimited backlog with no business context.

Q26. How do you mentor senior engineers?

Do less solving and more coaching. Give them ownership of architecture decisions, ask them to articulate trade-offs, review decisions rather than only code and gradually increase their organizational scope.

Q27. How do you communicate bad news to executives?

What happened:
Customer/business impact:
Why it happened:
What we have done:
What remains:
Options:
Recommendation:
Decision required:
Next update:

Be direct, avoid surprises and distinguish confirmed facts from hypotheses.

Q28. How do you lead a production incident?

Assign an incident commander, establish severity, stabilize customer impact, assign investigation roles, communicate on a predictable cadence, control changes, recover, then conduct a blameless postmortem with owners and dates.

35.15 Executive Answer — 60 Seconds

"The platform has three material issues: reliability, delivery speed
and rising operating cost. I would first establish a baseline and
stabilize observability. In parallel, I would standardize CI/CD and
security controls. Then I would modernize the highest-value legacy
seams incrementally and introduce AI only where a measurable business
or engineering productivity benefit exists.

I would manage this through quarterly outcomes, explicit architecture
decisions, risk tracking and metrics for reliability, delivery,
cost and customer impact."

35.16 Executive Scenario — AI Modernization Investment

Q29. “Why should we spend money on GenAI?”

Strong answer: “I would not start with a technology budget. I would start with a measurable business problem. We can identify high-volume engineering or customer workflows, establish the current baseline, run a controlled pilot and measure time saved, quality, risk and total cost. If the benefit exceeds the full operating and change cost, we scale it. If it does not, we stop.”

35.21 Enterprise Project 12 — Internal Developer Platform

Developer Portal ↓ Service Template ↓ Spring Boot Scaffold ↓ CI/CD ↓ Security Defaults ↓ AKS Deployment ↓ Observability ↓ Golden Path

The platform should reduce developer cognitive load while preserving team autonomy.

35.22 Enterprise Project 13 — Multi-Tenant SaaS Platform

Tenant ↓ API Gateway ↓ Tenant Context ↓ Authorization ↓ Service ↓ Tenant-aware Data ↓ Tenant-aware Cache ↓ Tenant-aware Events

Discuss tenant isolation models, noisy-neighbor controls, per-tenant quotas, data residency, tenant-aware observability and billing.

35.23 Enterprise Project 14 — Fraud Detection Platform

Transaction ↓ Risk API ↓ Rules ↓ Feature Store ↓ ML Model ↓ Risk Score ↓ Decision ├── Approve ├── Review └── Reject ↓ Event Stream ↓ Analytics

For a Principal interview, discuss model latency, explainability, feature freshness, false positives, model drift, auditability and fail-safe behavior.

35.24 Enterprise Project 15 — Notification Platform

Business Event ↓ Notification Router ↓ Preferences ↓ Template ↓ Queue ├── Email ├── SMS └── Push ↓ Provider Failover ↓ Delivery Tracking

35.25 Enterprise Project 16 — Distributed Audit Platform

Services ↓ Audit SDK ↓ Event Bus ↓ Immutable Audit Store ↓ Search Index ↓ Compliance / Security Analytics

Audit records should capture who, what, when, source, target, result and correlation identifiers without unnecessarily storing sensitive payloads.

35.26 Enterprise Project 17 — Real-Time Analytics Platform

Events ↓ Event Hub / Kafka / Pulsar ↓ Stream Processing ↓ Hot Store ↓ Dashboard ↓ Data Lake / Warehouse ↓ BI / ML

35.27 Enterprise Project 18 — API Platform

Consumers ↓ Front Door ↓ API Gateway ↓ Auth / Rate Limit ↓ Routing ↓ Microservices ↓ Observability ↓ Developer Portal

Include versioning, quotas, API keys/OAuth, idempotency, schema evolution and consumer lifecycle management.

35.28 Enterprise Project 19 — Resilient Data Platform

  • Primary database.
  • Read replicas.
  • Backup and restore.
  • Point-in-time recovery.
  • Data validation.
  • Migration automation.
  • Disaster recovery drills.

35.29 Enterprise Project 20 — Multi-Region SaaS

Global Traffic ↓ Front Door ┌────┴────┐ ↓ ↓ Region A Region B ↓ ↓ AKS AKS ↓ ↓ Data / Cache / Events └────┬────┘ ↓ Global Recovery Strategy

Be explicit about active-active vs active-passive, data replication, conflict resolution, RPO/RTO and operational complexity.

35.30 Enterprise Project 21 — Secure File Processing Platform

Upload ↓ Blob Storage ↓ Malware / Policy Scan ↓ Queue ↓ Processing Worker ↓ Metadata DB ↓ Search / Analytics ↓ Secure Download

35.31 Enterprise Project 22 — Customer 360 Platform

CRM + Transactions + Support + Digital ↓ Data Ingestion ↓ Identity Resolution ↓ Customer 360 ↓ APIs / Analytics / AI

35.32 Enterprise Project 23 — Enterprise Search + RAG

Enterprise Sources ↓ Connectors ↓ Parsing ↓ Metadata + ACLs ↓ Hybrid Search ↓ Reranking ↓ LLM ↓ Citations + Authorization

35.33 Enterprise Project 24 — Cloud Cost Intelligence

Azure Billing ↓ Usage Data ↓ Cost Allocation ↓ Service / Team / Tenant ↓ Anomaly Detection ↓ Recommendations ↓ FinOps Dashboard

35.34 Enterprise Project 25 — Complete Principal Platform

Global Users ↓ Front Door + WAF ↓ API Platform ↓ AKS ┌────────────────┼─────────────────┐ ↓ ↓ ↓ Business APIs Event Platform AI Platform ↓ ↓ ↓ SQL / Redis Analytics RAG / Agents │ │ │ └────────────────┼─────────────────┘ ↓ Observability Platform ↓ Governance + Security ↓ Executive Metrics

Master Project Acceptance Criteria

ArchitectureADR, diagrams, boundaries and ownership.
JavaProduction Spring Boot services and tests.
CloudAzure deployment and managed services.
AKSScaling, probes, security and rollout.
DevOpsAutomated CI/CD with security gates.
AIRAG/agents with evaluation and guardrails.
SecurityIdentity, authorization, secrets and audit.
OperationsLogs, metrics, traces, alerts and runbooks.

35.35 Principal Interview Final Rapid-Fire

  1. Why Java for enterprise systems?
  2. How does the JVM manage memory?
  3. How do you diagnose a concurrency bug?
  4. How does Spring Boot auto-configuration work?
  5. How does Spring transaction management work?
  6. When would you not use microservices?
  7. How do you handle distributed transactions?
  8. How do you design idempotency?
  9. How do you make a system resilient to dependency failures?
  10. How do you design AKS for high availability?
  11. How do you secure cloud workloads?
  12. How do you design CI/CD?
  13. How do you implement progressive delivery?
  14. How do you evaluate a RAG system?
  15. How do you secure agent tools?
  16. How do you prevent prompt injection?
  17. How do you modernize a legacy Java platform?
  18. How do you influence without authority?
  19. How do you communicate technical risk to executives?
  20. How do you decide whether a technology is worth adopting?
Final Principal answer pattern: Requirements → assumptions → scale → architecture → data → security → failure modes → observability → deployment → cost → trade-offs → business outcome.
43 • SQL Mastery

From SELECT basics to Principal Engineer SQL: joins, CTEs, window functions, transactions, ACID, locking, indexes, EXPLAIN, performance tuning, security, banking queries, interview answers and capstone.

44 • MongoDB / NoSQL Mastery

From documents and CRUD to enterprise MongoDB: embedding vs referencing, aggregation, indexes, transactions, replication, sharding, security, Spring Boot/Python, event-driven systems, GenAI/RAG and interview answers.

45

Kafka Zero-to-Hero

Practical Spring Boot event-driven architecture: brokers, topics, partitions, offsets, producers, consumers, consumer groups, ordering, replication, retries, DLT, outbox pattern, transactions, schema evolution, Kafka Streams, security, observability, banking hands-on project and interview Q&A.

46

Redis Cache Zero-to-Hero

Redis data structures, TTL, eviction, cache-aside, invalidation, cache stampede, penetration, avalanche, hot keys, distributed locks, rate limiting, sessions, Pub/Sub, Streams, Spring Boot caching, production monitoring, banking project and interview Q&A.

47

JWT + OAuth2 + OIDC

Authentication, authorization, JWT claims and validation, access/refresh tokens, Authorization Code + PKCE, Client Credentials, scopes, roles, OIDC, key rotation, Spring Security, microservices/API security, banking authorization, security capstone and interview Q&A.
